# La Chanson de Roland — full-treatment edition

*Oxford manuscript (Bodleian Digby 23), canonical Bédier line numbering (1–4002,
291 laisses).* Each laisse is a self-contained block: the **numbered original** Old
French (with the **modern infinitive of every verb** bracketed at the end of each
line), immediately followed by a **fresh line-by-line translation** sharing the same
canonical numbers. See `docs/chanson-full-treatment-prompt.md` for the conventions and
the verb-gloss mappings table.

**Translation note.** The translation below is Claude's own, re-rendered line for line:
Claude (Opus 4.8) translated laisses I–CLI (verses 1–2034) in June 2026, and Claude (Sonnet 5.5)
translated CLII–CCXCI (2035–4002) on 2026-10-02, after which a skeptic pass checked every
second-half line the app shows. It is faithful over poetic; uncertain lines carry a trailing
`<!-- uncertain: ... -->` comment rather than a confident guess.

**Edition note.** Laisse I was transcribed from the UT-Austin Bédier text (`amet`,
`Saraguce`, `emperere`). From Laisse II onward the original is transcribed verbatim
from the working raw source `corpus/originals/literature/chanson-roland-oxford.txt` (Orbis Latinus /
orbilat.com), which differs in minor orthography and punctuation (`aimet`,
`Sarraguce`, `emper[er]e`, editorial brackets like `[S]erai`, parentheticals like
`(...)`). Line numbers and laisse divisions agree across both.

**Numbering note.** Line numbers are Bédier's throughout, checked on 2026-10-02 against the margin
numbers of Bédier's own edition and Mortier's. The raw source's row numbers are not Bédier's
between 1311 and 1823, so verses 1389–2034 were renumbered on that date. Numbered lacuna lines:
1389, 1666, 3146, 3390, 3494. Details in `corpus/working/chanson_progress.md`.

---

## Laisse I (lines 1–9)

**Original**
1. Carles li reis, nostre emperere magnes
2. set anz tuz pleins ad estet en Espaigne: [avoir, être]
3. Tresqu'en la mer cunquist la tere altaigne. [conquérir]
4. N'i ad castel ki devant lui remaigne; [avoir, remaindre (rester)]
5. Mur ne citet n'i est remés a fraindre [être, remaindre (rester), fraindre (briser)]
6. Fors Saraguce, ki est en une muntaigne. [être]
7. Li reis Marsilie la tient, ki Deu nen amet, [tenir, aimer]
8. Mahumet sert e Apollin recleimet: [servir, réclamer]
9. Nes poet guarder que mals ne l'i ateignet. AOI. [pouvoir, garder, atteindre]

**Translation**
1. Charles the king, our great emperor,
2. has been in Spain seven full years:
3. he conquered the high land all the way to the sea.
4. There is no fortress that stands against him;
5. no wall or city is left to be breached
6. except Saragossa, which sits upon a mountain.
7. King Marsile holds it — he who does not love God,
8. serves Mahomet and calls upon Apollin;
9. he cannot keep harm from reaching him there. AOI.

---

## Laisse II (lines 10–23)

**Original**
10. Li reis Marsilie esteit en Sarraguce. [être]
11. Alez en est en un verger suz l'umbre; [aller, être]
12. Sur un perrun de marbre bloi se culchet, [se coucher]
13. Envirun lui plus de vint milie humes.
14. Il en apelet e ses dux e ses cuntes: [appeler]
15. «Oëz, seignurs, quel pecchet nus encumbret: [ouïr (entendre), encombrer (accabler)]
16. Li emper[er]es Carles de France dulce
17. En cest païs nos est venuz cunfundre. [être, venir, confondre]
18. Jo nen ai ost qui bataille li dunne, [avoir, donner]
19. Ne n'ai tel gent ki la sue derumpet. [avoir, derumpre (rompre)]
20. Cunseilez mei cume mi savie hume, [conseiller]
21. Si m(e) guarisez e de mort et de hunte.» [garir (guérir)] <!-- uncertain: guarir here = 'protéger/sauver' rather than modern 'guérir' -->
22. N'i ad paien ki un sul mot respundet, [avoir, répondre]
23. Fors Blancandrins de Castel de Valfunde.

**Translation**
10. King Marsile was in Saragossa.
11. He has gone off into an orchard beneath the shade;
12. Upon a block of blue-grey marble he lies down,
13. Around him more than twenty thousand men.
14. He calls forth both his dukes and his counts:
15. «Hear, my lords, what calamity weighs upon us:
16. Charlemagne the emperor of sweet France
17. Has come into this land to destroy us.
18. I have no army that may give him battle,
19. Nor have I such a host as might break his.
20. Counsel me, as my wise men,
21. And save me from death and from shame.»
22. There is no pagan who answers a single word,
23. Except Blancandrin from the Castle of Valfunde.

---

## Laisse III (lines 24–46)

**Original**
24. Blancandrins fut des plus saives paiens: [être]
25. De vasselage fut asez chevaler, [être]
26. Prozdom i out pur sun seignur aider; [avoir, aider]
27. E dist al rei: «Ore ne vus esmaiez! [dire, esmaier (s'inquiéter)]
28. Mandez Carlun a l'orguillus, (e) al fier, [mander]
29. Fedeilz servises e mult granz amistez.
30. Vos li durrez urs e leons e chens, [donner]
31. Set cenz camelz e mil hosturs muers,
32. D'or e d'argent .IIII.C. muls cargez, [charger]
33. Cinquante carre, qu'en ferat carier: [faire, carier (charrier)]
34. Ben en purrat luer ses soldeiers. [pouvoir, louer]
35. En ceste tere ad asez osteiet; [avoir, osteier (guerroyer)]
36. En France, ad Ais, s'en deit ben repairer. [devoir, repairer (rentrer)]
37. Vos le sivrez a la feste seint Michel: [suivre]
38. Si recevrez la lei de chrestiens, [recevoir]
39. Serez ses hom par honur e par ben. [être]
40. S'en volt ostages, e vos l'en enveiez, [vouloir, envoyer]
41. U dis u vint pur lui afiancer. [afiancer (garantir)]
42. Enveiu[n]s i les filz de noz muillers: [envoyer]
43. Par nun d'ocire i enveierai le men. [ocire (tuer), envoyer]
44. Asez est melz qu'il i perdent le chefs, [être, perdre]
45. Que nus perduns l'onur ne la deintet, [perdre]
46. Ne nus seiuns cunduiz a mendeier.» AOI. [être, conduire, mendier]

**Translation**
24. Blancandrin was among the wisest pagans:
25. In valor he was very much a knight,
26. A worthy man, and there he was to aid his lord;
27. And he said to the king: «Now do not be dismayed!
28. Send word to Charles, to the proud, to the fierce,
29. Of loyal services and very great friendship.
30. You will give him bears and lions and dogs,
31. Seven hundred camels and a thousand moulted hawks,
32. Four hundred mules laden with gold and silver,
33. Fifty carts, that he will have carried away: <!-- uncertain: "qu'en ferat carier" - "which he will have carted/hauled" -->
34. Well will he be able to pay his soldiers with it.
35. In this land he has campaigned long enough;
36. To France, to Aix, he ought well to return.
37. You will follow him there at the feast of Saint Michael:
38. Thus you will receive the law of the Christians,
39. You will be his man in honor and in good faith.
40. If he wants hostages, then send them to him,
41. Either ten or twenty, to give him assurance.
42. Let us send there the sons of our wives:
43. Even at the risk of death I will send mine. <!-- uncertain: "Par nun d'ocire" - lit. "in the name of killing," i.e. though it mean death -->
44. It is far better that they lose their heads,
45. Than that we lose our honor and our dignity,
46. And that we be reduced to begging.» AOI.

---

## Laisse IV (lines 47–61)

**Original**
47. Dist Blancandrins: «Pa[r] ceste meie destre [dire]
48. E par la barbe ki al piz me ventelet, [venteler (flotter)]
49. L'ost des Franceis verrez sempres desfere. [voir, desfaire (défaire)]
50. Francs s'en irunt en France la lur tere. [aller]
51. Quant cascuns ert a sun meillor repaire, [être]
52. Carles serat ad Ais, a sa capele; [être]
53. A seint Michel tendrat mult halte feste. [tenir]
54. Vendrat li jurz, si passerat li termes, [venir, passer]
55. N'orrat de nos paroles ne nuveles. [oïr (entendre)]
56. Li reis est fiers e sis curages pesmes: [être]
57. De noz ostages ferat tre[n]cher les testes; [faire, trancher]
58. Asez est mielz, qu'il i perdent les testes, [être, perdre]
59. Que nus perduns clere Espaigne, la bele, [perdre]
60. Ne nus aiuns les mals ne les suffraites.» [avoir]
61. Dient paien: «Issi poet il ben estre!» [dire, pouvoir, être]

**Translation**
47. Blancandrin said: «By this my right hand
48. And by the beard that flutters on my chest,
49. You will soon see the host of the Franks disband.
50. The Franks will go away to France, their own land.
51. When each is back at his best dwelling,
52. Charles will be at Aix, in his chapel;
53. At Saint Michael's he will hold a very high feast.
54. The day will come, and so the term will pass,
55. He will hear of us neither word nor news.
56. The king is fierce and his heart is cruel:
57. He will have the heads of our hostages cut off;
58. It is far better that they lose their heads there,
59. Than that we lose bright Spain, the fair,
60. And that we have the woes and the privations.»
61. The pagans say: «It may well be so!»

---

## Laisse V (lines 62–77)

**Original**
62. Li reis Marsilie out sun cunseill finet: [avoir, finer (finir)]
63. Sin apelat Clarin (...) de Balaguet, [appeler]
64. Estamarin e Eudropin, sun per,
65. E Priamun e Guarlan le barbet,
66. E Machiner e sun uncle, Maheu,
67. E Joüner e Malbien d'ultremer,
68. E Blancandrins, por la raisun cunter. [conter]
69. Des plus feluns dís en ad apelez: [avoir, appeler]
70. «Seignurs baruns, a Carlemagnes irez; [aller]
71. Il est al siege a Cordres la citet. [être]
72. Branches d'olives en voz mains porterez, [porter]
73. Ço senefiet pais e humilitet. [senefier (signifier)]
74. Par voz saveirs sem puez acorder, [savoir, pouvoir, acorder (accorder)]
75. Jo vos durrai or e argent asez, [donner]
76. Teres e fiéz tant cum vos en vuldrez.» [vouloir]
77. Dient paien: «De ço avun nus asez!» AOI. [dire, avoir]

**Translation**
62. King Marsile had ended his council:
63. So he summoned Clarin (...) of Balaguer,
64. Estamarin and Eudropin, his peer,
65. And Priamon and Guarlan the bearded,
66. And Machiner and his uncle, Maheu,
67. And Jouner and Malbien from overseas,
68. And Blancandrin, to set forth the matter.
69. Of the most felonious he has summoned ten of them:
70. «Lord barons, you shall go to Charlemagne;
71. He is at the siege of the city of Cordres.
72. Olive branches you shall carry in your hands,
73. This signifies peace and humility.
74. By your wisdom, if you can bring me to an accord,
75. I shall give you gold and silver in plenty,
76. Lands and fiefs as much as you will want of them.»
77. The pagans say: «Of this we have enough!» AOI.

---

## Laisse VI (lines 78–88)

**Original**
78. Li reis Marsilie out finet sun cunseill; [avoir, finer (finir)]
79. Dist a ses humes: «Seignurs, vos en ireiz; [dire, aller]
80. Branches d'olive en voz mains portereiz, [porter]
81. Si me direz a Carlemagne, le rei, [dire]
82. Pur le soen Deu qu'il ait m(er)ercit de mei. [avoir]
83. Ja einz ne verrat passer cest premer meis, [voir, passer]
84. Que jel sivrai od mil de mes fedeilz, [sivre (suivre)]
85. Si recevrai la chrestiene lei, [recevoir]
86. [S]erai ses hom par amur e par feid; [être]
87. S'il voelt ostages, il en avrat par veir.» [voloir (vouloir), avoir]
88. Dist Blancandrins: «Mult bon plait en avreiz.» AOI. [dire, avoir]

**Translation**
78. King Marsile had ended his council;
79. He said to his men: «Lords, you shall go forth;
80. Olive branches you shall carry in your hands,
81. And you shall say for me to Charlemagne, the king,
82. For the sake of his God, that he have mercy on me.
83. He will not see this first month pass
84. Before I follow him with a thousand of my faithful,
85. And I will receive the Christian faith,
86. I will be his man through love and through faith;
87. If he wants hostages, he shall have them indeed.»
88. Blancandrin said: «You shall have a very good agreement out of it.» AOI.

---

## Laisse VII (lines 89–95)

**Original**
89. Dis blanches mules fist amener Marsilies, [faire, amener]
90. Que li tramist li reis de Suatilie; [tramettre (transmettre)]
91. Li frein sunt d'or, les seles d'argent mises. [être, mettre]
92. Cil sunt muntez ki le message firent, [être, monter, faire]
93. Enz en lur mains portent branches d'olive. [porter]
94. Vindrent a Charles ki France ad en baillie: [venir, avoir]
95. Nes poet guarder que alques ne l'engignent. AOI. [pouvoir, garder, engigner (tromper)]

**Translation**
89. Marsile had ten white mules brought,
90. Which the king of Suatilie had sent to him;
91. The bridles are of gold, the saddles set with silver.
92. Those who carried the message have mounted,
93. In their hands they bear branches of olive.
94. They came to Charles, who has France in his keeping:
95. He cannot guard against their deceiving him in some way. AOI. <!-- uncertain: "alques" = "somewhat/in some way"; "Nes" = "ne s'(en)" reading -->

---

## Laisse VIII (lines 96–121)

**Original**
96. Li empereres se fait e balz e liez, [faire]
97. Cordres ad prise e les murs peceiez, [avoir, prendre, peceier (dépecer)]
98. Od ses cadables les turs en abatied. [abattre]
99. Mult grant eschech en unt si chevaler [avoir]
100. D'or e d argent e de guarnemenz chers.
101. En la citet nen ad remes paien, [avoir, remaindre (rester)]
102. Ne seit ocis, u devient chrestien. [être, ocire (tuer), devenir]
103. Li empereres est en un grant verger, [être]
104. Ensembl od lui Rollant et oliver
105. Sansun li dux e anseis li fiers
106. Gefreid d anjou le rei gunfanuner,
107. E si i furent e gerin et gerers, [être]
108. La u cist furent, des altres i out bien: [être, avoir]
109. De dulce france i ad quinze milliers. [avoir]
110. Sur palies blancs siedent cil cevaler, [seoir (asseoir)]
111. As tables juent pur els esbaneier [jouer, esbaneier (s'amuser)]
112. E as eschecs li plus saive e li veill,
113. E escremissent cil bacheler leger. [escrimer]
114. Desuz un pin delez un eglenter
115. Un faldestoed i unt fait tut d or mer, [avoir, faire]
116. La siet li reis, ki dulce france tient. [seoir (asseoir), tenir]
117. Blanche ad la barbe e tut flurit le chef, [avoir, fleurir]
118. Gent ad le cors e le cuntenant fier, [avoir]
119. S'est, kil demandet, ne l estoet enseigner. [être, demander, estovoir (falloir), enseigner]
120. E li message descendirent a pied, [descendre]
121. Sil saluerent par amur e par bien. [saluer]

**Translation**
96. The emperor makes himself both joyful and glad,
97. He has taken Cordres and shattered its walls,
98. With his siege engines he beats down its towers.
99. His knights take very great booty from it
100. Of gold and silver and costly war-gear.
101. In the city there remains no pagan,
102. Who is not slain, or becomes a Christian.
103. The emperor is in a great orchard,
104. Together with him Roland and Oliver,
105. Duke Samson and Anseïs the fierce,
106. Geoffrey of Anjou, the king's standard-bearer,
107. And there too were Gerin and Gerier,
108. Where these were, there were indeed many others:
109. From sweet France there are fifteen thousand.
110. Upon white silks those knights are seated,
111. At backgammon they play to amuse themselves
112. And at chess the wisest and the old,
113. And the nimble young squires fence.
114. Beneath a pine, beside an eglantine,
115. A throne they have made all of pure gold,
116. There sits the king who holds sweet France.
117. White is his beard and all blossomed his head,
118. Noble is his body and his bearing proud,
119. He is so, whoever seeks him, that there is no need to point him out.
120. And the messengers dismounted on foot,
121. And greeted him with love and good will.

---

## Laisse IX (lines 122–138)

**Original**
122. Blancandrins ad tut premereins parled, [avoir, parler]
123. E dist al rei: «Salvez seiez de Deu [dire, être]
124. Le glorius, que de[v]u[n]s aürer! [devoir, aürer (adorer)]
125. Iço vus mandet reis Marsilies, li bers: [mander]
126. Enquis ad mult la lei de salvetez; [enquérir, avoir]
127. De sun aveir vos voelt asez duner, [vouloir, donner]
128. Urs e leuns e veltres enchaignez, [enchaîner]
129. Set cenz cameilz e mil hosturs muez, [muer]
130. D'or e d'argent .IIII. cenz muls trussez, [trousser]
131. Cinquante care, que carier en ferez; [carier (charroyer), faire]
132. Tant i avrat de besanz esmerez [avoir, esmerer (affiner)]
133. Dunt bien purrez voz soldeiers luer. [pouvoir, luer (payer)]
134. En cest païs avez estet asez; [avoir, être]
135. En France, ad Ais, devez bien repairer; [devoir, repairer (rentrer)]
136. La vos sivrat, ço dit mis avoez.» [suivre, dire, avouer]
137. Li empereres tent (...) ses mains vers Deu, [tendre]
138. Baisset sun chef, si cumencet a penser. AOI. [baisser, commencer, penser]

**Translation**
122. Blancandrin spoke first of all,
123. and said to the king: «Be saved by God
124. the glorious, whom we must adore!
125. This is what King Marsile, the baron, sends word to you:
126. he has inquired much into the law of salvation;
127. of his wealth he wishes to give you plenty,
128. bears and lions and leashed hunting-dogs,
129. seven hundred camels and a thousand moulted hawks,
130. four hundred mules loaded with gold and silver,
131. fifty carts, which you will have carted away;
132. there will be so much of it in refined bezants
133. that you will well be able to pay your soldiers.
134. In this land you have stayed long enough;
135. to France, to Aix, you ought rightly to return;
136. there he will follow you, so says my lord.» <!-- uncertain: "mis avoez" rendered as "my (acknowledged) lord/advocate" -->
137. The emperor stretches out his hands toward God,
138. he bows his head, and begins to think. AOI.

---

## Laisse X (lines 139–156)

**Original**
139. Li empereres en tint sun chef enclin; [tenir]
140. De sa parole ne fut mie hastifs: [être]
141. Sa custume est qu'il parolet a leisír. [être, parler]
142. Quant se redrecet, mult par out fier lu vis; [redrecer (redresser), avoir]
143. Dist as messages: «Vus avez mult ben dit. [dire, avoir, dire]
144. Li reis Marsilies est mult mis enemis. [être]
145. De cez paroles que vos avez ci dit, [avoir, dire]
146. En quel mesure en purrai estre fiz?» [pouvoir, être]
147. - «Voet par hostages,» ço dist li Sarrazins, [vouloir, dire]
148. «Dunt vos avrez ú dis ú quinze ú vint. [avoir]
149. Pa[r] num de ocire i metrai un mien filz, [ocire (tuer), mettre]
150. E sin avrez, ço quid, de plus gentilz. [avoir, cuidier (croire)]
151. Quant vus serez el palais seignurill, [être]
152. A la grant feste seint Michel del Peril,
153. Mis avoez la vos sivrat, ço dit; [sivre (suivre), dire]
154. Enz en voz bainz que Deus pur vos i fist, [faire]
155. La vuldrat il chrestiens devenir.» [vouloir, devenir]
156. Charles respunt: «Uncore purrat guarir.» AOI. [répondre, pouvoir, guarir (guérir)]

**Translation**
139. The emperor kept his head bowed;
140. He was in no way hasty with his speech:
141. His custom is that he speaks at leisure.
142. When he straightens up, his face looked most proud;
143. He said to the messengers: «You have spoken very well.
144. King Marsile is a great enemy of mine.
145. Concerning these words that you have spoken here,
146. In what measure can I be assured of it?»
147. - «By hostages,» said the Saracen,
148. «Of whom you shall have ten or fifteen or twenty.
149. At risk of being slain, I will put there a son of mine, <!-- uncertain: "Par num de ocire" = "by way of/on pain of being killed" -->
150. And you shall have, I think, [some] of more noble birth.
151. When you are in the lordly palace,
152. At the great feast of Saint Michael of the Peril,
153. My lord will follow you there, so he says; <!-- uncertain: "Mis avoez" = my advocate/overlord -->
154. There within your baths that God made for you,
155. There he will wish to become a Christian.»
156. Charles answers: «He may yet be saved.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XI (lines 157–167)

**Original**
157. Bels fut li vespres e li soleilz fut cler. [être, être]
158. Les dis mulez fait Char[l]es establer, [faire, establer (mettre à l'écurie)]
159. El grant verger fait li reis tendre un tref, [faire, tendre]
160. Les dis messages ad fait enz hosteler; [avoir, faire, hosteler (héberger)]
161. .XII. serjanz les unt ben cunreez. [avoir, cunreer (équiper)]
162. La noit demurent tresque vint al jur cler. [demeurer, venir]
163. Li empereres est par matin levet; [être, lever]
164. Messe e matines ad li reis escultet. [avoir, esculter (écouter)]
165. Desuz un pin en est li reis alez, [être, aller]
166. Ses baruns mandet pur sun cunseill finer: [mander, finer (finir)]
167. Par cels de France voelt il del tut errer. AOI. [vouloir, errer (voyager)]

**Translation**
157. Beautiful was the evening and the sun was bright.
158. The ten mules Charles has stabled,
159. In the great orchard the king has a tent pitched,
160. The ten messengers he has had lodged within;
161. Twelve sergeants have well equipped them.
162. The night they remain until the bright day came.
163. The emperor is risen at morning;
164. Mass and matins the king has heard. <!-- uncertain: "escultet" here = attended/heard the service -->
165. Beneath a pine the king has gone,
166. His barons he summons to settle his counsel:
167. Through those of France he wishes to act in everything. AOI. <!-- uncertain: "errer del tut" = proceed/act entirely by means of the men of France -->

---

## Laisse XII (lines 168–179)

**Original**
168. Li emper[er]es s'en vait desuz un pin. [s'en aller]
169. Ses baruns mandet pur sun cunseill fenir: [mander, finer (finir)]
170. Le duc Oger, (e) l'arcevesque Turpin,
171. Richard li Vélz e sun nev[old] Henri,
172. E de Gascuigne li proz quens Acelin
173. Tedbald de Reins e Milun, sun cusin;
174. E si i furent e Gerers e Gerin; [être]
175. Ensembl' od els li quens Rollant i vint, [venir]
176. E Oliver, li proz e li gentilz;
177. Des Francs de France en i ad plus de mil. [avoir]
178. Guenes i vint, ki la traïsun fist. [venir, faire]
179. Des ore cumencet le cunseill que mal prist. AOI. [commencer, prendre]

**Translation**
168. The emperor goes off beneath a pine tree.
169. He summons his barons to settle his counsel:
170. Duke Ogier, (and) Archbishop Turpin,
171. Richard the Old and his nephew Henri,
172. And from Gascony the valiant count Acelin,
173. Tedbald of Reims and Milun, his cousin;
174. And there too were Gerers and Gerin;
175. Together with them Count Roland came there,
176. And Oliver, the valiant and the noble;
177. Of the Franks of France there are more than a thousand of them.
178. Guenes came there, who committed the treason.
179. From now begins the counsel that came to ill. AOI. <!-- uncertain: "que mal prist" lit. "which took ill/turned out badly" -->

---

## Laisse XIII (lines 180–192)

**Original**
180. «Seignurs barons,» dist li emperere Carles, [dire]
181. «Li reis Marsilie m'ad tramis ses messages; [avoir, tramettre (transmettre)]
182. De sun aveir me voelt duner grant masse, [vouloir, donner]
183. Urs e leuns e veltres caeignables,
184. Set cenz cameilz e mil hosturs muables,
185. Quatre cenz mulz cargez del ór d'Arabe, [charger]
186. Avoec iço plus de cinquante care;
187. Mais il me mandet que en France m'en alge: [mander, aller]
188. Il me sivrat ad Aís, a mun estage, [suivre]
189. Si recevrat la nostre lei plus salve; [recevoir]
190. Chrestiens ert, de mei tendrat ses marches; [être, tenir]
191. Mais jo ne sai quels en est sis curages.» [savoir, être]
192. Dient Franceis: «Il nus i cuvent guarde!» AOI. [dire, cuvenir (falloir)] <!-- uncertain: cuvent = impersonal "it behooves/is fitting"; nearest modern sense is falloir -->

**Translation**
180. «My lord barons,» said the emperor Charles,
181. «King Marsile has sent me his messengers;
182. From his wealth he wishes to give me a great quantity,
183. Bears and lions and leashed hunting-dogs,
184. Seven hundred camels and a thousand mewed hawks,
185. Four hundred mules laden with the gold of Arabia,
186. Along with this, more than fifty carts;
187. But he commands me that I depart from France: <!-- uncertain: "mandet ... que ... m'en alge" read as a request/demand that Charles go to Aix, i.e. leave France for home -->
188. He will follow me to Aix, to my residence,
189. And there will receive our most saving faith;
190. He will be a Christian, will hold his marches from me;
191. But I do not know what his intention is.»
192. The Franks say: «We must be on our guard in this!» AOI.

---

## Laisse XIV (lines 193–213)

**Original**
193. Li empereres out sa raisun fenie. [avoir, finer (finir)]
194. Li quens Rollant, ki ne l'otriet mie, [otreier (octroyer)]
195. En piez se drecet, si li vint cuntredire. [se dresser, venir, contredire]
196. Il dist al rei: «Ja mar crerez Marsilie. [dire, croire]
197. Set anz [ad] pleins, que en Espaigne venimes; [avoir, venir]
198. Jo vos cunquis e Noples e Commibles, [conquérir]
199. Pris ai Valterne e la tere de Pine [prendre, avoir]
200. E Balasgued e Tuele e Sezilie.
201. Li reis Marsilie i fist mult que traïtre: [faire]
202. De ses pai[ens il vus] enveiat quinze, [envoyer]
203. Cha(n)cuns portout une branche d'olive; [porter]
204. Nuncerent vos cez paroles meïsme. [noncier (annoncer)]
205. A vos Franceis un cunseill en presistes: [prendre]
206. Loerent vos alques de legerie. [loer (conseiller)]
207. Dous de voz cuntes al paien tramesistes, [tramettre (transmettre)]
208. L'un fut Basan e li altres Basilies; [être]
209. Les chef en prist es puis desuz Haltilie. [prendre]
210. Faites la guer[re] cum vos l'avez enprise: [faire, avoir, entreprendre]
211. En Sarraguce menez vostre ost banie, [mener]
212. Metez le sege a tute vostre vie, [mettre]
213. Si vengez cels que li fels fist ocire!» AOI. [venger, faire, ocire (tuer)]

**Translation**
193. The emperor had finished his speech.
194. Count Roland, who did not agree to it at all,
195. Rises to his feet, and came to contradict him.
196. He said to the king: «It would be folly to trust Marsile. <!-- uncertain: "Ja mar crerez" = "you will wrongly believe / it is ill that you should believe" -->
197. Seven full years it is, since we came into Spain;
198. I conquered for you both Noples and Commibles,
199. I have taken Valterne and the land of Pine
200. And Balasgued and Tuele and Sezilie.
201. King Marsile acted there very much like a traitor:
202. Of his pagans he sent you fifteen,
203. Each one carried an olive branch;
204. They announced to you these very words.
205. With your Franks you took a counsel on it:
206. They advised you somewhat foolishly. <!-- uncertain: "alques de legerie" = "somewhat out of lightness/rashness" -->
207. Two of your counts you sent to the pagan,
208. The one was Basan and the other Basile;
209. He took their heads in the hills below Haltilie. <!-- uncertain: "Les chef en prist" = he had their heads taken / beheaded them -->
210. Wage the war as you have undertaken it:
211. To Saragossa lead your summoned host,
212. Lay the siege for the whole of your life,
213. And avenge those whom the villain had killed!» AOI.

---

## Laisse XV (lines 214–229)

**Original**
214. Li empe[re]re en tint sun chef enbrunc, [tenir]
215. Si duist sa barbe, afaitad sun gernun, [duire (lisser), afaitier (arranger)]
216. Ne ben ne mal ne respunt sun nevuld. [répondre]
217. Franceis se taisent ne mais que Guenelun, [se taire]
218. En piez se drecet, si vint devant Carlun, [se dresser, venir]
219. Mult fierement cumencet sa raisun, [commencer]
220. E dist al rei: «Ja mar crerez bricun, [dire, croire]
221. Ne mei ne altre, se de vostre prod nun.
222. Quant ço vos mandet li reis Marsiliun, [mander]
223. Qu'il devendrat jointes ses mains tis hom, [devenir, joindre]
224. E tute Espaigne tendrat par vostre dun, [tenir]
225. Puis recevrat la lei que nus tenum, [recevoir, tenir]
226. Ki ço vos lodet que cest plait degetuns, [loer (conseiller), degeter (rejeter)]
227. Ne li chalt, sire, de quel mort nus muriuns. [chaloir (importer), mourir]
228. Cunseill d orguill n'est dreiz que a plus munt, [monter]
229. Laissun les fols, as sages nus tenuns.» AOI. [laisser, se tenir]

**Translation**
214. The emperor held his head bowed down,
215. He smoothed his beard, arranged his mustache,
216. Neither good nor ill does he answer his nephew.
217. The French keep silent, all save Ganelon,
218. He rises to his feet, and came before Charles,
219. Very fiercely he begins his speech,
220. And said to the king: «Never to your harm will you believe a knave,
221. Neither me nor another, except for your own profit. <!-- uncertain: "se de vostre prod nun" — "unless for your benefit" -->
222. When the king Marsile sends you word of this,
223. That he will become, his hands joined, your vassal,
224. And will hold all Spain by your gift,
225. Then will receive the faith that we hold,
226. Whoever advises you that we reject this agreement,
227. Cares not, sire, by what death we may die.
228. Counsel of pride, it is not right that it rise further, <!-- uncertain: "que a plus munt" — "that it amount to more / rise higher" -->
229. Let us leave the fools, let us hold to the wise.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XVI (lines 230–243)

**Original**
230. Apres iço i est Neimes venud; [être, venir]
231. Meillor vassal n'aveit en la curt nul, [avoir]
232. E dist al rei: «Ben l'avez entendud, [dire, avoir, entendre]
233. Guenes li quens ço vus ad respondud, [avoir, répondre]
234. Saveir i ad, mais qu'il seit entendud. [savoir, avoir, être, entendre]
235. Li reis Marsilie est de guere vencud: [être, vaincre]
236. Vos li avez tuz ses castels toluz, [avoir, tolir (enlever)]
237. Od voz caables avez fruiset ses murs, [avoir, fruisier (briser)]
238. Ses citez arses e ses humes vencuz; [ardre (brûler), vaincre]
239. Quant il vos mandet, qu'aiez mercit de lui, [mander, avoir]
240. Pecchet fereit, ki dunc li fesist plus, [faire]
241. U par ostage vos (en) voelt faire soürs; [vouloir, faire]
242. Ceste grant guerre ne deit munter a plus.» [devoir, monter]
243. Dient Franceis: «Ben ad parlet li dux.» AOI. [dire, avoir, parler]

**Translation**
230. After this, Naimes came forward;
231. There was no better vassal in the court,
232. And he said to the king: «You have indeed heard it,
233. Ganelon the count has answered you thus,
234. There is wisdom in it, provided it be heeded.
235. King Marsile is defeated in war:
236. You have taken from him all his castles,
237. With your siege engines you have shattered his walls,
238. Burned his cities and vanquished his men;
239. When he sends word to you, that you have mercy on him,
240. He would commit a sin, whoever should then do more to him, <!-- uncertain: sense of "ki dunc li fesist plus" -->
241. Or by hostages he wishes to give you assurance; <!-- uncertain: "faire soürs" = render secure/give guarantees -->
242. This great war ought not to mount up to more.»
243. The Franks say: «The duke has spoken well.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XVII (lines 244–251)

**Original**
244. - «Seignurs baruns, qui i enveieruns [envoyer]
245. En Sarraguce al rei Marsiliuns?»
246. Respunt dux Neimes: «Jo irai par vostre dun! [répondre, aller]
247. Livrez m'en ore le guant e le bastun.» [livrer]
248. Respunt li reis: «Vos estes saives hom; [répondre, être]
249. Par ceste barbe e par cest men gernun,
250. Vos n'irez pas uan de mei si luign. [aller]
251. Alez sedeir, quant nuls ne vos sumunt.» [aller, seoir (asseoir), semondre (inviter)]

**Translation**
244. «My lord barons, whom shall we send
245. To Saragossa, to King Marsile?»
246. Duke Naimes replies: «I will go, by your leave!
247. Hand me now the glove and the staff.»
248. The king replies: «You are a wise man;
249. By this beard and by this moustache of mine,
250. You shall not go this year so far from me.
251. Go sit down, since no one summons you.»

---

## Laisse XVIII (lines 252–263)

**Original**
252. - «Seignurs baruns, qui i purruns enveier [pouvoir, envoyer]
253. Al Sarrazin ki Sarraguce tient?» [tenir]
254. Respunt Rollant: «Jo i puis aler mult ben!» [répondre, pouvoir, aller]
255. - «Nu ferez certes!» dist li quens Oliver; [faire, dire]
256. «Vostre curages est mult pesmes e fiers; [être]
257. Jo me crendreie, que vos vos meslisez. [craindre, mesler (se quereller)]
258. Se li reis voelt, jo i puis aler ben.» [vouloir, pouvoir, aller]
259. Respunt li reis: «Ambdui vos en taisez! [répondre, se taire]
260. Ne vos ne il n'i porterez les piez. [porter]
261. Par ceste barbe que veez [blancheier], [voir, blancheier (blanchir)]
262. Li duze per mar i serunt jugez!» [être, juger]
263. Franceis se taisent: as les vus aquisez. [se taire, aquiser (apaiser)]

**Translation**
252. - «Lord barons, whom can we send
253. To the Saracen who holds Saragossa?»
254. Roland answers: «I can go there very well!»
255. - «You shall certainly not!» said Count Oliver;
256. «Your temper is very harsh and fierce;
257. I would fear that you would come to blows.
258. If the king wills it, I can well go there.»
259. The king answers: «Both of you, be silent about it!
260. Neither you nor he shall set foot there.
261. By this beard that you see growing white,
262. The twelve peers would be ill-judged [for it]!» <!-- uncertain: "mar i serunt jugez" — sense is that they will be doomed/condemned to misfortune -->
263. The Franks fall silent: behold them quieted.

---

## Laisse XIX (lines 264–273)

**Original**
264. Turpins de Reins en est levet del renc, [être, lever]
265. E dist al rei: «Laisez ester voz Francs! [dire, laisser, ester (rester)]
266. En cest païs avez estet set anz; [avoir, être]
267. Mult unt oüd e peines e ahans. [avoir]
268. Dunez m'en, sire, le bastun e le guant, [donner]
269. E jo irai al Sarazin en Espaigne, [aller]
270. Sin vois vedeir alques de sun semblant.» [aller, voir]
271. Li empereres respunt par maltalant: [répondre]
272. «Alez sedeir desur cel palie blanc! [aller, seoir (asseoir)]
273. N'en parlez mais, se jo nel vos cumant!» AOI. [parler, commander]

**Translation**
264. Turpin of Reims has risen from the ranks,
265. And said to the king: «Let your Franks be!
266. In this land you have been seven years;
267. Much have they endured, both hardships and toils.
268. Give me, sire, the staff and the glove,
269. And I shall go to the Saracen in Spain,
270. And go see something of his disposition.» <!-- uncertain: "alques de sun semblant" = "somewhat of his appearance/bearing" -->
271. The emperor answers with displeasure:
272. «Go sit down upon that white silk cloth!
273. Speak of it no more, unless I command you to!» AOI.

---

## Laisse XX (lines 274–295)

**Original**
274. - «Francs chevalers,» dist li emperere Carles, [dire]
275. «Car m'eslisez un barun de ma marche, [eslire (choisir)]
276. Qu'a Marsiliun me portast mun message.» [porter]
277. Ço dist Rollant: «Ço ert Guenes, mis parastre.» [dire, être]
278. Dient Franceis: «Car il le poet ben faire; [dire, pouvoir, faire]
279. Se lui lessez, n'i trametrez plus saive.» [laisser, tramettre (transmettre)]
280. E li quens Guenes en fut mult anguisables; [être]
281. De sun col getet ses grandes pels de martre, [jeter]
282. E est remes en sun blialt de palie. [être, remaindre (rester)]
283. Vairs out [les oilz] e mult fier lu visage, [avoir]
284. Gent out le cors e les costez out larges; [avoir]
285. Tant par fut bels tuit si per l'en esguardent. [être, esgarder (regarder)]
286. Dist a Rollant: «Tut fol, pur quei t'esrages? [dire, esragier (s'enrager)]
287. Ço set hom ben que jo sui tis parastres; [savoir, être]
288. Si as juget qu'a Marsiliun en alge! [avoir, juger, aller]
289. Se Deus ço dunet que jo de la repaire, [donner, repairer (rentrer)]
290. Jo t'en muvra[i] un si grant contr[a]ire [mouvoir (susciter)]
291. Ki durerat a trestut tun edage.» [durer]
292. Respunt Rollant: «Orgoill ói e folage. [répondre, oïr (entendre)]
293. Ço set hom ben, n'ai cure de manace; [savoir, avoir]
294. Mai[s] saives hom, il deit faire message: [devoir, faire]
295. Si li reis voelt, prez sui por vus le face.» AOI. [vouloir, être, faire]

**Translation**
274. - «Frankish knights,» said the emperor Charles,
275. «Now choose me a baron of my march,
276. Who would carry my message to Marsile.»
277. Rollant said this: «That will be Guenes, my stepfather.»
278. The Franks say: «For he can do it well;
279. If you leave him aside, you will not send a wiser one.»
280. And the count Guenes was very distressed by it;
281. From his neck he throws his great pelts of marten,
282. And he remained in his tunic of silk.
283. He had bright eyes and a very fierce face,
284. He had a noble body and his sides were broad;
285. He was so very handsome that all his peers gaze at him.
286. He said to Rollant: «Utter fool, why do you rage?
287. One knows well that I am your stepfather;
288. So you have decided that I should go to Marsile!
289. If God grants that I return from there,
290. I will stir up against you such a great calamity
291. That it will last all your lifetime.»
292. Rollant answers: «I hear arrogance and folly.
293. One knows well, I have no care for a threat;
294. But a wise man, he ought to carry a message:
295. If the king wishes, I am ready to do it for you.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XXI (lines 296–302)

**Original**
296. Guenes respunt: «Pur mei n'iras tu mie! [répondre, aller]
297. Tu n'ies mes hom ne jo ne sui tis sire. [être]
298. Carles comandet que face sun servise: [commander, faire]
299. En Sarraguce en irai a Marsilie; [aller]
300. Einz i f[e]rai un poi de [le]gerie, [faire]
301. Que jo n'esclair ceste meie grant ire.» [esclairier (soulager)]
302. Quant l'ot Rollant, si cumençat a rire. AOI. [oïr (entendre), commencer, rire]

**Translation**
296. Ganelon answers: «You will not go at all on my behalf!
297. You are not my vassal, nor am I your lord.
298. Charles commands that I perform his service:
299. To Saragossa I shall go, to Marsile;
300. But first I shall do a bit of recklessness, <!-- uncertain: legerie = rashness/folly -->
301. To relieve this great wrath of mine.» <!-- uncertain: "Que jo n'esclair" rendered as purpose "so that I may relieve"; literal negation is awkward here -->
302. When Roland heard it, he began to laugh. AOI.

---

## Laisse XXII (lines 303–309)

**Original**
303. Quant ço veit Guenes que ore s'en rit Rollant, [voir, rire]
304. Dunc ad tel doel pur poi d'ire ne fent, [avoir, fendre]
305. A ben petit que il ne pert le sens; [perdre]
306. E dit al cunte: «Jo ne vus aim nient; [dire, aimer]
307. Sur mei avez turnet fals jugement. [avoir, tourner]
308. Dreiz emperere, veiz me ci en present, [voir]
309. Ademplir voeill vostre comandement.» AOI. [accomplir, vouloir]

**Translation**
303. When Ganelon sees that Roland now laughs about it,
304. then he has such grief that he nearly bursts from rage,
305. he very nearly loses his mind;
306. and he says to the count: «I do not love you at all;
307. you have turned a false judgment upon me.
308. Rightful emperor, here you see me present,
309. I will fulfill your command.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XXIII (lines 310–318)

**Original**
310. «En Sarraguce sai ben, [qu']aler m'estoet. [savoir, aller, estovoir (falloir)]
311. Hom ki la vait, repairer ne s'en poet. [aller, repairer (rentrer), pouvoir]
312. Ensurquetut si ai jo vostre soer, [avoir]
313. Sin ai un filz, ja plus bels n'en estoet: [avoir, estovoir (falloir)]
314. Ço est Baldewin,» ço dit, «ki ert prozdoem. [être, dire, être]
315. A lui lais jo mes honurs e mes fieus. [laisser]
316. Gua[r]dez le ben, ja nel verrai des oilz.» [garder, voir]
317. Carles respunt: «trop avez tendre coer. [répondre, avoir]
318. Puisquel comant, aler vus en estoet.» [commander, aller, estovoir (falloir)]

**Translation**
310. «I know well that I must go to Saragossa.
311. A man who goes there cannot return from it.
312. Above all, I have your sister,
313. and by her I have a son, there is none more handsome:
314. it is Baldwin,» he says, «who will be a worthy man.
315. To him I leave my lands and my fiefs.
316. Guard him well, for I shall never see him again with my eyes.»
317. Charles answers: «you have too tender a heart.
318. Since I command it, you must go.»

---

## Laisse XXIV (lines 319–330)

**Original**
319. Ço dist li reis: «Guenes venez avant. AOI. [dire, venir]
320. Si recevez le bastun e lu guant. [recevoir]
321. Oït l'avez, sur vos le jugent Franc.» [oïr (entendre), avoir, juger]
322. - «Sire,» dist Guenes, «ço ad tut fait Rollant! [dire, avoir, faire]
323. Ne l'amerai a trestut mun vivant, [aimer, vivre]
324. Ne Oliver, por ço qu'il est si cumpainz; [être]
325. Li duze per, por [ço] qu'il l'aiment tant, [aimer]
326. Desfi les ci, sire, vostre veiant.» [défier]
327. Ço dist li reis: «Trop avez maltalant. [dire, avoir]
328. Or irez vos certes, quant jol cumant.» [aller, commander]
329. - «Jo i puis aler, mais n'i avrai guarant: AOI. [pouvoir, aller, avoir]
330. Nu l'out Basilies ne sis freres Basant.» [avoir]

**Translation**
319. The king said: «Ganelon, come forward. AOI.
320. And receive the staff and the glove.
321. You have heard it, the Franks judge it against you.»
322. - «Sire,» said Ganelon, «Roland has done all this!
323. I shall not love him all the days of my life,
324. Nor Oliver, because he is his companion;
325. The twelve peers, because they love him so much,
326. I defy them here, sire, in your sight.»
327. The king said: «You have too much ill will.
328. Now you will go indeed, since I command it.»
329. - «I can go there, but I shall have no protection: AOI.
330. Basil had none of it, nor his brother Basant.»

---

## Laisse XXV (lines 331–336)

**Original**
331. Li empereres li tent sun guant le destre; [tendre]
332. Mais li quens Guenes iloec ne volsist estre: [vouloir, être]
333. Quant le dut prendre, si li caït a tere. [devoir, prendre, cheoir (tomber)]
334. Dient Franceis: «Deus! que purrat ço estre? [dire, pouvoir, être]
335. De cest message nos avendrat grant perte.» [avenir (arriver)]
336. - «Seignurs» dist Guenes, «vos en orrez noveles!» [dire, oïr (entendre)]

**Translation**
331. The emperor holds out to him his right glove;
332. But Count Ganelon would not wish to be there:
333. When he had to take it, it fell from him to the ground.
334. The Franks say: «God! what can this be?
335. From this mission great loss will befall us.»
336. - «Lords,» said Ganelon, «you will hear news of it!»

---

## Laisse XXVI (lines 337–341)

**Original**
337. - «Sire,» dist Guenes, «dunez mei le cungied; [dire, donner]
338. Quant aler dei, n'i ai plus que targer.» [aller, devoir, avoir, targier (tarder)]
339. Ço dist li reis: «Al Jhesu e al mien!» [dire]
340. De sa main destre l'ad asols e seignet, [avoir, absoudre, seignier (bénir)]
341. Puis li livrat le bastun e le bref. [livrer]

**Translation**
337. - «Lord,» said Ganelon, «give me leave to go;
338. Since I must depart, there is no more reason for me to tarry.»
339. The king said this: «In Jesus' keeping and in mine!»
340. With his right hand he absolved and blessed him,
341. Then he handed over to him the staff and the letter.

---

## Laisse XXVII (lines 342–365)

**Original**
342. Guenes li quens s'en vait a sun ostel, [s'en aller]
343. De guarnemenz se prent a cunreer, [se prendre, cunreer (équiper)]
344. De ses meillors que il pout recuvrer: [pouvoir, recuvrer (recouvrer)]
345. Esperuns d'or ad en ses piez fermez, [avoir, fermer]
346. Ceint Murglies, s'espee, a sun costed; [ceindre]
347. En Tachebrun, sun destrer est munted; [être, monter]
348. L'estreu li tint sun uncle Guinemer. [tenir]
349. La veïsez tant chevaler plorer, [voir, plorer (pleurer)]
350. Ki tuit li dient «Tant mare fustes, ber! [dire, être]
351. En (la) cort al rei mult i avez ested, [avoir, être]
352. Noble vassal vos i solt hom clamer. [soleir (avoir coutume), clamer (appeler)]
353. Ki ço jugat, que doüsez aler [juger, devoir, aller]
354. Par Charlemagne n'er(cs) guariz ne tensez. [être, garir (protéger), tenser (protéger)]
355. Li quens Rollant nel se doüst penser, [devoir, penser]
356. Que estrait estes de mult grant parented.» [estraire (descendre/être issu), être]
357. Enpres li dient: «Sire, car nos menez!» [dire, mener]
358. Ço respunt Guenes: «Ne placet Damnedeu! [répondre, plaire]
359. Mielz est que sul moerge que tant bon chevaler. [être, mourir]
360. En dulce France, seignurs, vos en irez: [s'en aller]
361. De meie part ma muiller saluez, [saluer]
362. E Pinabel, mun ami e mun per,
363. E Baldewin, mun filz que vos savez, [savoir]
364. E lui aidez e pur seignur le tenez.» [aider, tenir]
365. Entret en sa veie, si s'est achiminez. AOI. [entrer, s'acheminer]

**Translation**
342. Ganelon the count goes off to his lodging,
343. He sets about equipping himself with gear,
344. With the best of it that he could obtain:
345. He has fastened golden spurs on his feet,
346. He girds Murgleis, his sword, at his side;
347. He has mounted Tachebrun, his warhorse;
348. His uncle Guinemer held the stirrup for him.
349. There you would have seen so many knights weep,
350. Who all say to him: «What an evil hour was yours, baron!
351. In the king's court you have long been,
352. A noble vassal one was wont to call you there.
353. He who decreed that you should go
354. Will not be protected nor defended by Charlemagne.
355. Count Roland ought not to have thought of it,
356. Since you are descended from a very great lineage.»
357. Then they say to him: «Lord, do lead us along!»
358. Ganelon answers this: «May it not please the Lord God!
359. Better that I alone die than so many good knights.
360. To sweet France, lords, you will go back:
361. On my behalf greet my wife,
362. And Pinabel, my friend and my peer,
363. And Baldwin, my son whom you know,
364. And help him and hold him as your lord.»
365. He sets out on his way, and thus has started off. AOI.

---

## Laisse XXVIII (lines 366–376)

**Original**
366. Guenes chevalchet suz une olive halte, [chevaucher]
367. Asemblet s'est as sarrazins messag[es]. [être, s'assembler]
368. Mais Blancandrins ki envers lu s'atarget; [atargier (s'attarder)]
369. Par grant saveir parolet li uns a l'altre. [parler]
370. Dist Blancandrins: «Merveilus hom est Charles, [dire, être]
371. Ki cunquist Puille e trestute Calabre; [conquérir]
372. Vers Engletere passat il la mer salse, [passer]
373. Ad oes seint Perre en cunquist le chevage: [conquérir]
374. Que nus requert ça en la nostre marche?» [requérir]
375. Guenes respunt: «Itels est sis curages, [répondre, être]
376. Jamais n'ert hume ki encuntre lui vaille.» AOI. [être, valoir]

**Translation**
366. Ganelon rides beneath a tall olive tree,
367. He has joined the Saracen messengers;
368. But Blancandrin, who lingers back toward him —
369. With great wisdom the one speaks to the other.
370. Blancandrin said: «Charles is a marvelous man,
371. Who conquered Apulia and all of Calabria;
372. Toward England he crossed the salt sea,
373. For Saint Peter's use he won from it the tribute:
374. Why does he make demands on us here in our march?»
375. Ganelon answers: «Such is his temper,
376. Never will there be a man who can match himself against him.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XXIX (lines 377–391)

**Original**
377. Dist Blancandrins: «Francs sunt mult gentilz home; [dire, être]
378. Mult grant mal funt e [cil] duc e cil cunte [faire]
379. A lur seignur, ki tel cunseill li dunent: [donner]
380. Lui e altrui travaillent, e cunfundent.» [travailler, confondre]
381. Guenes respunt: «Jo ne sai veirs nul hume, [répondre, savoir]
382. Ne mes Rollant, ki uncore en avrat hunte. [avoir]
383. Er matin sedeit li emperere suz l'umbre; [seoir (asseoir)]
384. Vint i ses nies, out vestue sa brunie, [venir, avoir, vêtir]
385. E out predet dejuste Carcasonie; [avoir, preer (piller)]
386. En sa main tint une vermeille pume: [tenir]
387. «Tenez bel sire,» dist Rollant a sun uncle, [tenir, dire]
388. «De trestuz reis vos present les curunes.» [présenter]
389. Li soens orgoilz le devreit ben cunfundre, [devoir, confondre]
390. Kar chascun jur de mort [il] s'abandunet. [s'abandonner]
391. Seit, ki l'ociet, tute pais puis avriúmes.» AOI. [être, ocire (tuer), avoir]

**Translation**
377. Blancandrin said: «The Franks are very noble men;
378. Great harm do these dukes and these counts
379. To their lord, who give him such counsel:
380. They torment him and others, and bring ruin.»
381. Ganelon answers: «I know of no man truly,
382. Save Roland, who will yet have shame from it.
383. Yesterday morning the emperor sat in the shade;
384. His nephew came, had donned his hauberk,
385. And had taken booty near Carcassonne;
386. In his hand he held a crimson apple:
387. «Take it, fair lord,» said Roland to his uncle,
388. «Of all kings I present to you the crowns.»
389. His own pride ought well to bring him to ruin,
390. For each day he gives himself up to death.
391. Were someone to slay him, we should then have full peace.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XXX (lines 392–401)

**Original**
392. Dist Blancandrins: «Mult est pesmes Rollant, [dire, être]
393. Ki tute gent voelt faire recreant, [vouloir, faire]
394. E tutes teres met en chalengement! [mettre]
395. Par quele gent quiet il espleiter tant?» [cuidier (croire), esploitier (accomplir)]
396. Guenes respunt: «Par la franceise gent. [répondre]
397. Il l'a[i]ment tant ne li faldrunt nient; [aimer, faillir]
398. Or e argent lur met tant en present, [mettre]
399. Muls e destrers, e palies e guarnemenz;
400. L'emperere meïsmes ad tut a sun talent. [avoir]
401. Cunquerrat li les teres d'ici qu'en Orient.» AOI. [conquérir]

**Translation**
392. Blancandrin said: «Roland is most fearsome,
393. He who wants to make every people cry for mercy,
394. And puts all lands into contention!
395. With what people does he think to achieve so much?»
396. Ganelon replies: «With the French people.
397. They love him so much they will not fail him at all;
398. He puts so much gold and silver before them as gifts,
399. Mules and warhorses, and silks and armor;
400. The emperor himself has everything to his liking.
401. He will conquer lands for them from here to the Orient.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XXXI (lines 402–413)

**Original**
402. Tant chevalcherent Guenes e Blancandrins, [chevaucher]
403. Que l'un a l'altre la sue feit plevit, [plevir (jurer)]
404. Que il querreient, que Rollant fust ocis. [querre (chercher), être, ocire (tuer)]
405. Tant chevalcherent e veies e chemins, [chevaucher]
406. Que en Sarraguce descendent suz un if. [descendre]
407. Un faldestoet out suz l'umbre d'un pin; [avoir]
408. Esvolupet fut d'un palie alexandrin: [être, envoluper (envelopper)]
409. La fut li reis ki tute Espaigne tint; [être, tenir]
410. Tut entur lui vint milie Sarrazins.
411. N'i ad celoi ki mot sunt ne mot tint, [avoir, soner (dire), tentir (retentir)]
412. Pur les nuveles qu'il vuldreient oïr. [vouloir, oïr (entendre)]
413. Atant as vos Guenes e Blanchandrins.

**Translation**
402. So long did Ganelon and Blancandrin ride together,
403. That each pledged his faith to the other,
404. That they would seek a way for Roland to be killed.
405. So long did they ride, both byways and roads,
406. That in Saragossa they dismount beneath a yew.
407. There was a throne in the shade of a pine;
408. It was wrapped in a silk cloth of Alexandria:
409. There was the king who held all Spain;
410. All around him, twenty thousand Saracens.
411. There is none among them who speaks a word nor utters a word,
412. Because of the news they would wish to hear.
413. And behold, here come Ganelon and Blancandrin.

---

## Laisse XXXII (lines 414–424)

**Original**
414. Blancandrins vint devant l'empereür; [venir]
415. Par le puig[n] tint le cunte Guenelun, [tenir]
416. E dist al rei: «Salvez seiez de Mahun [dire, être]
417. E d'Apollin, qui seintes leis tenuns! [tenir]
418. Vostre message fesime[s] a Charlun. [faire]
419. Ambes ses mains en levat cuntre munt, [lever]
420. Loat sun Deu, ne fist altre respuns. [louer, faire]
421. Ci vos enveiet un sun noble barun, [envoyer]
422. Ki est de France, si est mult riches hom: [être]
423. Par lui orrez si avrez pais u nun.» [oïr (entendre), avoir]
424. Respunt Marsilie: «Or diet, nus l'orrum!» AOI. [répondre, dire, oïr (entendre)]

**Translation**
414. Blancandrin came before the emperor;
415. By the fist he held the count Ganelon,
416. And said to the king: «Be saved by Mahomet
417. And by Apollo, whose holy laws we hold!
418. We delivered your message to Charles.
419. He raised both his hands toward the heavens,
420. Praised his God, made no other reply.
421. He sends you here one of his noble barons,
422. Who is from France, and he is a very mighty man:
423. From him you will hear whether you will have peace or not.»
424. Marsile replies: «Let him speak now, we shall hear him!» AOI.

---

## Laisse XXXIII (lines 425–440)

**Original**
425. Mais li quens Guenes se fut ben purpenset. [se porpenser (réfléchir), être]
426. Par grant saver cumencet a parler [savoir, commencer, parler]
427. Cume celui ki ben faire le set, [faire, savoir]
428. E dist al rei: «Salvez seiez de Deu [dire, être]
429. Li Glorius, qui devum aürer! [devoir, aürer (adorer)]
430. Iço vus mandet Carlemagnes, li ber, [mander]
431. Que recevez seinte chrestientet; [recevoir]
432. Demi Espaigne vos voelt en fiu duner. [vouloir, donner]
433. Se cest acorde ne vulez otrier, [vouloir, otreier (octroyer)]
434. Pris e liez serez par poested; [prendre, lier, être]
435. Al siege ad Ais en serez amenet, [être, amener]
436. Par jugement serez iloec finet; [être, finer (finir)]
437. La murrez vus a hunte e a viltet.» [mourir]
438. Li reis Marsilies en fut mult esfreed. [être, esfreer (effrayer)]
439. Un algier tint, ki d'or fut enpenet, [tenir, être, empenner]
440. Ferir l'en volt, se n'en fust desturnet. AOI. [ferir (frapper), vouloir, être, détourner]

**Translation**
425. But Count Ganelon had thought it over well.
426. With great wisdom he begins to speak,
427. Like one who well knows how to do it,
428. And said to the king: «May you be saved by God,
429. The Glorious One, whom we must adore!
430. This is what Charlemagne the baron commands you:
431. That you receive holy Christianity;
432. He wishes to give you half of Spain in fief.
433. If you will not grant this accord,
434. You will be seized and bound by force,
435. To the seat at Aix you will be brought,
436. By judgment you will there be put to death;
437. There you will die in shame and in disgrace.»
438. King Marsile was much frightened by this.
439. He held a javelin, which was feathered with gold,
440. He wished to strike him with it, had he not been prevented. AOI.

---

## Laisse XXXIV (lines 441–450)

**Original**
441. Li reis Marsilies ad la culur muee; [avoir, muer]
442. De sun algeir ad la hanste crollee. [avoir, croller (secouer)]
443. Quant le vit Guenes, mist la main a l'espee, [voir, mettre]
444. Cuntre dous deie l'ad del furrer getee, [avoir, jeter]
445. Si li ad dit: «Mult estes bele e clere! [avoir, dire, être]
446. Tant vus avrai en curt a rei portee! [avoir, porter]
447. Ja nel dirat de France li emperere, [dire]
448. Que suls i moerge en l'estrange cuntree, [mourir]
449. Einz vos avrunt li meillor comperee.» [avoir, comperer (payer cher)]
450. Dient paien: «Desfaimes la mellee!» [dire, défaire]

**Translation**
441. King Marsile has changed color;
442. With his javelin he has shaken the shaft.
443. When Ganelon saw it, he put his hand to his sword,
444. He drew it two fingers' width out of the scabbard,
445. And said to it: «You are very fair and bright!
446. So long shall I have borne you at the king's court!
447. Never shall the emperor of France say
448. That I died alone in the foreign land,
449. Before the best men shall have paid dearly for you.»
450. The pagans say: «Let us break off the quarrel!»

---

## Laisse XXXV (lines 451–467)

**Original**
451. Tuit li preierent li meillor Sarrazin, [prier]
452. Qu'el faldestoed s'es[t] Marsilies asis. [s'asseoir]
453. Dist l'algalifes: «Mal nos avez baillit, [dire, avoir, baillir (traiter)]
454. Que li Franceis asmastes a ferir; [asmer (oser), ferir (frapper)]
455. Vos le doüssez esculter e oïr.» [devoir, esculter (écouter), oïr (entendre)]
456. - «Sire,» dist Guenes, «mei l'avent a suffrir; [dire, avenir (échoir), souffrir]
457. Jo ne lerreie, por l'or que Deus fist [laisser, faire]
458. Ne por tut l'aveir, ki seit en cest païs, [être]
459. Que jo ne li die, se tant ai de leisir, [dire, avoir]
460. Que Charles li mandet, li reis poesteïfs, [mander]
461. Par mei li mandet, sun mortel enemi.» [mander]
462. Afublez est d'un mantel sabelin, [être, afubler (revêtir)]
463. Ki fut cuvert d'une palie alexandrin. [être, couvrir]
464. Getet le a tere, sil receit Blancandrin; [jeter, recevoir]
465. Mais de s'espee ne volt mie guerpir; [vouloir, guerpir (abandonner)]
466. En sun puign destre par l'orie punt la tint. [tenir]
467. Dient paien: «Noble baron ad ci!» AOI. [dire, avoir]

**Translation**
451. All the noblest Saracens begged him,
452. For Marsile has seated himself on the throne.
453. The caliph said: «You have served us ill,
454. In that you dared to strike the Frenchman;
455. You ought to have listened to him and heard him out.»
456. - «Sire,» said Ganelon, «it falls to me to endure it;
457. I would not refrain, for all the gold that God made
458. Nor for all the wealth that may be in this land,
459. From telling him, if I have so much leisure,
460. What Charles commands him, the mighty king,
461. Commands him through me, his mortal enemy.»
462. He is wrapped in a sable mantle,
463. Which was covered with an Alexandrian silk.
464. He throws it to the ground, and Blancandrin catches it;
465. But he would by no means give up his sword;
466. In his right fist he held it by the golden pommel.
467. The pagans say: «Here is a noble baron!» AOI.

---

## Laisse XXXVI (lines 468–484)

**Original**
468. Envers le rei s'est Guenes aproismet, [s'approcher]
469. Si li ad dit: «A tort vos curuciez, [avoir, dire, courroucer]
470. Quar ço vos mandet Carles, ki France tient, [mander, tenir]
471. Que recevez la lei de chrestiens; [recevoir]
472. Demi Espaigne vus durat il en fiet. [donner]
473. L'altre meitet avrat Rollant, sis nies: [avoir]
474. Mulz orguillos parçuner i avrez! [avoir]
475. Si ceste acorde ne volez otrier, [vouloir, otreier (octroyer)]
476. En Sarraguce vus vendrat aseger; [venir, assiéger]
477. Par poestet serez pris e liez; [être, prendre, lier]
478. Menet serez . . . [tut] dreit ad Ais le siet: [mener, être]
479. Vus n'i avrez palefreid ne destrer, [avoir]
480. Ne mul ne mule que puissez chevalcher; [pouvoir, chevaucher]
481. Getet serez sur un malvais sumer. [jeter, être]
482. Par jugement iloec perdrez le chef. [perdre]
483. Nostre emperere vus enveiet cest bref.» [envoyer]
484. El destre poign al paien l'ad liv(e)ret. [avoir, livrer]

**Translation**
468. Toward the king Ganelon has drawn near,
469. And said to him: «Wrongly do you grow angry,
470. For thus Charles, who rules France, commands you:
471. That you receive the faith of Christians;
472. Half of Spain he will give you in fief.
473. The other half Roland, his nephew, will have:
474. A very proud co-sharer you will have there!
475. If you will not grant this accord,
476. He will come to besiege you in Saragossa;
477. By force you will be taken and bound;
478. You will be led straight to the seat of Aix:
479. You will have there neither palfrey nor warhorse,
480. Nor mule nor she-mule that you may ride;
481. You will be thrown upon a wretched pack-horse.
482. By judgment there you will lose your head.
483. Our emperor sends you this letter.»
484. Into the right fist of the pagan he handed it over.

---

## Laisse XXXVII (lines 485–500)

**Original**
485. Marsilies fut esculurez de l'ire; [être, escolorer (pâlir)]
486. Freint le seel, getet en ad la cire, [fraindre (briser), jeter, avoir]
487. Guardet al bref, vit la raisun escrite: [garder (regarder), voir, écrire]
488. «Carle me mandet, ki France ad en baillie, [mander, avoir, baillir (avoir en garde)]
489. Que me remembre de la dolur e (de) l'ire, [remembrer (se souvenir)]
490. Ço est de Basan e de sun frere Basilie, [être]
491. Dunt pris les chefs as puis de Haltoíe; [prendre]
492. Se de mun cors voeil aquiter la vie, [vouloir, aquiter (acquitter)]
493. Dunc li envei mun uncle, l'algalife; [envoyer]
494. Altrement ne m'amerat il mie.» [aimer]
495. Apres parlat ses filz envers Marsilies, [parler]
496. E dist al rei: «Guenes ad dit folie; [dire, avoir]
497. Tant ad erret nen est dreiz que plus vivet. [avoir, errer (agir), être, vivre]
498. Livrez le mei, jo en ferai la justise.» [livrer, faire]
499. Quant l'oït Guenes, l'espee en ad branlie; [oïr (entendre), avoir, brandir]
500. Vait s'apuier suz le pin a la tige. [aller, s'appuyer]

**Translation**
485. Marsile was pale with rage;
486. He breaks the seal, has cast away the wax,
487. He looks at the letter, saw the writing set down there:
488. «Charles, who has France in his keeping, commands me,
489. That I remember the grief and the wrath,
490. That is, of Basan and of his brother Basile,
491. Whose heads I took at the heights of Haltoie;
492. If I wish to ransom the life of my body,
493. Then let me send him my uncle, the caliph;
494. Otherwise he will not love me at all.»
495. After this his son spoke to Marsile,
496. And said to the king: «Ganelon has spoken folly;
497. He has gone so far that it is not right that he live longer.
498. Hand him over to me, I will do justice on him for it.»
499. When Ganelon heard this, he brandished his sword;
500. He goes to lean back against the trunk of the pine.

---

## Laisse XXXVIII (lines 501–511)

**Original**
501. Enz el verger s'en est alez li reis, [s'en aller]
502. Ses meillors humes enmeinet ensembl'od sei: [emmener]
503. E Blancandrins i vint, al canud peil, [venir]
504. E Jurfaret, ki est ses filz e ses heirs, [être]
505. E l'algalifes, sun uncle e sis fedeilz.
506. Dist Blancandrins: «Apelez le Franceis, [dire, appeler]
507. De nostre prod m'ad plevie sa feid.» [avoir, plevir (jurer)]
508. Ço dist li reis: «E vos l'i ameneiz.» [dire, amener]
509. E Guenes (l')ad pris par la main destre ad deiz, [avoir, prendre]
510. Enz el verger l'en meinet josqu'al rei. [mener]
511. La purparolent la traïson seinz dreit. AOI. [porparler (comploter)]

**Translation**
501. Into the orchard the king has gone,
502. He brings his best men along with him:
503. And Blancandrin came there, with the hoary hair,
504. And Jurfaret, who is his son and his heir,
505. And the caliph, his uncle and his vassal.
506. Said Blancandrin: «Call the Frenchman,
507. He has pledged me his faith concerning our advantage.»
508. Thus said the king: «And you, bring him here to me.»
509. And Ganelon took him by the right hand by the fingers,
510. Into the orchard he leads him as far as the king.
511. There they plot the treason without right. AOI.

---

## Laisse XXXIX (lines 512–519)

**Original**
512. «Bel sire Guenes,» ço li ad dit Marsilie, [avoir, dire]
513. «Jo vos ai fait alques de legerie, [avoir, faire]
514. Quant por ferir vus demustrai grant ire. [ferir (frapper), démontrer (montrer)]
515. Guaz vos en dreit par cez pels sabelines; [guagier (donner en gage)]
516. Melz en valt l'or que ne funt cinc cenz livres: [valoir, faire]
517. Einz demain noit en iert bele l'amendise.» [être]
518. Guenes respunt: «Jo nel desotrei mie. [répondre, desotreier (refuser)]
519. Deus se lui plaist, a bien le vos mercie!» AOI. [plaire, mercier]

**Translation**
512. «Fair sir Ganelon,» Marsile said to him,
513. «I have done you something of a wrong,
514. when I showed you great anger by making to strike you.
515. I pledge you amends by these sable furs;
516. their gold is worth more than five hundred pounds:
517. before tomorrow night the reparation will be fair.»
518. Ganelon answers: «I do not refuse it at all.
519. May God, if it please Him, reward you well for it!» AOI.

---

## Laisse XL (lines 520–536)

**Original**
520. Ço dist Marsilies: «Guenes par veir sacez, [dire, savoir]
521. En talant ai que mult vos voeill amer, [avoir, vouloir, aimer]
522. De Carlemagne vos voeill oïr parler. [vouloir, oïr (entendre), parler]
523. Il est mult vielz, si ad sun tens uset; [être, avoir, user]
524. Men escient dous cenz anz ad passet. [avoir, passer]
525. Par tantes teres ad sun cors demened, [avoir, demener (conduire)]
526. Tanz [colps] ad pris sur sun escut bucler, [avoir, prendre]
527. Tanz riches reis cunduit a mendisted: [conduire]
528. Quant ert il mais recreanz d'osteier?» [être, osteier (guerroyer)]
529. Guenes respunt: «Carles n'est mie tels. [répondre, être]
530. N'est hom kil veit e conuistre le set [être, voir, connaître, savoir]
531. Que ço ne diet que l'emperere est ber. [dire, être]
532. Tant nel vos sai ne preiser ne loer [savoir, priser, loer (conseiller)]
533. Que plus n'i ad d'onur e de bontet. [avoir]
534. Sa grant valor, kil purreit acunter? [pouvoir, aconter (raconter)]
535. De tel barnage l'ad Deus enluminet, [avoir, enluminer (illuminer)]
536. Meilz voelt murir que guerpir sun barnet.» [vouloir, mourir, guerpir (abandonner)]

**Translation**
520. Marsile said this: «Ganelon, know in truth,
521. I have the desire that I much want to love you,
522. I want to hear you speak of Charlemagne.
523. He is very old, and has worn out his time;
524. To my knowledge he has passed two hundred years.
525. Through so many lands he has driven his body,
526. So many blows has he taken upon his buckler-shield,
527. So many rich kings has he led to beggary:
528. When will he ever be weary of waging war?»
529. Ganelon replies: «Charles is not at all such a man.
530. There is no man who sees him and comes to know him
531. Who would not say that the emperor is noble.
532. I cannot so praise him to you nor extol him
533. That there is not yet more of honor and goodness in him.
534. His great valor, who could recount it?
535. With such nobility has God illumined him,
536. He would rather die than abandon his noble company.»

---

## Laisse XLI (lines 537–549)

**Original**
537. Dist li paiens: «Mult me puis merveiller [dire, pouvoir, se merveiller (s'étonner)]
538. De Carlemagne, ki est canuz e vielz! [être]
539. Men escientre dous cenz anz ad e mielz. [avoir]
540. Par tantes teres ad sun cors traveillet, [avoir, travailler]
541. Tanz colz ad pris de lances e d'espiet, [avoir, prendre]
542. Tanz riches reis cunduiz a mendistiet: [avoir, conduire]
543. Quant ert il mais recreanz d'osteier?» [être, osteier (guerroyer)]
544. - «Ço n'iert,» dist Guenes: «tant cum vivet sis niés: [être, dire, vivre]
545. N'at tel vassal suz la cape del ciel. [avoir]
546. Mult par est proz sis cumpainz Oliver. [être]
547. Les .XII. pers, que Carles ad tant chers, [avoir]
548. Funt les enguardes a .XX. milie chevalers. [faire]
549. Soürs est Carles, que nuls home ne crent.» AOI. [être, craindre]

**Translation**
537. The pagan said: «Greatly may I marvel
538. at Charlemagne, who is white-haired and old!
539. To my knowledge he is two hundred years old and more.
540. Through so many lands he has worn out his body,
541. so many blows he has taken from lances and spears,
542. so many rich kings he has brought to beggary:
543. when will he ever be weary of waging war?»
544. - «That will not be,» said Ganelon, «as long as his nephew lives:
545. there is no such vassal under the cope of heaven.
546. His companion Oliver is very valiant indeed.
547. The Twelve Peers, whom Charles holds so dear,
548. form the vanguards with twenty thousand knights.
549. Charles is secure, for he fears no man.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XLII (lines 550–562)

**Original**
550. Dist li Sarrazins: «Merveille en ai grant [dire, avoir]
551. De Carlemagne, ki est canuz e blancs! [être]
552. Mien escientre plus ad de .II.C. anz. [avoir]
553. Par tantes teres est alet cunquerant, [être, aller, conquérir]
554. Tanz colps ad pris de bons espiez trenchanz, [avoir, prendre, trancher]
555. Tanz riches reis morz e vencuz en champ: [mourir, vaincre]
556. Quant ier il mais d'osteier recreant?» [être, osteier (guerroyer)]
557. - «Ço n'iert,» dist Guenes, «tant cum vivet Rollant: [être, dire, vivre]
558. N'ad tel vassal d'ici qu en Orient. [avoir]
559. Mult par est proz Oliver, sis cumpainz; [être]
560. Li .XII. per, que Carles aimet tant, [aimer]
561. Funt les enguardes a .XX. milie de Francs, [faire]
562. Soürs est Carlles, ne (cre) crent hume vivant.» AOI. [être, craindre, vivre]

**Translation**
550. Said the Saracen: «Great is the wonder I feel
551. About Charlemagne, who is hoary and white!
552. By my reckoning he is more than two hundred years old.
553. Through so many lands has he gone conquering,
554. So many blows has he taken from good sharp spears,
555. So many rich kings dead and vanquished in the field:
556. When will he ever weary of waging war?»
557. - «That will not be,» said Ganelon, «so long as Roland lives:
558. There is no such vassal from here to the Orient.
559. Very valiant is Oliver, his companion;
560. The Twelve Peers, whom Charles loves so much,
561. Form the vanguard with twenty thousand Franks,
562. Charles is secure, he fears no living man.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XLIII (lines 563–579)

**Original**
563. - «Bel sire Guenes» dist marsilies li reis, [dire]
564. «Jo ai tel gent, plus bele ne verreiz; [avoir, voir]
565. Quarte cenz milie chevalers puis aveir. [pouvoir, avoir]
566. Puis m'en cumbatre a Carlles e a Franceis?» [pouvoir, combattre]
567. Guenes respunt: «Ne vus a ceste feiz! [répondre]
568. De voz paiens mult grant perte i avreiz. [avoir]
569. Lessez (la) folie, tenez vos al saveir. [laisser, tenir, savoir]
570. L'empereür tant li dunez aveir, [donner, avoir]
571. N'i ait Franceis ki tot ne s'en merveilt. [avoir, se merveiller (s'étonner)]
572. Par .XX. hostages que li enveiereiz [envoyer]
573. En dulce France s'en repairerat li reis; [repairer (rentrer)]
574. Sa rereguarde lerrat derere sei: [laisser]
575. Iert i sis nies, li quens Rollant, (...) ço crei, [être, croire]
576. E Oliver, li proz e li curteis.
577. Mort sunt li cunte, se est ki mei en creit. [mourir, être, croire]
578. Carlles verrat sun grant orguill cadeir; [voir, cheoir (tomber)]
579. N'avrat talent, que ja mais vus guerreit.» AOI. [avoir, guerroyer]

**Translation**
563. - «Fair lord Ganelon,» said Marsile the king,
564. «I have such troops, you will never see finer;
565. Four hundred thousand knights I can have.
566. Can I then fight against Charles and the Franks?»
567. Ganelon answers: «Not this time!
568. Of your pagans you would suffer there very great loss.
569. Leave aside folly, hold to wisdom.
570. Give the emperor so much wealth,
571. That there will be no Frank who does not marvel at it.
572. By twenty hostages that you will send him
573. The king will return to sweet France;
574. His rearguard he will leave behind him:
575. There will be his nephew, Count Roland, (...) so I believe,
576. And Oliver, the valiant and the courteous.
577. The counts are dead, if there is anyone who believes me in this.
578. Charles will see his great pride fall;
579. He will have no desire ever again to make war on you.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XLIV (lines 580–595)

**Original**
580. - «Bel sire Guenes,[» ço dist li reis Marsilies,] [dire]
581. «Cum faitement purrai Rollant ocire?» [pouvoir, ocire (tuer)]
582. Guenes respont: «Ço vos sai jo ben dire. [répondre, savoir, dire]
583. Li reis serat as meillors porz de Sizer; [être]
584. Sa rereguarde avrat detres sei mise; [avoir, mettre]
585. Iert i sis nies, li quens Rollant, li riches, [être]
586. E Oliver, en qui il tant se fiet; [se fier]
587. .XX. milie Francs unt en lur cumpaignie. [avoir]
588. De voz paiens lur enveiez .C. milie: [envoyer]
589. Une bataille lur i rendent cil primes; [rendre]
590. La gent de France iert blecee e blesmie; [être, blesser, blesmir (meurtrir)]
591. Nel di por ço, des voz iert la martirie. [dire, être]
592. Altre bataille lur livrez de meïsme: [livrer]
593. De quel que seit Rollant n'estuertrat mie. [être, estordre (échapper)]
594. Dunc avrez faite gente chevalerie; [avoir, faire]
595. N'avrez mais guere en tute vostre vie.» AOI. [avoir]

**Translation**
580. - «Fair sir Ganelon,» said King Marsile to him,
581. «How might I be able to kill Roland?»
582. Ganelon answers: «That I can well tell you.
583. The king will be at the great passes of Cize;
584. He will have placed his rearguard behind him;
585. There will be his nephew, Count Roland, the mighty one,
586. And Oliver, in whom he so trusts;
587. Twenty thousand Franks they have in their company.
588. Of your pagans send them a hundred thousand:
589. Let these give them a first battle there;
590. The host of France will be wounded and bruised;
591. I do not say it for that, the slaughter will be of yours.
592. Give them another battle likewise:
593. From one or the other Roland will not escape.
594. Then you will have done a noble feat of arms;
595. You will have no more war in all your life.» AOI.

---

## Laisse XLV (lines 596–602)

**Original**
596. - «Chi purreit faire, que Rollant i fust mort, [pouvoir, faire, être, mourir]
597. Dunc perdreit Carles le destre braz del cors, [perdre]
598. Si remeindreient les merveilluses óz; [remaindre (rester)]
599. N'asemblereit jamais Carles si grant esforz; [assembler]
600. Tere Major remeindreit en repos.» [remaindre (rester)]
601. Quan l'ot Marsilie, si l'ad baiset el col, [oïr (entendre), avoir, baiser]
602. Puis si cumencet a venir ses tresors. AOI. [commencer, venir] <!-- uncertain: "venir ses tresors" idiom = faire venir/apporter -->

**Translation**
596. - «Whoever could bring it about that Roland be killed there,
597. Then Charles would lose the right arm of his body,
598. And the marvelous armies would be left behind;
599. Charles would never again assemble so great a host;
600. The Great Land would remain in peace.»
601. When Marsile heard this, he kissed him on the neck,
602. Then he begins to bring forth his treasures. AOI.

---

## Laisse XLVI (lines 603–608)

**Original**
603. Ço dist Marsilies: «Qu'en parlereient il plus? [dire, parler]
604. Cunseill n'est proz dunt hume n'est sevus. [être, savoir]
605. La traïsun me jurrez de Rollant si illi est.» [jurer, être]
606. Ço respunt Guenes: «Issi seit cum vos plaist!» [répondre, être, plaire]
607. Sur les reliques de s'espee Murgleis,
608. La traïsun jurat, e si s'en est forsfait. AOI. [jurer, être, forsfaire (se rendre coupable)]

**Translation**
603. Marsile said: «Why should they speak further about it?
604. Counsel is not worthwhile of which no man can be sure.
605. You will swear to me the betrayal of Roland, if it is so.»
606. Ganelon answered: «So be it, as it pleases you!»
607. Upon the relics of his sword Murgleis,
608. He swore the betrayal, and thus made himself guilty of it. AOI.

---

## Laisse XLVII (lines 609–616)

**Original**
609. Un faldestoed i out d'un olifant; [avoir]
610. Marsilies fait porter un livre avant: [faire, porter]
611. La lei i fut Mahum e Tervagan. [être]
612. Ço ad juret li Sarrazins espans: [avoir, jurer]
613. Se en rereguarde troevet le cors Rollant, [trouver]
614. Cumbatrat sei a trestute sa gent, [combattre]
615. E, se il poet, murrat i veirement. [pouvoir, mourir]
616. Guenes respunt: «Ben seit vostre comant!» AOI. [répondre, être]

**Translation**
609. There was a throne there made of ivory;
610. Marsile has a book brought forward:
611. In it was the law of Mahomet and Tervagant.
612. The Spanish Saracen has sworn this:
613. If in the rearguard he finds Roland's person,
614. He will fight him with all his people,
615. And, if he can, he will truly die there.
616. Ganelon answers: «May your command be a good one!» AOI.

---

## Laisse XLVIII (lines 617–626)

**Original**
617. A tant i vint uns paiens, Valdabruns: [venir]
618. Icil en vait al rei Marsiliun; [aller]
619. Cler en riant l'ad dit a Guenelun: [rire, avoir, dire]
620. «Tenez m'espee, meillur n'en at nuls hom; [tenir, avoir]
621. Entre les helz ad plus de mil manguns. [avoir]
622. Par amistiez, bel sire, la vos duins, [donner]
623. Que (v)[n]os aidez de Rollant le barun, [aider]
624. Qu'en rereguarde trover le poüsum.» [trouver, pouvoir]
625. - «Ben serat fait,» li quens Guenes respunt. [être, faire, répondre]
626. Puis se baiserent es vis e es mentuns. [se baiser]

**Translation**
617. Thereupon came a pagan, Valdabrun:
618. He goes to the king Marsile;
619. Brightly, laughing, he said to Ganelon:
620. «Take my sword, no man has a better one;
621. Between the hilts there are more than a thousand mangons.
622. In friendship, fair lord, I give it to you,
623. That you help us with Roland the baron,
624. That we may find him in the rearguard.»
625. - «It will be well done,» answers Count Ganelon.
626. Then they kissed each other on the faces and on the chins.

---

## Laisse XLIX (lines 627–633)

**Original**
627. Apres i vint un paien, Climorins. [venir]
628. Cler en riant a Guenelun l'ad dit: [rire, avoir, dire]
629. «Tenez mun helme, unches meillor ne vi. [tenir, voir]
630. Si nos aidez de Rollant li marchis, [aider]
631. Par quel mesure le poüssum hunir.» [pouvoir, honnir (déshonorer)]
632. - «Ben serat fait,» Guenes respundit. [être, faire, répondre]
633. Puis se baiserent es buches e es vis. AOI. [se baiser]

**Translation**
627. After him came a pagan, Climorins.
628. Brightly, laughing, he said to Ganelon:
629. «Take my helmet, never did I see a better one.
630. Thus help us regarding Roland the marquis,
631. As to by what means we might dishonor him.»
632. - «It will be well done,» Ganelon answered.
633. Then they kissed each other on the mouths and on the faces. AOI.

---

## Laisse L (lines 634–641)

**Original**
634. A tant i vint la reine Bramimunde. [venir]
635. «Jo vos aim mult, sire,» dist ele al cunte, [aimer, dire]
636. «Car mult vos priset mi sire e tuit si hume. [priser (estimer)]
637. A vostre femme enveierai dous nusches; [envoyer]
638. Bien i ad or, matices e jacunces: [avoir]
639. Eles valent mielz que tut l'aveir de Rume, [valoir]
640. Vostre emperere si bones n'en out unches.» [avoir]
641. Il les ad prises, en sa hoese les butet, AOI. [avoir, prendre, bouter (mettre)]

**Translation**
634. Then came the queen Bramimonde.
635. «I love you greatly, sire,» she said to the count,
636. «For my lord esteems you greatly, and all his men.
637. To your wife I will send two necklaces;
638. There is much gold in them, amethysts and jacinths:
639. They are worth more than all the wealth of Rome,
640. Your emperor never had any so fine.»
641. He took them, into his boot he stuffed them, AOI.

---

## Laisse LI (lines 642–646)

**Original**
642. Li reis apelet Malduit sun tresorer: [appeler]
643. «L'aveir Carlun est il apareillez?» [être, appareiller (préparer)]
644. E cil respunt: «Oïl, sire, asez bien: [répondre]
645. .VII.C. cameilz, d'or e argent cargiez, [charger]
646. E .XX. hostages, des plus gentilz desuz cel.» AOI.

**Translation**
642. The king calls Malduit, his treasurer:
643. «Is Charles's tribute made ready?»
644. And he replies: «Yes, lord, very amply:
645. seven hundred camels, laden with gold and silver,
646. and twenty hostages, of the noblest under heaven.» AOI.

---

## Laisse LII (lines 647–660)

**Original**
647. Marsilies tint Guen[elun] par l'espalle; [tenir]
648. Si li ad dit: «Mult par ies ber e sage. [avoir, dire, être]
649. Par cele lei que vos tenez plus salve, [tenir]
650. Guardez de nos ne turnez le curage. [garder, tourner]
651. De mun aveir vos voeill dunner grant masse: [vouloir, donner]
652. .X. muls cargez del plus fin or d'Arabe; [charger]
653. Jamais n'iert an, altretel ne vos face. [être, faire]
654. Tenez les clefs de ceste citet large, [tenir]
655. Le grant aveir en presentez al rei Carles, [présenter]
656. Pois me jugez Rollant a rereguarde. [juger]
657. Sel pois trover a port ne a passage, [pouvoir, trouver]
658. Liverrai lui une mortel bataille.» [livrer]
659. Guenes respunt: «Mei est vis que trop targe!» [répondre, être, targier (tarder)]
660. Pois est munted, entret en sun veiage. AOI. [être, monter, entrer]

**Translation**
647. Marsile took Ganelon by the shoulder;
648. And said to him: «You are most valiant and wise.
649. By the law that you hold most sacred,
650. Take care that you do not turn your heart away from us.
651. Of my wealth I will give you a great quantity:
652. Ten mules laden with the finest gold of Arabia;
