1340. Des Sarrazins lur fait mult grant damage. [faire]
1341. Ki lui veïst l'un geter mort su[r] l'altre, [voir, jeter]
1342. Li sanc tuz clers gesir par cele place! [gésir (être couché)]
1343. Sanglant en ad e l'osberc e [la] brace, [avoir]
1344. Sun bon cheval le col e les [es]palles.
1345. E Oliver de ferir ne se target, [ferir (frapper), targier (tarder)]
1346. Li .XII. per n'en deivent aveir blasme, [devoir, avoir]
1347. E li Franceis i fierent e si caplent. [ferir (frapper), capler (frapper)]
1348. Moerent paien e alquanz en i pasment. [mourir, se pâmer]
1349. Dist l'arcevesque: «Ben ait nostre barnage!» [dire, avoir]
1350. - «Munjoie!» escriet, ço est l'enseigne Carle. AOI. [escrier (s'écrier), être]

**Translation**
1338. Count Roland rides through the middle of the field,
1339. Holds Durendal, which cuts and slashes well,
1340. Does the Saracens very great harm.
1341. Whoever had seen him throw one dead upon another,
1342. The bright blood lying all over that place!
1343. He has blood on both his hauberk and his arm,
1344. His good horse on the neck and the shoulders.
1345. And Oliver does not delay in striking,
1346. The Twelve Peers ought to have no blame for it,
1347. And the Franks strike and hew there.
1348. The pagans die and several of them faint there.
1349. Said the archbishop: «Blessed be our baronage!»
1350. - «Munjoie!» he cries, that is Charles's battle-cry. AOI.

---

## Laisse CVI (lines 1351–1366)

**Original**
1351. E Oliver chevalchet par l'estor, [chevaucher]
1352. Sa hanste est frait, n'en ad que un trunçun, [être, avoir]
1353. E vait fer(en)[ir] un paien, Malun: [aller, ferir (frapper)]
1354. L'escut li freint, ki est ad or e a flur, [fraindre (briser), être]
1355. Fors de la teste li met les oilz ansdous, [mettre]
1356. E la cervele li chet as piez desuz; [cheoir (tomber)]
1357. Mort le tresturnet od tut .VII.C. des lur. [tresturner (renverser)]
1358. Pois ad ocis Turgis e Esturguz; [avoir, ocire (tuer)]
1359. La hanste briset e esclicet josqu'as poinz. [briser, esclicier (voler en éclats)]
1360. Ço dist Rollant: «Cumpainz, que faites vos? [dire, faire]
1361. En tel bataille n'ai cure de bastun; [avoir]
1362. Fers e acers i deit aveir valor. [devoir, avoir, valoir]
1363. U est vostre espee, ki Halteclere ad num? [être, avoir]
1364. D'or est li helz e de cristal li punz.» [être]
1365. - «Ne la poi traire,» Oliver li respunt, [pouvoir, traire (tirer), répondre]
1366. «Kar de ferir oi jo si grant bosoign.» AOI. [ferir (frapper), avoir]

**Translation**
1351. And Oliver rides through the fray,
1352. His lance is broken, he has but one stump of it,
1353. And he goes to strike a pagan, Malun:
1354. He shatters his shield, which is adorned with gold and flowers,
1355. From his head he drives out both his eyes,
1356. And his brains fall down at his feet;
1357. He casts him down dead, along with seven hundred of their men.
1358. Then he has slain Turgis and Esturguz;
1359. His lance breaks and splinters all the way to his fists.
1360. Roland says this: «Comrade, what are you doing?
1361. In such a battle I have no care for a stick;
1362. Iron and steel ought to be of worth here.
1363. Where is your sword, which is named Halteclere?
1364. The hilt is of gold and the pommel of crystal.»
1365. - «I could not draw it,» Oliver answers him,
1366. «For I had such great need to strike.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CVII (lines 1367–1378)

**Original**
1367. Danz Oliver trait ad sa bone espee, [traire (tirer), avoir]
1368. Que ses cumpainz Rollant li ad tant demandee, [avoir, demander]
1369. E il li ad cum chevaler mustree. [avoir, montrer]
1370. Fiert un paien, Justin de Val Ferree: [ferir (frapper)]
1371. Tute la teste li ad par mi sevree, [avoir, sevrer (séparer)]
1372. Trenchet le cors e [la] bronie safree, [trancher]
1373. La bone sele, ki a ór est gemmee, [être, gemmer]
1374. E al ceval a l'eschine trenchee; [trancher]
1375. Tut abat mort devant loi en la pree. [abattre]
1376. Ço dist Rollant: «Vos receif jo, frere! [dire, recevoir]
1377. Por itels colps nos eimet li emperere.» [aimer]
1378. De tutes parz est «Munjo[i]e» escriee. AOI. [être, escrier (s'écrier)]

**Translation**
1367. Lord Oliver has drawn his good sword,
1368. Which his companion Roland had so much asked of him,
1369. And he has shown it to him in knightly fashion.
1370. He strikes a pagan, Justin of Val Ferrée:
1371. He has cut his whole head clean in two,
1372. Cleaves the body and the gilded mail-coat,
1373. The good saddle, which is jeweled with gold,
1374. And cut through the horse's spine;
1375. He fells him dead before himself on the meadow.
1376. Roland said: «I accept you, brother!
1377. For such blows the emperor loves us.»
1378. On all sides «Munjoie» is cried. AOI.

---

## Laisse CVIII (lines 1379–1395)

**Original**
1379. Li quens Gerins set el ceval Sorel [seoir (être assis)]
1380. E sis cumpainz Gerers en Passecerf,
1381. Laschent lor reisnes, brochent amdui a ait, [lâcher, brocher (éperonner)]
1382. E vunt ferir un paien, Timozel, [aller, ferir (frapper)]
1383. L'un en l'escut e li altre en l'osberc,
1384. Lur dous espiez enz el cors li unt frait, [avoir, fraindre (briser)]
1385. Mort le tresturnent tres enmi un guaret, [tresturner (renverser)]
1386. Ne l'oï dire ne jo mie nel sai [oïr (entendre), dire, savoir]
1387. Liquels d'els dous en fut li plus isnels. [être]
1388. Esprieres icil fut filz Burdel, [être]
1389. [lacuna — line lost in Oxford ms]
1390. E l'arcevesque lor ocist Siglorel, [ocire (tuer)]
1391. L'encanteür ki ja fut en enfer: [être]
1392. Par artimal l'i cundoist Jupiter. [conduire]
1393. Ço dist Turpin: «Icist nos ert forsfait.» [dire, forsfaire (faire tort)]
1394. Respunt Rollant: «Vencut est le culvert. [répondre, vaincre, être]
1395. Oliver, frere, itels colps me sunt bel!» [être]

**Translation**
1379. Count Gerin sits on his horse Sorel
1380. And his companion Gerer on Passecerf,
1381. They slacken their reins, both spur eagerly,
1382. And go to strike a pagan, Timozel,
1383. The one on the shield and the other on the hauberk,
1384. Their two spears they have shattered in his body,
1385. Dead they tumble him down in the middle of a fallow field.
1386. I never heard it told, nor do I know at all
1387. Which of the two was the swifter.
1388. Esprieris, this one was the son of Burdel,
1389. [lacuna]
1390. And the archbishop slew Siglorel for them,
1391. The enchanter who had already been in hell:
1392. By magic Jupiter led him there.
1393. Turpin says: «This one has done us wrong.»
1394. Roland answers: «The villain is vanquished.
1395. Oliver, brother, such blows please me!»

---

## Laisse CIX (lines 1396–1411)

**Original**
1396. La bataille est aduree endementres. [être]
1397. Franc e paien merveilus colps i rendent. [rendre]
1398. Fierent li un, li altre se defendent. [ferir (frapper), défendre]
1399. Tant hanste i ad e fraite e sanglente, [avoir, fraindre (briser)]
1400. Tant gunfanun rumpu e tant enseigne! [rompre]
1401. Tant bon Franceis i perdent lor juvente! [perdre]
1402. Ne reverrunt lor meres ne lor femmes, [revoir]
1403. Ne cels de France ki as porz les atendent. AOI. [attendre]
1404. Karles li magnes en pluret, si se demente. [pleurer, se démenter (se lamenter)]
1405. De ço qui calt? N'en avrunt sucurance. [chaloir (importer), avoir]
1406. Malvais servis[e] le jur li rendit Guenes, [rendre]
1407. Qu'en Sarraguce sa maisnee alat vendre; [aller, vendre]
1408. Puis en perdit e sa vie e ses membres; [perdre]
1409. El plait ad Ais en fut juget a prendre, [être, juger, prendre]
1410. De ses parenz ensembl'od lui tels trente
1411. Ki de murir nen ourent esperance. AOI. [mourir, avoir]

**Translation**
1396. The battle is hard-fought meanwhile.
1397. Frank and pagan exchange marvelous blows.
1398. The one side strikes, the others defend themselves.
1399. So many a lance there is, both shattered and bloody,
1400. So many a gonfalon torn and so many a standard!
1401. So many good Franks there lose their youth!
1402. They will not see again their mothers nor their wives,
1403. Nor those of France who await them at the passes. AOI.
1404. Charles the Great weeps for it, and laments.
1405. What does it matter? They will have no help from it.
1406. An evil service Ganelon rendered him that day,
1407. When he went to Saragossa to sell his household;
1408. Afterward he lost for it both his life and his limbs;
1409. At the trial at Aix he was condemned to be seized,
1410. Of his kinsmen along with him fully thirty such
1411. Who had no hope of escaping death. AOI.

---

## Laisse CX (lines 1412–1437)

**Original**
1412. La bataille est merveilluse e pesant; [être]
1413. Mult ben i fiert Oliver e Rollant, [ferir (frapper)]
1414. Li arcevesques plus de mil colps i rent, [rendre]
1415. Li .XII. per ne s'en targent nient, [targier (tarder)]
1416. E li Franceis i fierent cumunement. [ferir (frapper)]
1417. Moerent paien a miller(e)[s] e a cent; [mourir]
1418. Ki ne s'en fuit de mort n'i ad guarent; [s'enfuir, avoir]
1419. Voillet o nun, tut i laisset sun tens. [vouloir, laisser]
1420. Franceis i perdent lor meillors guarnemenz; [perdre]
1421. Ne reverrunt lor peres ne lor parenz [revoir]
1422. Ne Carlemagne, ki as porz les atent. [attendre]
1423. En France en ad mult merveillus turment; [avoir]
1424. Orez i ad de tuneire e de vent, [avoir]
1425. Pluies e gresilz desmesureement;
1426. Chiedent i fuildres e menut e suvent, [cheoir (tomber)]
1427. E terremoete ço i ad veirement. [avoir]
1428. De seint Michel de(l) P(aris)[eril] josqu'as Seinz
1429. Des Besençun tresqu'as [port] de Guitsand
1430. N'en ad recet dunt del mur ne cravent. [avoir, cravanter (s'écrouler)]
1431. Cuntre midi tenebres i ad granz; [avoir]
1432. N'i ad clartet, se li ciels nen i fent. [avoir, fendre]
1433. Hume nel veit,ki mult ne s espaent. [voir, espaenter (s'épouvanter)]
1434. Dient plusor: «Ço est li definement, [dire, être]
1435. La fin del secle ki nus est en present.» [être]
1436. Il nel sevent, ne dient veir nient: [savoir, dire]
1437. Ço est li granz dulors por la mort de Rollant. [être]

**Translation**
1412. The battle is marvelous and hard;
1413. Oliver and Roland strike well there,
1414. The archbishop deals more than a thousand blows,
1415. The Twelve Peers do not hold back at all,
1416. And the French strike there all together.
1417. Pagans die there by thousands and by hundreds;
1418. Whoever does not flee has no protection from death there;
1419. Willing or not, each leaves his life there.
1420. The French lose there their best warriors;
1421. They will not see again their fathers nor their kinsmen,
1422. Nor Charlemagne, who awaits them at the passes.
1423. In France there is a most marvelous storm;
1424. There is a tempest of thunder and of wind,
1425. Rains and hail beyond measure;
1426. Thunderbolts fall there, both small and frequent,
1427. And there is an earthquake there in truth.
1428. From Saint Michael of the Peril as far as the Saints,
1429. From Besançon up to the port of Wissant,
1430. There is no dwelling whose wall does not crumble.
1431. Around midday there are great shadows there;
1432. There is no light, unless the sky splits open.
1433. No man sees it who is not greatly terrified.
1434. Many say: «This is the ending,
1435. The end of the world that is upon us.»
1436. They do not know it, they speak no truth at all:
1437. This is the great mourning for the death of Roland.

---

## Laisse CXI (lines 1438–1448)

**Original**
1438. Franceis i unt ferut de coer e de vigur; [avoir, ferir (frapper)]
1439. Paien sunt morz a millers e a fuls: [être, mourir]
1440. De cent millers n'en poent guarir dous. [pouvoir, garir (réchapper)]
1441. Rollant dist: «Nostre hume sunt mult proz: [dire, être]
1442. Suz ciel n'ad home plus en ait de meillors.» [avoir]
1443. Il est escrit en la Geste Francor [être, écrire]
1444. Que vassals ad li nostre empereür. [avoir]
1445. Vunt par le camp, si requerent les lor, [aller, requérir]
1446. Plurent des oilz de doel e de tendrur [pleurer]
1447. Por lor parenz par coer e par amor.
1448. Li reis Marsilie od sa grant ost lor surt. AOI. [sourdre (surgir)]

**Translation**
1438. The French have struck there with heart and with vigor;
1439. The pagans are dead by the thousands and in droves:
1440. Of a hundred thousand not two of them can escape.
1441. Roland said: «Our men are very valiant:
1442. Under heaven there is no one who has more of the best.»
1443. It is written in the Geste of the Franks
1444. That valiant men our emperor has.
1445. They go through the field, and they seek their own,
1446. They weep from their eyes out of grief and tenderness
1447. For their kinsmen out of heart and out of love.
1448. King Marsile with his great host surges upon them. AOI.

---

## Laisse CXII (lines 1449–1466)

**Original**
1449. Marsilie vient par mi une valee [venir]
1450. Od sa grant ost que il out asemblee. [avoir, assembler]
1451. .XX. escheles ad li reis anumbrees. [avoir, anombrer (dénombrer)]
1452. Lacent cil'elme as perres d'or gemmees, [lacer]
1453. E cil escuz e cez bronies sasfrees;
1454. .VII. milie graisles i sunent la menee: [sonner]
1455. Grant est la noise par tute la contree. [être]
1456. Ço dist Rollant: «Oliver, compaign, frere, [dire]
1457. Guenes li fels ad nostre mort juree; [avoir, jurer]
1458. La traïsun ne poet estre celee; [pouvoir, être, celer]
1459. Mult grant venjance en prendrat l'emperere. [prendre]
1460. Bataille avrum e forte [e] aduree, [avoir, adurer (endurcir)]
1461. Unches mais hom tel ne vit ajustee. [voir, ajuster]
1462. Jo i ferrai de Durendal, m'espee, [ferir (frapper)]
1463. E vos, compainz, ferrez de Halteclere. [ferir (frapper)]
1464. En tanz lius les avum nos portees! [avoir, porter]
1465. Tantes batailles en avum afinees! [avoir, afiner (achever)]
1466. Male chançun n'en deit estre cantee.» AOI. [devoir, être, chanter]

**Translation**
1449. Marsile comes through the middle of a valley
1450. With his great host that he had assembled.
1451. The king has counted twenty battalions.
1452. They lace on those helmets gemmed with gold,
1453. And those shields and those saffron-dyed hauberks;
1454. Seven thousand trumpets sound the charge there:
1455. Great is the din throughout the whole country.
1456. Roland said this: «Oliver, companion, brother,
1457. Ganelon the traitor has sworn our death;
1458. The treason cannot be concealed;
1459. The emperor will take very great vengeance for it.
1460. We shall have a battle, hard and grueling,
1461. Never did any man see such a one joined.
1462. I shall strike there with Durendal, my sword,
1463. And you, companion, will strike with Hauteclaire.
1464. In so many places have we borne them!
1465. So many battles have we ended with them!
1466. Let no evil song be sung about it.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXIII (lines 1467–1486)

**Original**
1467. Marsilies veit de sa gent le martirie, [voir]
1468. Si fait suner ses cors e ses buisines, [faire, sonner]
1469. Puis si chevalchet od sa grant ost banie. [chevaucher]
1470. Devant chevalchet un Sarrasin, Abisme: [chevaucher]
1471. Plus fel de lui n'out en sa cumpagnie. [avoir]
1472. Te(t)ches ad males e mult granz felonies; [avoir]
1473. Ne creit en Deu, le filz sainte Marie; [croire]
1474. Issi est neirs cume peiz ki est demise; [être]
1475. Plus aimet il traïsun e murdrie [aimer]
1476. Qu'(e) il ne fesist trestut l'or de Galice; [faire]
1477. Unches nuls hom nel vit juer ne rire. [voir, jouer, rire]
1478. Vasselage ad e mult grant estultie: [avoir]
1479. Por ço est drud al felun rei Marsilie; [être]
1480. Sun dragun portet a qui sa gent s'alient. [porter, s'allier]
1481. Li arcevesque ne l'amerat ja mie; [aimer]
1482. Cum il le vit, a ferir le desiret. [voir, ferir (frapper), désirer]
1483. Mult quiement le dit a sei meïsme: [dire]
1484. «Cel Sarraz[in] me semblet mult herite: [sembler]
1485. Mielz est mult que jo l'alge ocire. [être, aller, ocire (tuer)]
1486. Unches n'amai cuard ne cuardie.» AOI. [aimer]

**Translation**
1467. Marsile sees the slaughter of his people,
1468. So he has his horns and his trumpets sounded,
1469. Then he rides forth with his great mustered host.
1470. Out in front rides a Saracen, Abisme:
1471. There was none more wicked than he in his company.
1472. He has evil traits and very great villainies;
1473. He does not believe in God, the son of holy Mary;
1474. He is as black as melted pitch;
1475. He loves treachery and murder more
1476. Than he would for all the gold of Galicia;
1477. Never did any man see him play or laugh.
1478. He has valor and very great rashness:
1479. For this he is a favorite of the felon king Marsile;
1480. He carries his dragon-standard, around which his people rally.
1481. The archbishop will never love him at all;
1482. As soon as he saw him, he longed to strike him.
1483. Very quietly he says to himself:
1484. «This Saracen seems to me very much a heretic:
1485. It is far better that I go and slay him.
1486. Never did I love a coward or cowardice.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXIV (lines 1487–1509)

**Original**
1487. Li arcevesque cumencet la bataille. [commencer]
1488. Siet el cheval qu'il tolit a Grossaille, [seoir (être assis), tolir (enlever)]
1489. Ço ert uns reis qu'l ocist en Denemarche. [être, ocire (tuer)]
1490. Li destrers est e curanz e aates, [être]
1491. Piez ad copiez e les gambes ad plates, [avoir]
1492. Curte la quisse e la crupe bien large,
1493. Lungs les costez e l'eschine ad ben halte, [avoir]
1494. Blanche la cue e la crignete jalne
1495. Petites les oreilles, la teste tute falve;
1496. Beste nen est nule ki encontre lui alge. [être, aller]
1497. Li arcevesque brochet par tant grant vasselage: [brocher (éperonner)]
1498. Ne laisserat qu'Abisme nen asaillet; [laisser, assaillir]
1499. Vait le ferir en l'escut amiracle: [aller, ferir (frapper)]
1500. Pierres i ad, ametistes e topazes, [avoir]
1501. Esterminals e carbuncles ki ardent; [ardre (brûler)]
1502. En Val Metas li dunat uns diables, [donner]
1503. Si li tramist li amiralz Galafes. [tramettre (transmettre)]
1504. Turpins i fiert, ki nient ne l'esparignet, [ferir (frapper), épargner]
1505. Enpres sun colp ne quid que un dener vaillet, [cuidier (croire), valoir]
1506. Le cors li trenchet tres l'un costet qu'a l'altre, [trancher]
1507. Que mort l'abat en une voide place. [abattre]
1508. Dient Franceis: «Ci ad grant vasselage! [dire, avoir]
1509. En l'arcevesque est ben la croce salve.» [être]

**Translation**
1487. The archbishop begins the battle.
1488. He sits on the horse that he took from Grossaille,
1489. He was a king whom he slew in Denmark.
1490. The warhorse is both swift and nimble,
1491. It has hollow hooves and flat legs,
1492. Short the thigh and the croup very broad,
1493. Long the flanks and the spine very high,
1494. White the tail and the mane yellow,
1495. Small the ears, the head all tawny;
1496. There is no beast that can match it.
1497. The archbishop spurs with such great valor:
1498. He will not fail to assail Abisme;
1499. He goes to strike him on the marvelous shield:
1500. There are stones, amethysts and topazes,
1501. esterminals and carbuncles that blaze;
1502. In Val Metas a devil gave it to him,
1503. And the emir Galafe sent it on to him.
1504. Turpin strikes him there, who spares him not at all,
1505. After his blow I think it is not worth a denier,
1506. He cleaves his body from one side to the other,
1507. So that he strikes him dead in an empty space.
1508. The French say: «Here is great valor!
1509. In the archbishop the crozier is well safe.»

---

## Laisse CXV (lines 1510–1525)

**Original**
1510. Franceis veient que paiens i ad tant, [voir, avoir]
1511. De tutes parz en sunt cuvert li camp; [être, couvrir]
1512. Suvent regretent Oliver e Rollant [regretter]
1513. Les .XII. pers, qu'il lor seient guarant. [être]
1514. E l'arcevesque lur dist de sun semblant: [dire]
1515. «Seignors barons, n'en alez mespensant! [aller, mespenser (mal penser)]
1516. Pur Deu vos pri que ne seiez fuiant, [prier, être]
1517. Que nuls prozdom malvaisement n'en chant. [chanter]
1518. Asez est mielz que moerium cumbatant. [être, mourir, combattre]
1519. Pramis nus est, fin prendrum a itant, [promettre, prendre]
1520. Ultre cest jurn ne serum plus vivant; [être, vivre]
1521. Mais d'une chose vos soi jo ben guarant: [être]
1522. Seint pareïs vos est abandunant; [abandonner]
1523. As Innocenz vos en serez seant.» [seoir (être assis)]
1524. A icest mot si s'esbaldissent Franc, [esbaldir (s'enhardir)]
1525. Cel nen i ad «Munjoie!» ne demant. AOI. [avoir, demander]

**Translation**
1510. The French see that there are so many pagans there,
1511. On all sides the fields are covered with them;
1512. Often they lament Oliver and Roland,
1513. The Twelve Peers, that they might be their protectors.
1514. And the archbishop spoke to them in his fashion:
1515. «Lord barons, do not go thinking ill!
1516. For God's sake I pray you not to flee,
1517. So that no worthy man may sing ill of it.
1518. It is much better that we die fighting.
1519. It is promised to us, we shall come to our end thereby,
1520. Beyond this day we shall be alive no longer;
1521. But of one thing I am your good warrant:
1522. Holy paradise is granted to you;
1523. Among the Innocents you shall be seated.»
1524. At this word the Franks take heart,
1525. There is not one who does not cry «Munjoie!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXVI (lines 1526–1544)

**Original**
1526. Un Sarrazin i out de Sarraguce, [avoir]
1527. De la citet l'une meitet est sue: [être]
1528. Ço est Climborins, ki pas ne fut produme. [être]
1529. Fiance prist de Guenelun le cunte, [prendre]
1530. Par amistiet l'en baisat en la buche, [baiser]
1531. Si l'en dunat s'espee e s'escarbuncle. [donner]
1532. Tere Major ço dit, metrat a hunte, [dire, mettre]
1533. A l'emperere si toldrat la curone. [tolir (enlever)]
1534. Siet el ceval qu'il cleimet Barbamusche, [seoir (être assis), clamer (nommer)]
1535. Plus est isnels que esprever ne arunde. [être]
1536. Brochet le bien, le frein li abandunet, [brocher (éperonner), abandonner]
1537. Si vait ferir Engeler de Guascoigne. [aller, ferir (frapper)]
1538. Nel poet guarir sun escut ne sa bronie: [pouvoir, garir (protéger)]
1539. De sun espiet el cors li met la mure, [mettre]
1540. Empeint le ben, tut le fer li mist ultre, [empeindre (pousser), mettre]
1541. Pleine sa hanste el camp mort le tresturnet. [tresturner (renverser)]
1542. Apres escriet: «Cist sunt bon a (o)[c]unfundre! [escrier (s'écrier), être, confondre]
1543. Ferez, paien, pur la presse derumpre!» [ferir (frapper), derompre (rompre)]
1544. Dient Franceis: «Deus quel doel de prodome!» AOI. [dire]

**Translation**
1526. There was a Saracen there from Saragossa,
1527. Of the city one half is his:
1528. He is Climborin, who was no worthy man at all.
1529. He took an oath from Ganelon the count,
1530. In friendship kissed him on the mouth,
1531. And gave him his sword and his carbuncle.
1532. The Great Land, he says, he will put to shame,
1533. And from the emperor he will take away the crown.
1534. He sits on the horse he calls Barbamusche,
1535. Swifter is it than sparrowhawk or swallow.
1536. He spurs it hard, gives it free rein,
1537. And goes to strike Engelier of Gascony.
1538. Neither his shield nor his hauberk can protect him:
1539. He thrusts the point of his spear into his body,
1540. Drives it in hard, sends all the iron through,
1541. With his lance's full length flings him dead in the field.
1542. Then he cries out: «These are good to destroy!
1543. Strike, pagans, to break the press!»
1544. The Franks say: «God, what grief for so brave a man!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXVII (lines 1545–1561)

**Original**
1545. Li quens Rollant en apelet Oliver: [appeler]
1546. «Sire cumpainz, ja est morz Engeler; [être, mourir]
1547. Nus n'avium plus vaillant chevaler.» [avoir]
1548. Respont li quens: «Deus le me doinst venger!» [répondre, donner, venger]
1549. Sun cheval brochet des esperuns d'or mier, [brocher (éperonner)]
1550. Tient Halteclere, sanglent en est l'acer, [tenir, être]
1551. Par grant vertut vait ferir le paien. [aller, ferir (frapper)]
1552. Brandist sun colp e li Sarrazins chiet; [brandir, cheoir (tomber)]
1553. L'anme de lui en portent aversers. [porter]
1554. Puis ad ocis le duc Alphaïen; [avoir, ocire (tuer)]
1555. Escababi i ad le chef trenchet; [avoir, trancher]
1556. .VII. Arrabiz i ad deschevalcet: [avoir, deschevalcier (désarçonner)]
1557. Cil ne sunt proz ja mais pur guerreier. [être, guerroyer]
1558. Ço dist Rollant: «Mis cumpainz est irez! [dire, être]
1559. Encuntre mei fait asez a preiser. [faire, priser]
1560. Pur itels colps nos ad Charles plus cher.» [avoir]
1561. A voiz escriet: «Ferez i, chevaler!» AOI. [escrier (s'écrier), ferir (frapper)]

**Translation**
1545. Count Roland calls out to Oliver:
1546. «Sir companion, Engelier is now dead;
1547. We had no more valiant knight.»
1548. The count replies: «May God grant me to avenge him!»
1549. He spurs his horse with spurs of pure gold,
1550. Grasps Halteclere, its steel is bloody,
1551. With great strength he goes to strike the pagan.
1552. He brandishes his blow and the Saracen falls;
1553. Demons carry off his soul.
1554. Then he has slain Duke Alphaïen;
1555. He has cut off the head of Escababi;
1556. He has unhorsed seven Arabs:
1557. They will never again be of use for waging war.
1558. Roland says: «My companion is enraged!
1559. Beside me he does very well, worthy of praise.
1560. For such blows Charles holds us the dearer.»
1561. In a loud voice he cries out: «Strike here, knights!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXVIII (lines 1562–1579)

**Original**
1562. D'altre part est un paien, Valdabrun: [être]
1563. Celoi levat le rei Marsiliun, [lever]
1564. Sire est par mer de .IIII.C. drodmunz; [être]
1565. N'i ad eschipre quis cleim se par loi nun. [avoir, clamer (réclamer)]
1566. Jerusalem prist ja par traïsun, [prendre]
1567. Si violat le temple Salomon, [violer]
1568. Le patriarche ocist devant les funz. [ocire (tuer)]
1569. Cil ot fiance del cunte Guenelon: [avoir]
1570. Il li dunat s'espee e mil manguns. [donner]
1571. Siet el cheval qu'il cleimet Gramimund, [seoir (être assis), clamer (nommer)]
1572. Plus est isnels que nen est uns falcuns. [être]
1573. Brochet le bien des aguz esperuns, [brocher (éperonner)]
1574. Si vait ferir li riche duc Sansun, [aller, ferir (frapper)]
1575. L'escut li freint e l'osberc li derumpt, [fraindre (briser), derompre (rompre)]
1576. El cors li met les pans del gunfanun, [mettre]
1577. Pleine sa hanste l'abat mort des arçuns: [abattre]
1578. «Ferez paien, car tres ben les veintrum!» [ferir (frapper), vaincre]
1579. Dient Franceis: «Deus quel doel de baron.!» AOI. [dire]

**Translation**
1562. On the other side is a pagan, Valdabrun:
1563. He stood godfather to King Marsile,
1564. He is lord by sea of four hundred dromons;
1565. There is no sailor who does not claim him as his rightful master.
1566. He took Jerusalem once by treason,
1567. And violated the temple of Solomon,
1568. He slew the patriarch before the baptismal fonts.
1569. This man had the pledge of Count Ganelon:
1570. He gave him his sword and a thousand mangons.
1571. He sits on the horse he calls Gramimund,
1572. Swifter is it than a falcon is.
1573. He spurs it well with sharp spurs,
1574. And goes to strike the mighty duke Samson,
1575. He shatters his shield and tears apart his hauberk,
1576. Into his body he drives the folds of the gonfanon,
1577. With his lance at full length he strikes him dead from the saddle-bows:
1578. «Strike, pagans, for we shall vanquish them right well!»
1579. The Franks say: «God, what grief for such a baron!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXIX (lines 1580–1592)

**Original**
1580. Li quens Rollant, quant il veit Sansun mort, [voir, mourir]
1581. Poez saveir que mult grant doel en out. [pouvoir, savoir, avoir]
1582. Sun ceval brochet, si li curt ad esforz; [brocher (éperonner), courir]
1583. Tient Durendal, qui plus valt que fin ór. [tenir, valoir]
1584. Vait le ferir li bers, quanque il pout, [aller, ferir (frapper), pouvoir]
1585. Desur sun elme, ki gemmet fut ad or:
1586. Trenchet la teste e la bronie e le cors, [trancher]
1587. La bone sele, ki est gemmet ad or,
1588. E al cheval parfundement le dos;
1589. Ambure ocit, ki quel blasme ne quil lot. [ocire (tuer), blâmer, louer]
1590. Dient paien(t) [...]: «Cist colp nus est mult fort!» [dire, être]
1591. Respont Rollant: «Ne pois amer les voz; [répondre, pouvoir, aimer]
1592. Devers vos est li orguilz e li torz.» AOI. [être]

**Translation**
1580. Count Roland, when he sees Samson dead,
1581. You may be sure that he felt very great grief at it.
1582. He spurs his horse, and rushes at him with all his might;
1583. He grasps Durendal, which is worth more than fine gold.
1584. The baron goes to strike him with all his strength,
1585. Upon his helmet, which was set with gems in gold:
1586. He slices through the head and the hauberk and the body,
1587. The good saddle, which is set with gems in gold,
1588. And deep into the horse's back;
1589. He kills them both, let who will blame or praise it.
1590. The pagans say: «This blow is very hard on us!»
1591. Roland replies: «I cannot love your people;
1592. On your side is the arrogance and the wrong.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXX (lines 1593–1604)

**Original**
1593. D'Affrike i ad un Affrican venut, [avoir, venir]
1594. Ço est Malquiant, le filz al rei Malcud. [être]
1595. Si guarnement sunt tut a or batud; [être, battre]
1596. Cuntre le ciel sur tuz les altres luist. [luire]
1597. Siet el ceval qu'il cleimet Salt Perdut: [seoir (être assis), clamer (nommer)]
1598. Beste nen est ki poisset curre a lui. [être, pouvoir, courir]
1599. Il vait ferir Anseïs en l'escut: [aller, ferir (frapper)]
1600. Tut li trenchat le vermeill e l'azur; [trancher]
1601. De sun osberc li ad les pans rumput, [avoir, rompre]
1602. El cors li met e le fer e le fust; [mettre]
1603. Morz est li quens, de sun tens n'i ad plus. [être, mourir, avoir]
1604. Dient Franceis: «Barun, tant mare fus!» [dire, être]

**Translation**
1593. From Africa there has come an African,
1594. This is Malquiant, the son of King Malcud.
1595. His trappings are all beaten with gold;
1596. Against the sky he shines above all the others.
1597. He sits on the horse that he calls Salt Perdut:
1598. There is no beast that can run as fast as it.
1599. He goes to strike Anseïs on the shield:
1600. He cuts clean through the vermilion and the azure;
1601. From his hauberk he has torn away the skirts,
1602. He thrusts into his body both the iron and the shaft;
1603. The count is dead, of his time there is no more.
1604. The Franks say: «Baron, how ill-fated you were!»

---

## Laisse CXXI (lines 1605–1612)

**Original**
1605. Par le camp vait Turpin li arcevesque; [aller]
1606. Tel coronet ne chantat unches messe [chanter]
1607. Ki de sun cors feïst [...] tantes proecces. [faire]
1608. Dist al paien: «Deus tut mal te tramette! [dire, tramettre (transmettre)]
1609. Tel ad ocis dunt al coer me regrette.» [avoir, ocire (tuer), regretter]
1610. Sun bon ceval i ad fait esdemetre, [avoir, faire, esdemetre (s'élancer)]
1611. Si l'ad ferut sur l'escut de Tulette, [avoir, ferir (frapper)]
1612. Que mort l'abat desur le herbe verte. [abattre]

**Translation**
1605. Through the field goes Turpin the archbishop;
1606. Never did such a tonsured man chant a mass
1607. Who with his body performed such feats of valor.
1608. He said to the pagan: «May God send you every evil!
1609. You have slain one for whom my heart grieves.»
1610. He spurred his good horse to leap forward,
1611. And struck him upon the shield of Toledo,
1612. So that he casts him dead upon the green grass.

---

## Laisse CXXII (lines 1613–1628)

**Original**
1613. De l'altre part est un paien, Grandonies, [être]
1614. Filz Capuel, le rei de Capadoce(neez).
1615. Siet el cheval que il cleimet Marmorie, [seoir (être assis), clamer (nommer)]
1616. Plus est isnels que n'est oisel ki volet; [être, voler]
1617. Laschet la resne, des esperuns le brochet, [lâcher, brocher (éperonner)]
1618. Si vait ferir Gerin par sa grant force. [aller, ferir (frapper)]
1619. L'escut vermeill li freint, de col li portet; [fraindre (briser), porter]
1620. Aprof li ad sa bronie desclose, [avoir, desclore (ouvrir)]
1621. El cors li met tute l'enseingne bloie, [mettre]
1622. Que mort l'abat en une halte roche. [abattre]
1623. Sun cumpaignun Gerers ocit uncore [ocire (tuer)]
1624. E Berenger e Guiun de Seint Antonie;
1625. Puis vait ferir un riche duc Austorje, [aller, ferir (frapper)]
1626. Ki tint Valeri e envers sur le Rosne. [tenir]
1627. Il l'abat mort; paien en unt grant joie. [abattre, avoir]
1628. Dient Franceis: «Mult decheent li nostre!» [dire, decheoir (déchoir)]

**Translation**
1613. On the other side is a pagan, Grandonie,
1614. Son of Capuel, the king of Cappadocia.
1615. He sits on the horse that he calls Marmorie,
1616. Swifter is it than a bird that flies;
1617. He slackens the rein, spurs it with his spurs,
1618. And goes to strike Gerin with his great force.
1619. He shatters his vermilion shield, tears it from his neck;
1620. Then he has ripped open his hauberk,
1621. Drives into his body all the blue ensign,
1622. So that he strikes him dead upon a high rock.
1623. His companion Gerier he slays as well
1624. And Berenger and Guy of Saint Antoine;
1625. Then he goes to strike a rich duke, Austorge,
1626. Who held Valence and the lands along the Rhône.
1627. He strikes him dead; the pagans have great joy of it.
1628. The Franks say: «Greatly do our men decline!»

---

## Laisse CXXIII (lines 1629–1635)

**Original**
1629. [L]i quens Rollant tint s'espee sanglente. [tenir]
1630. Ben ad oït que Franceis se dementent; [avoir, oïr (entendre), se démenter (se lamenter)]
1631. Si grant doel ad que par mi quiet fendre; [avoir, cuidier (croire), fendre]
1632. Dist al paien: «Deus tut mal te consente! [dire, consentir]
1633. Tel as ocis que mult cher te quid vendre!» [ocire (tuer), cuidier (croire), vendre]
1634. Sun ceval brochet, ki oït del cuntence. [brocher (éperonner), oïr (entendre)]
1635. Ki quel cumpert, venuz en sunt ensemble. [comperer (payer cher), être, venir]

**Translation**
1629. Count Roland held his bloodied sword.
1630. He has clearly heard that the Franks are lamenting;
1631. He has such great grief that he thinks he will split apart;
1632. He said to the pagan: «May God grant you every evil!
1633. You have killed one such that I think I will sell him to you very dear!»
1634. He spurs his horse, which heard the dispute.
1635. Whoever pays for it, they have come together.

---

## Laisse CXXIV (lines 1636–1652)

**Original**
1636. Grandonie fut e prozdom e vaillant [être]
1637. E vertuus e vassal cumbatant. [combattre]
1638. Enmi sa veie ad encuntret Rollant. [avoir, encontrer (rencontrer)]
1639. Enceis nel vit, sil recunut veirement [voir, reconoistre (reconnaître)]
1640. Al fier visage e al cors qu'il out gent [avoir]
1641. E al reguart e al contenement:
1642. Ne poet muer qu'il ne s'en espoent, [pouvoir, muer (s'empêcher), espoenter (s'épouvanter)]
1643. Fuïr s'en voel, mais ne li valt nient: [fuir, vouloir, valoir]
1644. Li quens le fiert tant vertuusement [ferir (frapper)]
1645. Tresqu'al nasel tut le elme li fent, [fendre]
1646. Trenchet le nes e la buche e les denz, [trancher]
1647. Trestut le cors e l'osberc jazerenc
1648. De l'oree sele (se)[les] dous alves d'argent
1649. E al ceval le dos parfundement;
1650. Ambure ocist seinz nul recoevrement, [ocire (tuer)]
1651. E cil d'Espaigne s'en cleiment tuit dolent. [clamer (se déclarer)]
1652. Dient Franceis: «Ben fiert nostre guarent!» [dire, ferir (frapper)]

**Translation**
1636. Grandonie was a worthy man and valiant
1637. And mighty and a battling vassal.
1638. In the middle of his path he encountered Roland.
1639. Never before had he seen him, yet he recognized him truly
1640. By his fierce face and his noble body
1641. And by his bearing and his demeanor:
1642. He cannot help but be terrified by it,
1643. He wishes to flee from it, but it avails him nothing:
1644. The count strikes him so mightily
1645. That he splits his whole helmet down to the nasal,
1646. Cuts through the nose and the mouth and the teeth,
1647. The whole body and the hauberk of Jazerant mail
1648. And the two silver flaps of the gilded saddle
1649. And deep into the horse's back;
1650. He slew both without any recovery,
1651. And those of Spain lament over it, all grieving.
1652. The Franks say: «Well does our protector strike!»

---

## Laisse CXXV (lines 1653–1660)

**Original**
1653. La bataille est e merveillose e grant. [être]
1654. Franceis i ferent des espiez brunisant. [ferir (frapper)]
1655. La veïssez si grant dulor de gent, [voir]
1656. Tant hume mort e nasfret e sanglent!
1657. L'un gist sur l'altre e envers e adenz. [gésir (être couché)]
1658. Li Sarrazin nel poent susfrir tant: [pouvoir, souffrir]
1659. Voelent u nun, si guerpissent le camp. [vouloir, guerpir (abandonner)]
1660. Par vive force les encacerent Franc. AOI. [enchacier (pourchasser)]

**Translation**
1653. The battle is both marvelous and great.
1654. The French strike there with their burnished spears.
1655. There you would have seen such great suffering of people,
1656. So many men dead and wounded and bloody!
1657. One lies upon another, both on his back and face down.
1658. The Saracens cannot endure it so long:
1659. Willing or not, they abandon the field.
1660. By living force the Franks pursue them. AOI.

---

## Laisse CXXVI (lines 1661–1670)

**Original**
1661. La (la) b[at]aille est m[erv]eilluse e hastive. [être]
1662. Franceis i ferent par vigur e par ire, [ferir (frapper)]
1663. Tren[chen]t cez poinz, cez costez, cez eschines, [trancher]
1664. Cez vestemenz entresque as chars vives.
1665. Sur l'erbe verte li cler sancs s'en afilet. [afiler (couler)]
1666. [lacuna — line lost in Oxford ms]
1667. «Tere Major, Mahummet te maldie! [maudire]
1668. Sur tute gent est la tue hardie.» [être]
1669. Cel nen i ad ki ne criet: «Marsilie! [avoir, crier]
1670. Cevalche, rei! Bosuign avum d'aïe!» [chevaucher, avoir]

**Translation**
1661. The battle is marvelous and swift.
1662. The French strike there with vigor and with wrath,
1663. They cut through these fists, these flanks, these spines,
1664. These garments down to the living flesh.
1665. On the green grass the bright blood flows away.
1666. [lacuna]
1667. «Great Land, may Mahomet curse you!
1668. Above every people your boldness stands.»
1669. There is not one who does not cry out: «Marsile!
1670. Ride, king! We have need of aid!»

---

## Laisse CXXVII (lines 1671–1690)

**Original**
1671. Li quens Rollant apelet Oliver: [appeler]
1672. «Sire cumpaign, sel volez otrier, [vouloir, otreier (octroyer)]
1673. Li arcevesque est mult bon chevaler, [être]
1674. Nen ad meillor en tere ne suz cel; [avoir]
1675. Ben set ferir e de lance e d'espiet.» [savoir, ferir (frapper)]
1676. Respunt li quens: «Kar li aluns aider!» [répondre, aller, aider]
1677. A icest mot l'unt Francs recumencet. [avoir, recommencer]
1678. Dur sunt li colps e li caples est grefs; [être]
1679. Mu(n)lt grant dulor i ad de chrestiens. [avoir]
1680. Ki puis veïst Rollant e Oliver [voir]
1681. De lur espees e ferir e capler! [ferir (frapper), capler (frapper)]
1682. Li arcevesque i fiert de sun espiet, [ferir (frapper)]
1683. Cels qu'il unt mort, ben les poet hom preiser; [avoir, mourir, pouvoir, priser]
1684. Il est escrit es cartres e es brefs, [être, écrire]
1685. Ço dit la Geste, plus de .IIII. milliers. [dire]
1686. As quatre [es]turs lor est avenut ben; [être, avenir (échoir)]
1687. Li quint apres lor est pesant e gref. [être]
1688. Tuz sunt ocis cist Franceis chevalers, [être, ocire (tuer)]
1689. Ne mes seisante, que Deus i ad esparniez: [avoir, espargnier (épargner)]
1690. Einz que il moergent, se vendrunt mult cher. [mourir, vendre]

**Translation**
1671. Count Roland calls out to Oliver:
1672. «Lord companion, if you will grant it,
1673. The archbishop is a very good knight,
1674. There is none better on earth nor under heaven;
1675. He well knows how to strike with both lance and spear.»
1676. The count replies: «Then let us go help him!»
1677. At this word the Franks renewed the fight.
1678. Hard are the blows and the fighting is grievous;
1679. Very great suffering there is among the Christians.
1680. Whoever then might have seen Roland and Oliver
1681. striking and hewing with their swords!
1682. The archbishop strikes there with his spear,
1683. those whom they have slain, one can well count them;
1684. It is written in the charters and in the records,
1685. So says the Geste, more than four thousand.
1686. In the four assaults it went well for them;
1687. The fifth, after, is heavy and grievous for them.
1688. All are slain, these French knights,
1689. all but sixty, whom God has spared:
1690. Before they die, they will sell themselves very dear.

---

## Laisse CXXVIII (lines 1691–1701)

**Original**
1691. Li quens Rollant des soens i veit grant perte; AOI. [voir]
1692. Sun cumpaignun Oliver en apelet: [appeler]
1693. «Bel sire, chers cumpainz, pur Deu, que vos enhaitet? [enhaitier (encourager)]
1694. Tanz bons vassals veez gesir par tere! [voir, gésir (être couché)]
1695. Pleindre poüms France dulce, la bele: [plaindre, pouvoir]
1696. De tels barons cum or remeint deserte! [remaindre (rester)]
1697. E! reis, amis, que vos ici nen estes? [être]
1698. Oliver, frere, cumment le purrum nus faire? [pouvoir, faire]
1699. Cum faitement li manderum nuveles?» [mander]
1700. Dist Oliver: «Jo nel sai cument quere. [dire, savoir, querre (chercher)]
1701. Mielz voeill murir que hunte nus seit retraite.» AOI. [vouloir, mourir, être, retraire (rapporter)]

**Translation**
1691. Count Roland sees great loss among his own men; AOI.
1692. He calls upon his companion Oliver:
1693. «Fair lord, dear comrade, in God's name, what cheers you?
1694. So many good vassals you see lying upon the ground!
1695. We may pity France the sweet, the fair:
1696. Of such barons how she now remains bereft!
1697. Ah! king, friend, why are you not here?
1698. Oliver, brother, how shall we manage it?
1699. In what way shall we send him news?»
1700. Said Oliver: «I know not how to seek it.
1701. I would rather die than that shame be reported of us.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXXIX (lines 1702–1712)

**Original**
1702. Ço dist Rollant: «Cornerai l'olifant, [dire, corner]
1703. Si l'orrat Carles, ki est as porz passant. [oïr (entendre), être, passer]
1704. Jo vos plevis ja returnerunt Franc.» [plevir (garantir), retourner]
1705. Dist Oliver: «Vergoigne sereit grant [dire, être]
1706. E reprover a trestuz voz parenz;
1707. Iceste hunte dureit al lur vivant! [durer]
1708. Quant jel vos dis, n'en feïstes nient; [dire, faire]
1709. Mais nel ferez par le men loement. [faire]
1710. Se vos cornez, n'ert mie hardement. [corner, être]
1711. Ja avez vos ambsdous les braz sanglanz!» [avoir]
1712. Respont li quens: «Colps i ai fait mult genz!» AOI. [répondre, avoir, faire]

**Translation**
1702. Roland said: «I will sound the olifant,
1703. So Charles, who is passing through the passes, will hear it.
1704. I pledge to you the Franks will return at once.»
1705. Oliver said: «It would be a great shame
1706. And a reproach to all your kinsmen;
1707. This disgrace would last all their lives!
1708. When I told you this, you did nothing about it;
1709. But you will not do it with my approval.
1710. If you sound the horn, it will be no act of valor.
1711. Already you have both your arms bloodied!»
1712. The count replied: «I have struck blows most fine!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXXX (lines 1713–1721)

**Original**
1713. Ço dit Rollant: «Forz est nostre bataille; [dire, être]
1714. Jo cornerai, si l'orrat li reis Karles.» [corner, oïr (entendre)]
1715. Dist Oliver: «Ne sereit vasselage! [dire, être]
1716. Quant jel vos dis, cumpainz, vos ne deignastes. [dire, daigner]
1717. Si fust li reis, n'i oüsum damage. [être, avoir]
1718. Cil ki la sunt n'en deivent aveir blasme.» [être, devoir, avoir]
1719. Dist Oliver: «Par ceste meie barbe, [dire]
1720. Se puis veeir ma gente sorur Alde, [pouvoir, voir]
1721. Ne jerrei(e)z ja mais entre sa brace!» AOI. [gesir (coucher)]

**Translation**
1713. Roland says: «Our battle is fierce;
1714. I will sound the horn, and King Charles will hear it.»
1715. Oliver said: «That would be no act of valor!
1716. When I told you of it, comrade, you did not deign to.
1717. Had the king been here, we would have suffered no harm.
1718. Those who are over there ought not bear blame for it.»
1719. Oliver said: «By this beard of mine,
1720. If I can see my fair sister Alde again,
1721. You shall never lie in her arms!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXXXI (lines 1722–1736)

**Original**
1722. Ço dist Rollant: «Por quei me portez ire?» [dire, porter]
1723. (E cil) E il respont: «Cumpainz, vos le feïstes, [répondre, faire]
1724. Kar vasselage par sens nen est folie; [être]
1725. Mielz valt mesure que ne fait estultie. [valoir, faire]
1726. Franceis sunt morz par vostre legerie. [être, mourir]
1727. Jamais Karlon de nus n'avrat servise. [avoir]
1728. Sem(e) creïsez, venuz i fust mi sire; [croire, venir, être]
1729. Ceste bataille oüsum faite u prise; [avoir, faire, prendre]
1730. U pris ú mort i fust li reis Marsilie. [prendre, mourir, être]
1731. Vostre proecce, Rollant, mar la ve[ï]mes! [voir]
1732. Karles li Magnes de nos n'avrat aïe. [avoir]
1733. N'ert mais tel home des qu'a Deu juïse. [être]
1734. Vos i murrez e France en ert (...) huníe. [mourir, être]
1735. Oi nus defalt la leial cumpaignie: [defaillir (manquer)]
1736. Einz le vesp(e)re mult ert gref la departie.» AOI. [être]

**Translation**
1722. Roland said this: «Why do you bear anger toward me?»
1723. And he answers: «Companion, you did it,
1724. For bravery with good sense is not folly;
1725. Moderation is worth more than recklessness.
1726. The French are dead through your rashness.
1727. Never again will Charles have service from us.
1728. Had you trusted me, my lord would have come here;
1729. This battle we would have fought and won;
1730. Captured or killed would have been King Marsile.
1731. Your prowess, Roland, in an evil hour did we see it!
1732. Charlemagne will have no help from us.
1733. There will be no such man until God's Judgment.
1734. You will die here and France will be shamed by it.
1735. Today the loyal fellowship fails us:
1736. Before evening the parting will be most grievous.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXXXII (lines 1737–1752)

**Original**
1737. Li arceves[ques] les ót cuntrarier, [oïr (entendre), cuntralier (se quereller)]
1738. Le cheval brochet des esperuns d'or mer, [brocher (éperonner)]
1739. Vint tresqu'a els, sis prist a castier: [venir, prendre, castier (réprimander)]
1740. «Sire Rollant, e vos, sire Oliver,
1741. Pur Deu vos pri, ne vos cuntraliez! [prier, cuntralier (se quereller)]
1742. Ja li corners ne nos avreit mester, [avoir]
1743. Mais nepurquant si est il asez melz: [être]
1744. Venget li reis, si nus purrat venger; [venir, pouvoir, venger]
1745. Ja cil d'Espaigne ne s'en deivent turner liez. [devoir, tourner]
1746. Nostre Franceis i descendrunt a pied, [descendre]
1747. Truverunt nos e morz e detrenchez, [trouver]
1748. Leverunt nos en bieres sur sumers, [lever]
1749. Si nus plurrunt de doel e de pitet, [pleurer]
1750. Enfuerunt [nos] en aitres de musters; [enfouir]
1751. N'en mangerunt ne lu ne porc ne chen.» [manger]
1752. Respunt Rollant: «Sire, mult dites bien.» AOI. [répondre, dire]

**Translation**
1737. The archbishop heard them quarreling,
1738. He spurs his horse with the spurs of pure gold,
1739. Came right up to them, and began to rebuke them:
1740. «Lord Roland, and you, Lord Oliver,
1741. For God's sake I pray you, do not quarrel!
1742. The sounding of the horn would now be of no use to us,
1743. But nevertheless it is much better so:
1744. Let the king come, for he will be able to avenge us;
1745. Those of Spain shall not return from here rejoicing.
1746. Our Frenchmen will dismount here on foot,
1747. They will find us both dead and cut to pieces,
1748. They will lift us onto biers on pack-horses,
1749. And will weep for us with grief and pity,
1750. They will bury us in the churchyards of minsters;
1751. Neither wolf nor pig nor dog shall eat of us.»
1752. Roland answers: «Lord, you speak very well.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXXXIII (lines 1753–1760)

**Original**
1753. Rollant ad mis l'olifan a sa buche, [avoir, mettre]
1754. Empeint le ben, par grant vertut le sunet. [empeindre (pousser), sonner]
1755. Halt sunt li pui e la voiz est mult lunge, [être]
1756. Granz .XXX. liwes l'oïrent il respundre. [oïr (entendre), répondre]
1757. Karles l'oït e ses cumpaignes tutes. [oïr (entendre)]
1758. Ço dit li reis: «Bataille funt nostre hume!» [dire, faire]
1759. E Guenelun li respundit encuntre: [répondre]
1760. «S'altre le desist, ja semblast grant mençunge!» AOI. [dire, sembler]

**Translation**
1753. Roland has put the oliphant to his mouth,
1754. He grips it well, sounds it with great force.
1755. High are the peaks and the sound carries very far,
1756. They heard it echo back a full thirty leagues.
1757. Charles heard it, and all his companions.
1758. The king says: «Our men are giving battle!»
1759. And Ganelon answered him in return:
1760. «Had another said it, it would seem a great lie!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXXXIV (lines 1761–1784)

**Original**
1761. Li quens Rollant, par peine e par ahans,
1762. Par grant dulor sunet sun olifan. [sonner]
1763. Par mi la buche en salt fors li cler sancs. [saillir (jaillir)]
1764. De sun cervel le temple en est rumpant. [rompre]
1765. Del corn qu'il tient l'oiïe en est mult grant: [tenir]
1766. Karles l'entent, ki est as porz passant. [oïr (entendre), être]
1767. Naimes li duc l'oïd, si l'escultent li Franc. [oïr (entendre), écouter]
1768. Ce dist li reis: «Jo oi le corn Rollant! [dire, oïr (entendre)]
1769. Unc nel sunast se ne fust (cu)cumbatant.» [sonner, être, combattre]
1770. Guenes respunt: «De bataille est il nient! [répondre, être]
1771. Ja estes veilz e fluriz e blancs; [être]
1772. Par tels paroles vus resemblez enfant. [ressembler]
1773. Asez savez le grant orgoill Rollant; [savoir]
1774. Ço est merveille que Deus le soefret tant. [être, souffrir]
1775. Ja prist il Noples seinz le vostre comant; [prendre]
1776. Fors s'en eissirent li Sarrazins dedenz, [issir (sortir)]
1777. Sis cumbatirent al bon vassal Rollant; [combattre]
<!-- lacuna: narrative gap, no Bédier line here -->
1778. Puis od les ewes lavat les prez del sanc, [laver]
1779. Pur cel le fist ne fust a[pa]rissant. [faire, être]
1780. Pur un sul levre vat tute jur cornant, [aller, corner]
1781. Devant ses pers vait il ore gabant. [aller, gaber (railler)]
1782. Suz cel n'ad gent ki [l']osast (re)querre en champ. [oser, querre (chercher)]
1783. Car chevalcez! Pur qu'alez arestant? [chevaucher, aller, arester (s'arrêter)]
1784. Tere Major mult est loinz ça devant.» AOI. [être]

**Translation**
1761. Count Roland, with pain and with travail,
1762. With great anguish sounds his oliphant.
1763. Out through his mouth the bright blood leaps forth.
1764. The temple of his brain is bursting.
1765. Of the horn he holds the sound is very great:
1766. Charles hears it, who is passing through the gorges.
1767. Naimes the duke heard it, and the Franks listen to it.
1768. The king says: «I hear Roland's horn!
1769. He would never sound it unless he were fighting.»
1770. Ganelon answers: «There is no battle at all!
1771. You are already old and white-haired and hoary;
1772. By such words you seem like a child.
1773. You know well Roland's great pride;
1774. It is a marvel that God suffers him so long.
1775. He once took Noples without your command;
1776. The Saracens within sallied forth,
1777. And they fought the good vassal Roland;
1778. Then with the waters he washed the meadows of the blood,
1779. So that it should not be apparent.
1780. For a single hare he goes blowing his horn all day,
1781. Before his peers he goes now boasting.
1782. Under heaven there is no man who would dare seek him out in the field.
1783. So ride on! Why do you keep stopping?
1784. The Great Land is very far on ahead.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXXXV (lines 1785–1795)

**Original**
1785. Li quens Rollant ad la buche sanglente. [avoir]
1786. De sun cervel rumput en est li temples. [rompre, être]
1787. L'olifan sunet a dulor e a peine. [sonner]
1788. Karles l'oït e ses Franceis l'entendent, [oïr (entendre), entendre]
1789. Ço dist li reis: «cCel corn ad lunge aleine!» [dire, avoir]
1790. Respont dux Neimes: «Baron i fait la p[e]ine! [répondre, faire]
1791. Bataille i ad, par le men escientre. [avoir]
1792. Cil l'at traït ki vos en roevet feindre. [avoir, trahir, rover (demander), feindre]
1793. Adubez vos, si criez vostre enseigne, [aduber (s'équiper), crier]
1794. Si sucurez vostre maisnee gente: [secourir]
1795. Asez oez que Rollant se dementet!» [oïr (entendre), se démenter (se lamenter)]

**Translation**
1785. Count Roland has a bloody mouth.
1786. The temple of his brain is burst.
1787. He sounds the oliphant in sorrow and in pain.
1788. Charles hears it and his Franks hear it,
1789. The king said: «That horn has long breath!»
1790. Duke Naimes answers: «A baron takes the trouble of it!
1791. There is a battle, by my knowledge.
1792. He who asks you to feign indifference has betrayed him.
1793. Arm yourselves, cry out your war-cry,
1794. And succor your noble household:
1795. You hear well enough that Roland laments!»

---

## Laisse CXXXVI (lines 1796–1806)

**Original**
1796. Li empereres ad fait suner ses corns. [avoir, faire, sonner]
1797. Franceis descendent, si adubent lor cors [descendre, aduber (équiper)]
1798. D'osbercs e de helmes e d'espees a or.
1799. Escuz unt genz e espiez granz e forz, [avoir]
1800. E gunfanuns blancs e vermeilz e blois.
1801. Es destrers muntent tuit li barun de l'ost, [monter]
1802. Brochent ad ait tant cum durent li port. [brocher (éperonner), durer]
1803. N'i ad celoi (a celoi) a l'altre ne parolt: [avoir, parler]
1804. «Se veïssum Rollant einz qu'il fust mortz, [voir, être, mourir]
1805. Ensembl'od lui i durriums granz colps.» [donner]
1806. De ço qui calt? car demuret i unt trop. [chaloir (importer), avoir, demeurer]

**Translation**
1796. The emperor has had his horns sounded.
1797. The French dismount, and they equip their bodies
1798. With hauberks and helmets and swords of gold.
1799. They have fine shields and great strong spears,
1800. And gonfalons white and vermilion and blue.
1801. Onto their war-horses mount all the barons of the host,
1802. They spur eagerly as far as the passes extend.
1803. There is not one of them but speaks to the other:
1804. «If only we might see Roland before he is dead,
1805. Together with him we would deal great blows.»
1806. What does it matter? For they have tarried too long.

---

## Laisse CXXXVII (lines 1807–1829)

**Original**
1807. Esclargiz est li vespres e li jurz. [être, esclargir (s'éclaircir)]
1808. Cuntre le soleil reluisent cil adub, [reluire]
1809. Osbercs e helmes i getent grant flambur, [jeter]
1810. E cil escuz, ki ben sunt peinz a flurs, [être, peindre]
1811. E cil espiez(z), cil oret gunfanun.
1812. Li empereres cevalchet par irur [chevaucher]
1813. E li Franceis dolenz et cur[uçus](ius);
1814. N'i ad celoi ki durement ne plurt, [avoir, pleurer]
1815. E de Rollant sunt en grant poür. [être]
1816. Li reis fait prendre le cunte Guenelun, [faire, prendre]
1817. Sil cumandat as cous de sa maisun. [commander]
1818. Tut li plus maistre en apelet, Besgun. [appeler]
1819. «Ben le me guarde, si cume tel felon! [garder]
1820. De ma maisnee ad faite traïsun.» [avoir, faire]
1821. Cil le receit, si met .C. cumpaignons [recevoir, mettre]
1822. De la quisine, des mielz e des pejurs.
1823. Icil li peilent la barbe e les gernuns; [peler (arracher)]
<!-- unnumbered: "(Morz est Turpin le guerreier Charlun)" is a stray verse here in the manuscript; Bédier and Mortier leave it unnumbered -->
1824. Cascun le fiert .IIII. colps de sun puign; [ferir (frapper)]
1825. Ben le batirent a fuz e a bastuns; [battre]
1826. E si li metent el col un caeignun, [mettre]
1827. Si l'encaeinent altresi cum un urs; [enchaîner]
1828. Sur un sumer l'unt mis a deshonor. [avoir, mettre]
1829. Tant le guardent quel rendent a Charlun. [garder, rendre]

**Translation**
1807. The evening and the day grow bright.
1808. Against the sun those pieces of armor gleam,
1809. Hauberks and helmets cast forth a great blaze of light,
1810. And those shields, which are finely painted with flowers,
1811. And those spears, those gilded gonfalons.
1812. The emperor rides on in wrath
1813. And the French, grieving and angry;
1814. There is none among them who does not weep bitterly,
1815. And for Roland they are in great fear.
1816. The king has Count Ganelon seized,
1817. And handed him over to the cooks of his household.
1818. He calls upon the chief of them all, Besgun.
1819. «Guard him well for me, as befits such a felon!
1820. He has committed treason against my house.»
1821. The cook receives him, and sets a hundred companions
1822. From the kitchen, of the best and of the worst.
1823. These pluck out his beard and his mustaches;
1824. Each strikes him four blows with his fist;
1825. They beat him soundly with rods and with sticks;
1826. And they put a chain around his neck,
1827. And chain him up just like a bear;
1828. On a pack-horse they have set him in dishonor.
1829. They guard him until they hand him back to Charles.

---

## Laisse CXXXVIII (lines 1830–1841)

**Original**
1830. Halt sunt li pui e tenebrus e grant, AOI. [être]
1831. Li val parfunt e les ewes curant. [courir]
1832. Sunent cil graisle e derere e devant, [sonner]
1833. E tuit rachatent encuntre l'olifant. [rachater (répondre par un écho)]
1834. Li empereres chevalchet ireement, [chevaucher]
1835. E li Franceis cur(i[...]us)uçus e dolent;
1836. N'i ad celoi n'i plurt e se dement, [avoir, pleurer, se démenter (se lamenter)]
1837. E p[ri]ent Deu qu'il guarisset Rollant [prier, garir (protéger)]
1838. Josque il vengent el camp cumunement: [venir]
1839. Ensembl'od lui i ferrunt veirement. [ferir (frapper)]
1840. De ço qui calt? car ne lur valt nient. [chaloir (importer), valoir]
1841. Demurent trop, n'i poedent estre a tens. AOI. [demeurer, pouvoir, être]

**Translation**
1830. High are the peaks and shadowy and great,
1831. The valleys deep and the rivers swift-running.
1832. Those trumpets sound, both in the rear and in the van,
1833. And all of them answer back the call of the oliphant.
1834. The emperor rides on in wrath,
1835. And the Franks ride troubled and grieving;
1836. There is not one of them who does not weep and lament,
1837. And they pray to God that He keep Roland safe
1838. Until they reach the field all together:
1839. There at his side they will truly strike their blows.
1840. But what use is it? It avails them nothing.
1841. They tarry too long, they cannot be there in time. AOI.

---

## Laisse CXXXIX (lines 1842–1850)

**Original**
1842. Par grant irur chevalchet li reis Charles; [chevaucher]
1843. Desur (...) sa brunie li gist sa blanche barbe. [gésir (être couché)]
1844. Puignent ad ait tuit li barun de France; [poindre (éperonner)]
1845. N'i ad icel ne demeint irance [avoir, demener (manifester)]
1846. Que il ne sunt a Rollant le cataigne, [être]
1847. Ki se cumbat as Sarrazins d'Espaigne; [combattre]
1848. Si est blecet, ne quit que anme i remaigne. [être, blecier (blesser), cuidier (croire), remaindre (rester)]
1849. Deus! quels seisante humes i ad en sa cumpaigne! [avoir]
1850. Unches meillurs n'en out reis ne c[at]aignes. AOI. [avoir]

**Translation**
1842. In great wrath rides King Charles;
1843. Over his hauberk hangs his white beard.
1844. The barons of France spur on eagerly;
1845. There is not one of them but feels grief
1846. That they are not beside Roland the captain,
1847. Who fights against the Saracens of Spain;
1848. He is so wounded, I do not think a soul remains in him.
1849. God! what sixty men he has in his company!
1850. Never did king or captain have better. AOI.

---

## Laisse CXL (lines 1851–1868)

**Original**
1851. Rollant reguardet es munz e es lariz; [regarder]
1852. De cels de France i veit tanz morz gesir! [voir, gésir (être couché)]
1853. E il les pluret cum chevaler gentill: [pleurer]
1854. «Seignors barons, de vos ait Deus mercit! [avoir]
1855. Tutes voz anmes otreit il pareïs! [otreier (octroyer)]
1856. En seintes flurs il les facet gesir! [faire, gésir (être couché)]
1857. Meillors vassals de vos unkes ne vi. [voir]
1858. Si lungement tuz tens m'avez servit, [avoir, servir]
1859. A oes Carlon si granz païs cunquis! [conquérir]
1860. Li empereres tant mare vos nurrit! [nourrir]
1861. Tere de France mult estes dulz païs [être]
1862. Oi desertet a tant rubostl exill. [déserter]
1863. Barons Franceis, pur mei vos vei murir: [voir, mourir]
1864. Jo ne vos pois tenser ne guarantir. [pouvoir, tenser (protéger), garantir]
1865. Aït vos Deus, ki unkes ne mentit! [aïdier (aider), mentir]
1866. Oliver, frere, vos ne dei jo faillir. [devoir, faillir]
1867. De doel murra, se altre ne m'i ocit. [mourir, ocire (tuer)]
1868. Sire cumpainz, alum i referir!» [aller, referir (frapper de nouveau)]

**Translation**
1851. Roland looks over the mountains and the slopes;
1852. of those of France he sees so many lying dead!
1853. And he weeps for them as a noble knight:
1854. «Lord barons, may God have mercy on you!
1855. May he grant paradise to all your souls!
1856. May he make them lie among holy flowers!
1857. Better vassals than you I never saw.
1858. So long, through all time, you have served me,
1859. and for Charles's use conquered such great lands!
1860. The emperor raised you for such an ill fate!
1861. Land of France, you are a most sweet country,
1862. today laid waste by such cruel ruin.
1863. French barons, for my sake I see you die:
1864. I cannot protect you nor save you.
1865. May God aid you, who never lied!
1866. Oliver, brother, I must not fail you.
1867. I shall die of grief, if nothing else slays me.
1868. Lord companion, let us go strike again!»

---

## Laisse CXLI (lines 1869–1885)

**Original**
1869. Li quens Rollant el champ est repairet: [être, repairer (rentrer)]
1870. Tient Durendal, cume vassal i fiert. [tenir, ferir (frapper)]
1871. Faldrun de Pui i ad par mi trenchet, [avoir, trancher]
1872. E .XXIIII. de tuz les melz preisez:
1873. Jamais n'iert home plus se voeillet venger. [être, vouloir, venger]
1874. Si cum li cerfs s'en vait devant les chiens, [s'en aller]
1875. Devant Rollant si s'en fuient paiens. [s'enfuir]
1876. Dist l'arcevesque: «Asez le faites ben! [dire, faire]
1877. Itel valor deit aveir chevaler [devoir, avoir]
1878. Ki armes portet e en bon cheval set; [porter, seoir (être assis)]
1879. En bataille deit estre forz e fiers, [devoir, être]
1880. U altrement ne valt .IIII. deners; [valoir]
1881. Einz deit monie estre en un de cez mustiers, [devoir, être]
1882. Si prierat tuz jurz por noz peccez.» [prier]
1883. Respunt Rollant: «Ferez, nes esparignez!» [répondre, ferir (frapper), épargner]
1884. A icest mot l'unt Francs recumencet. [avoir, recommencer]
1885. Mult grant damage i out de chrestiens. [avoir]

**Translation**
1869. Count Roland has returned to the field:
1870. He holds Durendal, and strikes like a brave warrior.
1871. He has cut Faldrun of Pui clean through the middle,
1872. And twenty-four of the most highly esteemed:
1873. Never will a man be more eager to take revenge.
1874. Just as the stag flees before the hounds,
1875. So the pagans flee before Roland.
1876. The archbishop said: «You do this very well indeed!
1877. Such valor a knight ought to have
1878. Who bears arms and sits upon a good horse;
1879. In battle he ought to be strong and fierce,
1880. Or otherwise he is not worth four deniers;
1881. Rather he ought to be a monk in one of these minsters,
1882. Where he would pray every day for our sins.»
1883. Roland answers: «Strike, do not spare them!»
1884. At this word the Franks have begun again.
1885. Very great was the loss there among the Christians.

---

## Laisse CXLII (lines 1886–1912)

**Original**
1886. Home ki ço set, que ja n'avrat prisun [savoir, avoir]
1887. En tel bataill[e] fait grant defension: [faire]
1888. Pur ço sunt Francs si fiers cume leuns. [être]
1889. As vus Marsilie en guise de barunt.
1890. Siet el cheval qu'il apelet Gaignun, [seoir (être assis), appeler]
1891. Brochet le ben, si vait ferir Bevon, [brocher (éperonner), aller, ferir (frapper)]
1892. Icil ert sire de Belne e de Digun, [être]
1893. L'escut li freint e l'osberc li derumpt, [fraindre (briser), derompre (rompre)]
1894. Que mort l'abat seinz altre descunfisun; [abattre]
1895. Puis ad ocis Yvoeries e Ivon [avoir, ocire (tuer)]
1896. Ensembl'od els Gerard de Russillun.
1897. Li quens Rollant ne li est guaires loign; [être]
1898. Dist al paien: «Damnesdeus mal te duinst! [dire, donner]
1899. A si grant tort m'ociz mes cumpaignuns! [ocire (tuer)]
1900. Colp en avras einz que nos departum, [avoir, départir (se séparer)]
1901. E de m'espee enquoi savras le nom.» [savoir]
1902. Vait le ferir en guise de baron: [aller, ferir (frapper)]
1903. Trenchet li ad li quens le destre poign. [avoir, trancher]
1904. Puis prent la teste de Jurfaleu le Blund, [prendre]
1905. Icil ert filz al rei Marsiliun. [être]
1906. Paien escrient: «Aíe nos, Mahum! [escrier (s'écrier)]
1907. Li nostre deu, vengez nos de Carlun. [venger]
1908. En ceste tere nus ad mis tels feluns! [avoir, mettre]
1909. Ja pur murir le camp ne guerpirunt.» [guerpir (abandonner)]
1910. Dist l'un a l'altre: «E! car nos en fuiums!» [dire, s'enfuir]
1911. A icest mot tels .C. milie s'en vunt: [s'en aller]
1912. Ki ques rapelt, ja n'en returnerunt. AOI. [rappeler, retourner]

**Translation**
1886. A man who knows this, that he will never be taken prisoner,
1887. puts up a great defense in such a battle:
1888. for this reason the Franks are as fierce as lions.
1889. See here Marsile, in the guise of a baron.
1890. He sits on the horse that he calls Gaignun,
1891. spurs it well, and goes to strike Beuve,
1892. who was lord of Beaune and of Dijon.
1893. He breaks his shield and tears apart his hauberk,
1894. so that he strikes him dead without any further undoing;
1895. then he has killed Yvoire and Yvon,
1896. together with them Gérard of Roussillon.
1897. Count Roland is not very far from him;
1898. he said to the pagan: «May the Lord God give you ill!
1899. So very wrongly you kill my companions!
1900. You will have a blow before we part,
1901. and you will know today the name of my sword.»
1902. He goes to strike him in the guise of a baron:
1903. the count has cut off his right fist.
1904. Then he takes the head of Jurfaleu the Blond,
1905. who was son of King Marsile.
1906. The pagans cry out: «Help us, Mahomet!
1907. O our gods, avenge us on Charles!
1908. He has set such villains in this land!
1909. Even on pain of death they will not abandon the field.»
1910. One said to the other: «Eh! let us flee from here!»
1911. At this word a good hundred thousand of them go off:
1912. whoever may recall them, they will never return. AOI.

---

## Laisse CXLIII (lines 1913–1931)

**Original**
1913. De ço qui calt? Se fuit s'en est Marsilies, [chaloir (importer), s'enfuir, être]
1914. Remes i est sis uncles, Marganices, [remaindre (rester), être]
1915. Ki tint Kartagene, Alfrere, Garmalie [tenir]
1916. E Ethiope, une tere maldite.
1917. La neire gent en ad en sa baillie; [avoir]
1918. Granz unt les nes e lees les oreilles, [avoir]
1919. E sunt ensemble plus de cinquante milie. [être]
1920. Icil chevalchent fierement e a íre, [chevaucher]
1921. Puis escrient l'enseigne paenime. [escrier (s'écrier)]
1922. Ço dist Rollant: «Ci recevrums ma[r]tyrie, [dire, recevoir]
1923. E or sai ben n'avons guaires a vivre; [savoir, avoir, vivre]
1924. Mais tut seit fel cher ne se vende primes! [être, vendre]
1925. Ferez, seignurs, des espees furbies, [ferir (frapper)]
1926. Si calengez e voz (e) mors e voz vies! [chalengier (revendiquer)]
1927. Que dulce France par nus ne seit hunie! [être, honnir (déshonorer)]
1928. Quant en cest camp vendrat Carles, mi sire, [venir]
1929. De Sarrazins verrat tel discipline, [voir]
1930. Cuntre un des noz en truverat morz .XV., [trouver]
1931. Ne lesserat que nos ne beneïsse.» AOI. [laisser, bénir]

**Translation**
1913. What does it matter? Marsile has fled,
1914. His uncle Marganice has remained there,
1915. Who held Carthage, Alfrere, Garmalie
1916. And Ethiopia, a cursed land.
1917. He has the black people under his command;
1918. They have large noses and broad ears,
1919. And together they number more than fifty thousand.
1920. These men ride fiercely and with wrath,
1921. Then they shout the pagan battle cry.
1922. Roland said: «Here we shall receive our martyrdom,
1923. And now I know well we have not much longer to live;
1924. But cursed be whoever does not sell himself dearly first!
1925. Strike, lords, with your burnished swords,
1926. And contest both your deaths and your lives!
1927. Let sweet France not be dishonored through us!
1928. When Charles, my lord, comes onto this field,
1929. He will see such slaughter of Saracens,
1930. For one of ours he will find fifteen of them dead,
1931. He will not fail to bless us.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXLIV (lines 1932–1939)

**Original**
1932. Quan Rollant veit la contredite gent [voir]
1933. Ki plus sunt neirs que nen est arrement, [être]
1934. Ne n'unt de blanc ne mais que sul les denz, [avoir]
1935. Ço dist li quens: «Or sai jo veirement [dire, savoir]
1936. Que hoi murrum par le mien escient. [mourir]
1937. Ferez Franceis, car jol vos recumenz!» [ferir (frapper), recommencer]
1938. Dist Oliver: «Dehet ait li plus lenz!» [dire, avoir]
1939. A icest mot Franceis se fierent enz. [ferir (frapper)]

**Translation**
1932. When Roland sees the accursed people,
1933. Who are blacker than ink is,
1934. And have nothing white except only their teeth,
1935. The count said this: «Now I know truly
1936. That today we shall die, to my knowledge.
1937. Strike, Franks, for I begin it again for you!»
1938. Oliver said: «Cursed be the slowest!»
1939. At this word the Franks hurl themselves in.

---

## Laisse CXLV (lines 1940–1951)

**Original**
1940. Quant paien virent que Franceis i out poi, [voir, avoir]
1941. Entr'els en unt e orgoil e cunfort. [avoir]
1942. Dist l'un a l'altre: «L'empereor ad tort.» [dire, avoir]
1943. Li Marganices sist sur un ceval sor, [seoir (être assis)]
1944. Brochet le ben des esperuns a or, [brocher (éperonner)]
1945. Fiert Oliver derere en mi le dos. [ferir (frapper)]
1946. Le blanc osberc li ad descust el cors, [avoir, descoudre (découdre)]
1947. Par mi le piz sun espiet li mist fors, [mettre]
1948. E dit apres: «Un col avez pris fort! [dire, avoir, prendre]
1949. Carles li magnes mar vos laissat as porz! [laisser]
1950. Tort nos ad fait: nen est dreiz qu'il s'en lot, [avoir, faire, être, loer (se féliciter)]
1951. Kar de vos sul ai ben venget les noz.» [avoir, venger]

**Translation**
1940. When the pagans saw that the French were few there,
1941. They take pride and comfort among themselves.
1942. One said to the other: «The emperor is in the wrong.»
1943. The Marganice sat upon a sorrel horse,
1944. He spurs it hard with his spurs of gold,
1945. He strikes Oliver from behind, in the middle of the back.
1946. He has split open the white hauberk against his body,
1947. He thrust his spear out through the middle of his breast,
1948. And said after: «You have taken a hard blow!
1949. Charlemagne left you at the passes to your sorrow!
1950. He has wronged us: it is not right that he should boast of it,
1951. For through you alone I have well avenged our men.»

---

## Laisse CXLVI (lines 1952–1964)

**Original**
1952. Oliver sent que a mort est ferut. [sentir, être, ferir (frapper)]
1953. Tient Halteclere, dunt li acer fut bruns, [tenir, être]
1954. Fiert Marganices sur l'elme a or, agut, [ferir (frapper)]
1955. E flurs (e) e cristaus en acraventet jus; [acraventer (abattre)]
1956. Trenchet la teste d'ici qu'as denz menuz, [trancher]
1957. Brandist sun colp, si l'ad mort abatut, [brandir, avoir, abattre]
1958. E dist apres: «Paien mal aies tu! [dire, avoir]
1959. Iço ne di que Karles n'i ait perdut; [dire, avoir, perdre]
1960. Ne a muiler ne a dame qu'aies veüd, [avoir, voir]
1961. N'en vanteras el regne dunt tu fus [vanter, être]
1962. Vaillant a un dener que m'i aies tolut, [avoir, tolir (enlever)]
1963. Ne fait damage ne de mei ne d'altrui!» [faire]
1964. Apres escriet Rollant qu'il li aiut. AOI. [escrier (s'écrier), aider]

**Translation**
1952. Oliver feels that he is wounded to death.
1953. He grips Halteclere, whose steel was burnished,
1954. He strikes Marganices on the helmet of gold, pointed,
1955. And cuts away its flowers and crystals;
1956. He cleaves the head down to the fine teeth,
1957. He brandishes his blow, and has struck him dead,
1958. And said afterward: «Pagan, a curse upon you!
1959. I do not say that Charles has lost nothing here;
1960. But neither to wife nor to lady whom you have seen
1961. Will you boast, in the realm from which you came,
1962. That you took from me a single denier's worth,
1963. Nor did harm to me or to any other!»
1964. Afterward he cries out to Roland to help him. AOI.

---

## Laisse CXLVII (lines 1965–1977)

**Original**
1965. Oliver sent qu'il est a mort nasfret. [sentir, être, nasfrer (blesser)]
1966. De lui venger ja mais ne li ert lez. [venger, être]
1967. En la grant presse or i fiert cume ber, [ferir (frapper)]
1968. Trenchet cez hanstes e cez escuz buclers, [trancher]
1969. E piez e poinz e seles e costez.
1970. Ki lui veïst Sarrazins desmembrer, [voir, démembrer]
1971. Un mort sur altre geter, [jeter]
1972. De bon vassal li poüst remembrer. [pouvoir, remembrer (se souvenir)]
1973. L'enseigne Carle n'i volt mie ublier: [vouloir, oublier]
1974. «Munjoie!» escriet e haltement e cler. [escrier (s'écrier)]
1975. Rollant apelet, sun ami e sun per: [appeler]
1976. «Sire cumpaign, a mei car vus justez! [joster (rejoindre)]
1977. A grant dulor ermes hoi desevrez.» AOI. [être, dessevrer (séparer)]

**Translation**
1965. Oliver feels that he is wounded to death.
1966. Never will it be pleasant for him to avenge himself.
1967. In the great throng he now strikes like a baron,
1968. He slices through those lances and those bossed shields,
1969. And feet and fists and saddles and sides.
1970. Whoever had seen him dismember the Saracens,
1971. Throw one dead man upon another,
1972. Might well remember a good vassal.
1973. Charles's battle-cry he does not wish to forget:
1974. «Munjoie!» he cries out both loudly and clearly.
1975. He calls Roland, his friend and his peer:
1976. «Lord companion, come now, join me here!
1977. With great sorrow shall we today be parted.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXLVIII (lines 1978–1988)

**Original**
1978. Rollant reguardet Oliver al visage: [regarder]
1979. Teint fut e pers, desculuret e pale. [être]
1980. Li sancs tuz clers par mi le cors li raiet: [raiier (ruisseler)]
1981. Encuntre tere en cheent les esclaces. [cheoir (tomber)]
1982. «Deus!» dist li quens, «or ne sai jo que face. [dire, savoir, faire]
1983. Sire cumpainz, mar fut vostre barnage! [être]
1984. Jamais n'iert hume ki tun cors cuntrevaillet. [être, contrevaloir (égaler)]
1985. E! France dulce, cun hoi remendras guaste [remaindre (rester)]
1986. De bons vassals, cunfundue e chaiete!
1987. Li emperere en avrat grant damage.» [avoir]
1988. A icest mot sur sun cheval se pasmet. AOI. [se pâmer]

**Translation**
1978. Roland looks at Oliver in the face:
1979. It was wan and livid, discolored and pale.
1980. The bright blood streams down across his body:
1981. The clots of it fall to the ground.
1982. «God!» said the count, «now I know not what to do.
1983. Lord companion, ill-fated was your valor!
1984. Never will there be a man who equals your worth.
1985. Ah! sweet France, how laid waste you will remain today,
1986. Bereft of good vassals, ruined and fallen low!
1987. The emperor will have great harm from it.»
1988. At this word, upon his horse he swoons. AOI.

---

## Laisse CXLIX (lines 1989–2009)

**Original**
1989. As vus Rollant sur sun cheval pasmet, [se pâmer]
1990. E Oliver ki est a mort nasfret: [être, nasfrer (blesser)]
1991. Tant ad seinet (ki) li oil li sunt trublet; [avoir, seiner (saigner), être, troubler]
1992. Ne loinz ne pres (es) ne poet vedeir si cler [pouvoir, voir]
1993. Que rec[on]oistre poisset nuls hom mortel. [reconoistre (reconnaître), pouvoir]
1994. Sun cumpaignun, cum il l'at encontret, [avoir, encontrer (rencontrer)]
1995. Sil fiert amunt sur l'elme a or gemet: [ferir (frapper)]
1996. Tut li detrenchet d'ici qu'al nasel; [detrenchier (taillader)]
1997. Mais en la teste ne l'ad mie adeset. [avoir, adeser (toucher)]
1998. A icel colp l'ad Rollant reguardet, [avoir, regarder]
1999. Si li demandet dulcement e suef: [demander]
2000. «Sire cumpain, faites le vos de gred? [faire]
2001. Ja est ço Rollant, ki tant vos soelt amer! [être, soleir (avoir coutume), aimer]
2002. Par nule guise ne m'aviez desfiet!» [avoir, défier]
2003. Dist Oliver: «Or vos oi jo parler; [dire, oïr (entendre), parler]
2004. Jo ne vos vei, veied vus Damnedeu! [voir]
2005. Ferut vos ai, car le me pardunez!» [ferir (frapper), avoir, pardonner]
2006. Rollant respunt: «Jo n'ai nient de mal. [répondre, avoir]
2007. Jol vos parduins ici e devant Deu.» [pardonner]
2008. A icel mot l'un a l'altre ad clinet. [avoir, cliner (incliner)]
2009. Par tel [...] amur as les vus desevred! [dessevrer (séparer)]

**Translation**
1989. There is Roland, fainting upon his horse,
1990. And Oliver, who is wounded to death:
1991. He has bled so much that his eyes are clouded;
1992. Neither far nor near can he see clearly enough
1993. That any mortal man could be recognized.
1994. His companion, as he has encountered him,
1995. He strikes him up high upon the gem-set golden helm:
1996. He cleaves it all the way down to the nasal;
1997. But he has not at all touched the head.
1998. At that blow Roland has looked at him,
1999. And asks him gently and softly:
2000. «Lord companion, do you do this on purpose?
2001. This is indeed Roland, who is so wont to love you!
2002. In no way had you defied me!»
2003. Oliver says: «Now I hear you speak;
2004. I do not see you, may the Lord God see you!
2005. I have struck you, so forgive me for it!»
2006. Roland answers: «I have no hurt at all.
2007. I forgive you for it here and before God.»
2008. At that word the one bowed to the other.
2009. With such love there you see them part!

---

## Laisse CL (lines 2010–2023)

**Original**
2010. Oliver sent que la mort mult l'angoisset. [sentir, angoisser]
2011. Ansdous les oilz en la teste li turnent, [tourner]
2012. L'oíe pert e la veüe tute; [perdre]
2013. Descent a piet, a l[a] tere se culchet, [descendre, se coucher]
2014. Durement en halt si recleimet sa culpe, [réclamer]
2015. Cuntre le ciel ambesdous ses mains juintes, [joindre]
2016. Si priet Deu que pareïs li dunget [prier, donner]
2017. E beneïst Karlun e France dulce, [bénir]
2018. Sun cumpaignun Rollant sur tuz humes.
2019. Falt li le coer, le helme li embrunchet, [faillir, embruncher (pencher)]
2020. Trestut le cors a la tere li justet. [joster (joindre)]
2021. Morz est li quens, que plus ne se demuret. [être, mourir, demeurer]
2022. Rollant li ber le pluret, sil duluset; [pleurer, doluser (se lamenter)]
2023. Jamais en tere n'orrez plus dolent hume! [oïr (entendre)]

**Translation**
2010. Oliver feels that death greatly anguishes him.
2011. Both his eyes turn back in his head,
2012. He loses his hearing and his sight entirely;
2013. He dismounts on foot, lies down on the ground,
2014. Loudly, on high, he confesses his sins,
2015. Toward heaven both his hands joined,
