2666. That he will not desist, for all the gold beneath heaven,
2667. Until [he comes] to Aix, where Charles is accustomed to hold court. <!-- uncertain: 'Que il ainz ad Ais': the verb of motion is elided; ad is the preposition, not avoir -->
2668. His men advise him to it, and have counseled him so.
2669. Then he called two of his knights,
2670. One Clarifan and the other Clarien:
2671. «You are sons of King Maltraien,
2672. Who used to serve as messengers willingly.
2673. I command you to go to Saragossa;
2674. Announce to Marsile on my behalf
2675. That I have come to help him against the Franks.
2676. If I find [them] there, a very great battle will be fought; <!-- uncertain: the word printed 'ó' is unclear; rendered as a reference to the enemy, a guess -->
2677. And so give him this glove, gold-pleated, <!-- uncertain: 'ad or pleiet' is obscure; rendered 'gold-pleated' and treated as an adjectival participle with no verb -->
2678. And have him put it on his right hand.
2679. Carry him this staff of pure gold,
2680. And let him come to me to acknowledge his fief.
2681. I will go to France to wage war on Charles;
2682. If he does not throw himself at my feet, at my mercy,
2683. And does not abandon the law of the Christians,
2684. I will take the crown from his head.»
2685. The pagans answer: «Lord, you speak very well.»

---

## Laisse CXCIV (lines 2686–2704)

**Original**
2686. Dist Baligant: «Car chevalchez, barun! [dire, chevaucher]
2687. L'un port le guant, li alt]r]e le bastun!» [porter]
2688. E cil respundent «Cher sire, si ferum.» [répondre, faire]
2689. Tant chevalcherent que en Sarraguce sunt. [chevaucher, être]
2690. Passent .X. portes, traversent .IIII. punz, [passer, traverser]
2691. Tutes les rues u li burgeis estunt. [ester (se tenir debout)]
2692. Cum il aproisment en la citet amunt, [aproismier (s'approcher)]
2693. Vers le paleis oïrent grant fremur; [oïr (entendre)]
2694. Asez i ad de cele gent paienur, [avoir]
2695. Plurent e crient, demeinent grant dolor, [pleurer, crier, demener (manifester)]
2696. Pleignent lur deus Tervagan e Mahum [plaindre]
2697. E Apollin, dunt il mie n'en unt. [avoir]
2698. Dist cascun a l'altre: «Caitifs, que devendrum? [dire, devenir]
2699. Sur nus est venue male confusiun. [être, venir]
2700. Perdut avum le rei Marsiliun: [avoir, perdre]
2701. Li quens Rollant li trenchat ier le destre poign. [trancher]
2702. Nus n'avum mie de Jurfaleu le Blunt. [avoir]
2703. Trestute Espaigne iert hoi en lur bandun.» [être]
2704. Li dui message descendent al perrun. [descendre]

**Translation**
2686. Baligant said: «Ride now, barons!
2687. Let one carry the glove, the other the staff!»
2688. And they answer: «Dear lord, so we shall.»
2689. They rode until they are in Saragossa.
2690. They pass ten gates, they cross four bridges,
2691. And all the streets where the townsmen stand.
2692. As they draw near in the upper city,
2693. Toward the palace they heard a great din;
2694. There are many of that pagan people there,
2695. Weeping and crying out, showing great grief,
2696. Lamenting their gods Tervagan and Mahomet
2697. And Apollo, from whom they have nothing. <!-- uncertain: dunt il mie n'en unt: 'from whom they have nothing' is a literal rendering; the object left unexpressed (help?) is not certain -->
2698. Each says to the other: «Wretches, what will become of us?
2699. A wicked confusion has come upon us.
2700. We have lost King Marsile:
2701. Count Roland cut off his right hand yesterday.
2702. We no longer have Jurfaleu the Blond at all.
2703. All Spain will today be in their power.»
2704. The two messengers dismount at the mounting block.

---

## Laisse CXCV (lines 2705–2723)

**Original**
2705. Lur chevals laisent dedesuz un olive: [laisser]
2706. Dui Sarrazin par les resnes les pristrent. [prendre]
2707. E li message par les mantels se tindrent, [se tenir]
2708. Puis sunt muntez sus el paleis altisme. [être, monter]
2709. Cum il entrent en la cambre voltice, [entrer]
2710. Par bel amur malvais saluz li firent: [faire]
2711. «Cil Mahumet ki nus ad en baillie, [avoir]
2712. E Tervagan e Apollin, nostre sire,
2713. Sálvent le rei e guardent la reïne!» [sauver, garder]
2714. Dist Bramimunde: «Or oi mult grant folie! [dire, oïr (entendre)]
2715. Cist nostre deu sunt en recreantise. [être]
2716. En Rencesval m[al]vaises vertuz firent: [faire]
2717. Noz chevalers i unt lesset ocire; [avoir, laisser, ocire (tuer)]
2718. Cest mien seignur en bataille faillirent; [faillir]
2719. Le destre poign ad perdut, n'en ad mie, [avoir, perdre]
2720. Si li trenchat li quens Rollant, li riches. [trancher]
2721. Trestute Espaigne avrat Carles en baillie. [avoir]
2722. Que devendrai, duluruse, caitive? [devenir]
2723. E! lasse, que n'en ai un hume ki m'ociet!» AOI. [avoir, ocire (tuer)]

**Translation**
2705. They leave their horses beneath an olive tree:
2706. Two Saracens took them by the reins.
2707. And the messengers held on to each other by the mantles,
2708. Then they went up into the very lofty palace.
2709. As they enter the vaulted chamber,
2710. With a show of fair friendship they gave a wicked greeting:
2711. «May that Mohammed who has us in his keeping,
2712. And Tervagan and Apollo, our lord,
2713. Save the king and guard the queen!»
2714. Bramimonde said: «Now I hear very great folly! <!-- uncertain: 'oi' read as 1st-person 'I hear'; it could conceivably be another word -->
2715. These gods of ours are in cowardice.
2716. At Roncevaux they wrought evil miracles:
2717. They have let our knights be killed there;
2718. They failed this lord of mine in battle;
2719. He has lost his right hand, he has none,
2720. For Count Roland, the mighty, cut it off for him.
2721. Charles will have all Spain in his keeping.
2722. What will become of me, sorrowful, wretched?
2723. Alas, that I have no man to kill me!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXCVI (lines 2724–2740)

**Original**
2724. Dist Clarien «Dame, ne parlez mie itant! [dire, parler]
2725. Messages sumes al paien Baligant. [être]
2726. Marsiliun, ço dit, serat guarant, [dire, être]
2727. Si l'en enveiet sun bastun e sun guant. [envoyer]
2728. En Sebre avum .IIII. milie calant, [avoir]
2729. Eschiez e barges e galees curant; [courir]
2730. Drodmunz i ad ne vos sai dire quanz. [avoir, savoir, dire]
2731. Li amiralz est riches e puisant: [être]
2732. En France irat Carlemagne querant; [aller, querre (chercher)]
2733. Rendre le quidet u mort ó recreant.» [rendre, cuidier (croire)]
2734. Dist Bramimunde «Mar en irat itant! [dire, aller]
2735. Plus pres d'ici purrez truver les Francs. [pouvoir, trouver]
2736. En ceste tere ad estet ja .VII. anz. [avoir, être]
2737. Li emperere est ber e cumbatant: [être]
2738. Meilz voel murir que ja fuiet de camp; [vouloir, mourir, fuir]
2739. Suz ciel n'ad rei qu'il prist a un enfant. [avoir, priser (estimer)]
2740. Carles ne creint nuls hom ki seit vivant.» [craindre, être]

**Translation**
2724. Clarien said: «Lady, do not speak so much!
2725. We are messengers of the pagan Baligant.
2726. Marsile, he says, will have a protector
2727. If he sends him his staff and his glove.
2728. On the Ebro we have four thousand barges,
2729. Skiffs and barges and galleys under way;
2730. There are dromonds there, I cannot tell you how many.
2731. The emir is mighty and powerful:
2732. He will go to France seeking Charlemagne;
2733. He thinks he will make him either dead or craven.» <!-- uncertain: 'ó' read as 'ou' (or); 'rendre' read as 'make him' (dead or craven) -->
2734. Bramimonde said: «To his cost will he go so far!
2735. Nearer here you can find the Franks.
2736. In this land he has been already seven years.
2737. The emperor is brave and a fighter:
2738. He would rather die than ever flee from the field; <!-- uncertain: 'voel' printed without -t; read as 3rd person (Charles), though a 1st-person reading is conceivable -->
2739. Beneath heaven there is no king he would rate above a child. <!-- uncertain: 'prist' read as subjunctive of priser (esteem); the sense 'ranks no king above a child' is not certain, and 'prist' could be taken from prendre -->
2740. Charles fears no man who is living.»

---

## Laisse CXCVII (lines 2741–2754)

**Original**
2741. - «Laissez ço ester!« dist Marsilies li reis. [laisser, ester (rester), dire]
2742. Dist as messages: «Seignurs, parlez a mei! [dire, parler]
2743. Ja veez vos que a mort sui destreit, [voir, être, destreindre (accabler)]
2744. Jo si nen ai filz ne fille ne heir: [avoir]
2745. Un en aveie, cil fut ocis her seir. [avoir, être, ocire (tuer)]
2746. Mun seignur dites qu'il me vienge veeir. [dire, venir, voir]
2747. Li amiraill ad en Espaigne dreit: [avoir]
2748. Quite li cleim, se il la voelt aveir, [clamer (proclamer), vouloir, avoir]
2749. Puis la defendet encuntre li Franceis! [défendre]
2750. Vers Carlemagne li durrai bon conseill: [donner]
2751. Cunquis l'avrat d'oi cest jur en un meis. [avoir, conquérir]
2752. De Sarraguce les clefs li portereiz; [porter]
2753. Pui li dites, il n'en irat, s'il me creit.» [dire, s'en aller, croire]
2754. Cil respundent: «Sire, vus dites veir.» AOI. [répondre, dire]

**Translation**
2741. «Let that be!» said King Marsile.
2742. He said to the messengers: «Lords, speak to me!
2743. You see now that I am hard pressed to death,
2744. For I have neither son nor daughter nor heir:
2745. I had one, and he was killed yesterday evening.
2746. Tell my lord to come and see me.
2747. The emir has a right to Spain:
2748. I declare it free to him, if he wishes to have it,
2749. Then let him defend it against the French!
2750. Against Charlemagne I will give him good counsel:
2751. Within a month from this day he will have conquered him. <!-- uncertain: l' read as Charles; it could be Spain -->
2752. You will carry him the keys of Saragossa;
2753. Then tell him he will not go away, if he believes me.» <!-- uncertain: who 'il' is and what he will not leave or go from; read as Charles not going away -->
2754. They answer: «Lord, you speak truly.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CXCVIII (lines 2755–2764)

**Original**
2755. Ço dist Marsilie: «Carles l'emperere [dire]
2756. Mort m'ad mes homes, ma tere deguastee, [avoir, mourir, guaster (dévaster)]
2757. E mes citez fraites e violees. [fraindre (briser), violer]
2758. Il jut anuit sur cel ewe de Sebre: [gésir (être couché)]
2759. Jo ai cunte n'i ad mais que .VII. liwes. [avoir, compter] <!-- spelling: cunte read as the noun 'reckoning' (j'ai compte), not as a participle -->
2760. L'amirail dites que sun host i amein. [dire, amener]
2761. Par vos li mand bataille i seit justee.» [mander, être, joster (joindre)]
2762. De Sarraguce les clefs li ad livrees. [avoir, livrer]
2763. Li messager ambedui l'enclinerent, [cliner (incliner)]
2764. Prenent cu(i)[n]get, a cel mot s'en turnerent. [prendre, s'en aller]

**Translation**
2755. Marsile said: «Charles the emperor
2756. Has killed my men, laid my land waste,
2757. And broken and violated my cities.
2758. He lay tonight on the bank of the Ebro:
2759. I reckon there are no more than seven leagues.
2760. Tell the emir to bring his host there;
2761. Through you I send word that battle is to be joined there.»
2762. He has handed over to him the keys of Saragossa.
2763. The two messengers both bowed to him,
2764. They take leave, at that word they went away.

---

## Laisse CXCIX (lines 2765–2789)

**Original**
2765. Li dui message es chevals sunt muntet. [être, monter]
2766. Isnelement issent de la citet, [issir (sortir)]
2767. A l'amiraill en vunt esfreedement; [s'en aller]
2768. De Sarra[gu]ce li presentent les cles. [présenter]
2769. Dist Baligant: «Que avez vos truvet? [dire, avoir, trouver]
2770. U est Marsilie, que jo aveie mandet?« [être, avoir, mander]
2771. Dist Clarïen: «Il est a mort nasfret. [dire, être, nasfrer (blesser)]
2772. Li emperere fut ier as porz passer, [être, passer]
2773. Si s'en vuolt en dulce France aler. [vouloir, s'en aller]
2774. Par grant honur se fist rereguarder: [faire, rereguarder (garder l'arrière)]
2775. Li quens Rollant i fut remes, sis nies, [être, remaindre (rester)]
2776. E Oliver e tuit li .XII. per,
2777. De cels de France .XX. milie adubez. [aduber (équiper)]
2778. Li reis Marsilie s'i cumbatit, li bers. [combattre]
2779. Il e Rollant el camp furent remes: [être, remaindre (rester)]
2780. De Durendal li dunat un colp tel [donner]
2781. Le destre poign li ad del cors sevret; [avoir, sevrer (séparer)]
2782. Sun filz ad mort, qu'il tant suleit amer, [avoir, mourir, soleir (avoir coutume), aimer]
2783. E li baron qu'il i out amenet. [avoir, amener]
2784. Fuiant s'en vint, qu'il n'i pout mes ester. [fuir, venir, pouvoir, ester (rester)]
2785. Li emperere l'ad enchalcet asez. [avoir, enchacier (pourchasser)]
2786. Li reis mandet que vos le sucurez. [mander, secourir]
2787. Quite vus cleimet d'Espaigne le regnet.» [clamer (proclamer)]
2788. E Baligant cumencet a penser; [commencer, penser]
2789. Si grant doel ad por poi qu'il n'est desvet. AOI. [avoir, être, desver (devenir fou)]

**Translation**
2765. The two messengers are mounted on horses.
2766. Swiftly they leave the city,
2767. They go off in fear to the emir;
2768. They present to him the keys of Saragossa.
2769. Baligant said: «What have you found?
2770. Where is Marsile, whom I had summoned?»
2771. Clarien said: «He is wounded to death.
2772. The emperor was yesterday crossing the passes,
2773. He wanted to go away to sweet France.
2774. In great honor he had a rearguard set behind him:
2775. Count Roland was left there, his nephew,
2776. And Oliver and all the twelve peers,
2777. Twenty thousand of those of France, armed.
2778. King Marsile fought there, the brave man.
2779. He and Roland were left on the field:
2780. With Durendal he gave him such a blow
2781. That he has severed his right hand from his body;
2782. His son is dead, whom he so loved,
2783. And the barons he had brought there.
2784. He came away fleeing, for he could stay there no longer.
2785. The emperor has pursued him hard.
2786. The king sends word that you are to help him.
2787. He declares the kingdom of Spain quit to you.»
2788. And Baligant begins to ponder;
2789. He has such grief that he is nearly mad. AOI.

---

## Laisse CC (lines 2790–2809)

**Original**
2790. «Sire amiralz,» dist Clarïens, [dire]
2791. «En Rencesvals une bataille out íer. [avoir]
2792. Morz est Rollant e li quens Oliver, [être, mourir]
2793. Li .XII. per, que Carles aveit tant cher; [avoir]
2794. De lur Franceis i ad mort .XX. millers. [avoir, mourir]
2795. Li reis Marsilie le destre poign i perdit, [perdre]
2796. E l'emperere asez l'ad enchalcet, [avoir, enchacier (pourchasser)]
2797. En ceste tere n'est remes chevaler [être, remaindre (rester)]
2798. Ne seit ocis o en Sebre neiet. [être, ocire (tuer), neier (noyer)]
2799. Desur la rive sunt Frances herbergiez: [être, se herberger (camper)]
2800. En cest païs nus sunt tant aproeciez, [être, approcher]
2801. Se vos volez, li repaires ert grefs.» [vouloir, être]
2802. E Baligant le reguart en ad fiers, [avoir]
2803. En sun curage en est joüs e liet. [être, joïr (se réjouir)]
2804. Del faldestod se redrecet en piez, [redrecer (redresser)]
2805. Puis escriet: «Baruns, ne vos targez! [escrier (s'écrier), targier (tarder)]
2806. Eissez des nefs, muntez, si cevalciez! [issir (sortir), monter, chevaucher]
2807. S'or ne s'en fuit Karlemagne li veilz, [s'enfuir]
2808. Li reis Marsilie enqui serat venget: [être, venger]
2809. Pur sun poign destre l'en liverai le che(s)[f].» [livrer]

**Translation**
2790. «Lord emir,» said Clarien,
2791. «At Roncesvals there was a battle yesterday.
2792. Roland is dead, and Count Oliver,
2793. The twelve peers, whom Charles held so dear;
2794. Of their Franks twenty thousand lie dead.
2795. King Marsile lost his right hand there,
2796. And the emperor has pursued him hard.
2797. In this land there is no knight left
2798. Who is not killed or drowned in the Ebro.
2799. The French are camped on the riverbank:
2800. They have drawn so near us in this country
2801. That, if you wish, their return will be hard.»
2802. And Baligant has a fierce look at it,
2803. In his heart he is glad and joyful about it.
2804. He rises to his feet from the folding throne,
2805. Then cries out: «Barons, do not delay!
2806. Come out of the ships, mount, and ride!
2807. If Charlemagne the old does not flee now,
2808. King Marsile will be avenged here:
2809. For his right hand I will deliver him the head.»

---

## Laisse CCI (lines 2810–2826)

**Original**
2810. Paien d'Arabe des nefs se sunt eissut, [être, issir (sortir)]
2811. Puis sunt muntez es chevals e es muls; [être, monter]
2812. Si chevalcherent, que fereient il plus? [chevaucher, faire]
2813. Li amiralz, ki trestuz les esmut, [esmovoir (mettre en mouvement)]
2814. Sin apelet Gemalfin, un sun drut: [appeler]
2815. «Jo te cumant de tutes mes oz l'aunde.» [comander (confier)]
2816. Puis en un sun destrer brun est munte; [être, monter]
2817. Ensembl'od lui emmeinet .IIII. dux. [emmener]
2818. Tant chevalchat qu'en Sarraguce fut. [chevaucher, être]
2819. A un perron de marbre est descenduz, [être, descendre]
2820. E quatre cuntes l'estreu li unt tenut. [avoir, tenir]
2821. Par les degrez el paleis muntet sus, [monter]
2822. E Bramidonie vient curant cuntre lui; [venir, courir]
2823. Si li ad dit: «Dolente, si mare fui! [avoir, dire, être]
2824. A itel hunte, sire, mon seignor ai perdut!» [avoir, perdre]
2825. Chet li as piez, li amiralz la reçut; [cheoir (tomber), recevoir]
2826. Sus en la chambre ad doel en sunt venut. AOI. [être, venir] <!-- spelling: ad doel read as preposition plus noun, 'in grief', not as a form of avoir -->

**Translation**
2810. The pagans of Arabia have come out of the ships,
2811. Then have mounted on horses and on mules;
2812. And they rode on; what more would they do?
2813. The emir, who set them all in motion,
2814. Calls Gemalfin, one of his favorites:
2815. «I entrust to you the command of all my hosts.» <!-- uncertain: l'aunde is obscure; read as 'command, charge' -->
2816. Then he mounts a brown war-horse of his own;
2817. Together with him he takes four dukes.
2818. He rode so far that he was in Saragossa.
2819. He dismounted at a marble block,
2820. And four counts held the stirrup for him.
2821. Up the steps into the palace he goes,
2822. And Bramimonde comes running toward him;
2823. She has said to him: «Wretched woman, in an ill hour I was born!
2824. In such shame, lord, I have lost my lord!»
2825. She falls at his feet, the emir received her;
2826. Up in the chamber, in grief, they have come. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCII (lines 2827–2844)

**Original**
2827. Li reis Marsilie, cum il veit Baligant, [voir]
2828. Dunc apelat dui Sarrazin espans: [appeler]
2829. «Pernez m'as braz, sim(e) drecez en seant.» [prendre, dresser]
2830. Al puign senestre ad pris un de ses guanz. [avoir, prendre]
2831. Ço dist Marsilie: «Sire reis, amiralz, [dire]
2832. Teres tutes ici [...] rengnes vos rendemas [rendre]
2833. E Sarraguce e l'onur qu'i apent. [apendre (appartenir)]
2834. Mei ai perdut e tute ma gent.» [avoir, perdre]
2835. E cil respunt: «Tant sy jo plus dolent. [répondre, être]
2836. Ne pois a vos tenir lung parlement: [pouvoir, tenir]
2837. Jo sai asez que Carles ne m'atent, [savoir, attendre]
2838. E nepurquant de vos receif le guant.» [recevoir]
2839. Al doel qu'il ad s'en est turnet plurant. AOI. [avoir, s'en aller, pleurer]
2840. Par les degrez jus del paleis descent, [descendre]
2841. Muntet el ceval, vient a sa gent puignant. [monter, venir, poindre (éperonner)]
2842. Tant chevalchat, qu'il est premers devant, [chevaucher, être]
2843. De uns ad altres si se vait escriant: [aller, escrier (s'écrier)]
2844. «Venez paien, car ja s'en fuient Frant!» AOI. [venir, s'enfuir]

**Translation**
2827. King Marsile, when he sees Baligant,
2828. Then called two Spanish Saracens:
2829. «Take me by the arms and raise me to sit up.»
2830. In his left fist he took one of his gloves.
2831. Marsile said: «Lord king, emir,
2832. All the lands here [...] realms I surrender to you <!-- uncertain: the verse has a gap [...] and rendemas is obscure; read as a form of rendre, 'I surrender' -->
2833. And Saragossa and the honor that belongs to it.
2834. I have lost myself and all my people.»
2835. And he answers: «So much the more grieved am I.
2836. I cannot hold a long conversation with you:
2837. I know well that Charles does not wait for me,
2838. And nevertheless I receive the glove from you.»
2839. In his grief he went away weeping. AOI.
2840. Down the steps, down from the palace he goes,
2841. Mounts the horse, comes to his people at a gallop.
2842. He rode so far that he is first in front,
2843. From one to another he goes crying out:
2844. «Come, pagans, for the Franks are already fleeing!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCIII (lines 2845–2854)

**Original**
2845. Al matin, quant primes pert li albe, [apparoir (apparaître)]
2846. Esveillez est li e[m]perere Carles. [être, s'éveiller]
2847. Sein Gabriel, ki de part Deu le guarde, [garder]
2848. Levet sa main, sur lui fait sun signacle. [lever, faire]
2849. Li reis descent, si ad rendut ses armes, [descendre, avoir, rendre]
2850. Si se desarment par tute l'ost li altre. [se désarmer]
2851. Puis sunt muntet, par grant vertut chevalchent [être, monter, chevaucher]
2852. Cez veiez lunges e cez chemins mult larges,
2853. Si vunt ve[d]eir le merveillus damage [aller, voir]
2854. En Rencesvals, la ó fut la bataille. AOI. [être]

**Translation**
2845. In the morning, when dawn first appears,
2846. The emperor Charles is awake.
2847. Saint Gabriel, who guards him on God's behalf,
2848. Raises his hand, makes his sign over him.
2849. The king dismounts, and has handed over his arms,
2850. And the others throughout the host disarm.
2851. Then they are mounted, with great force they ride
2852. Those long roads and those very wide paths,
2853. And go to see the terrible damage
2854. At Roncesvals, there where the battle was. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCIV (lines 2855–2869)

**Original**
2855. En Rencesvals en est Carles venuz. [être, venir]
2856. Des morz qu'il troevet cumencet a plurer. [trouver, commencer, pleurer]
2857. Dist a Franceis: «Segnu[r]s, le pas tenez; [dire, tenir]
2858. Kar mei meïsme estoet avant aler [estovoir (falloir), aller]
2859. Pur mun nev[ol]d que vuldreie truver. [vouloir, trouver]
2860. A Eis esteie, a une féste anoel: [être]
2861. Si se vant(t)[er]ent mi vaillant chevaler [se vanter]
2862. De granz batailles, de forz esturs pleners.
2863. D'une raisun oï Rollant parler: [oïr (entendre), parler]
2864. Ja ne (ne) murreit en estrange regnet [mourir]
2865. Ne trespassast ses hume[s] e ses pers; [trespasser (dépasser)]
2866. Vers lur païs avreit sun chef turnet; [avoir, tourner]
2867. Cunquerrantment si finereit li bers.» [finer (finir)]
2868. Plus qu'en ne poet un bastuncel jeter, [pouvoir, jeter]
2869. Devant les altres est en un pui muntet. [être, monter]

**Translation**
2855. Charles has come to Roncesvals.
2856. At the dead he finds there he begins to weep.
2857. He said to the French: «Lords, hold the pace;
2858. For I myself must go on ahead
2859. To find my nephew, whom I would wish to find.
2860. I was at Aix, at an annual feast:
2861. There my valiant knights boasted
2862. Of great battles, of fierce full-scale fights.
2863. I heard Roland say one thing:
2864. He would never die in a foreign realm
2865. Without having gone beyond his men and his peers;
2866. He would have turned his head toward their country;
2867. Conquering he would end, the brave man.»
2868. Farther than one can throw a stick,
2869. In front of the others he has climbed onto a hill.

---

## Laisse CCV (lines 2870–2880)

**Original**
2870. Quant l'empereres vait querre sun nevold, [aller, querre (chercher)]
2871. De tantes herbes el pre truvat les flors, [trouver]
2872. Ki sunt vermeilz del sanc de noz barons! [être]
2873. Pitet en ad, ne poet muer n'en plurt. [avoir, pouvoir, muer (s'empêcher), pleurer]
2874. Desuz dous arbres parvenuz est [...] li reis. [être, parvenir]
2875. Les colps Rollant conut en treis per(r)uns, [connaître]
2876. Sur l'erbe verte veit gesir sun nevuld; [voir, gésir (être couché)]
2877. Nen est merveille se Karles ad irur. [être, avoir]
2878. Descent a pied, aled i est pleins curs, [descendre, être, aller]
2879. Entre ses mains ansdous prent le priest suus; [prendre]
2880. Sur lui se pasmet, tant par est anguissus. [se pâmer, être]

**Translation**
2870. When the emperor goes to look for his nephew,
2871. Of so many herbs he found the flowers in the meadow,
2872. Which are red with the blood of our barons!
2873. He has pity for it, cannot keep from weeping.
2874. Beneath two trees the king has arrived [...]. <!-- uncertain: the verse has a gap [...] -->
2875. He recognizes Roland's blows on three boulders,
2876. He sees his nephew lying on the green grass;
2877. It is no wonder if Charles feels anger.
2878. He dismounts, has gone there at full speed,
2879. Between both his hands he takes his own closest one; <!-- uncertain: le priest suus is obscure; read as 'the nearest of his own' -->
2880. He swoons over him, so greatly is he anguished.

---

## Laisse CCVI (lines 2881–2891)

**Original**
2881. Li empereres de pasmeisuns revint. [revenir]
2882. Naimes li dux e li quens Acelin,
2883. Gefrei d'Anjou e sun frere Henri
2884. Prenent le rei, sil drecent suz un pin. [prendre, dresser]
2885. Guardet a la tere, veit sun nevold gesir. [garder (regarder), voir, gésir (être couché)]
2886. Tant dulcement a regreter le prist: [regretter, prendre]
2887. «Amis Rollant, de tei ait Deus mercit! [avoir]
2888. Unques nuls hom tel chevaler ne vit [voir]
2889. Por granz batailles juster e defenir. [jouster (combattre), defenir (achever)]
2890. La meie honor est turnet en declin.» [être, tourner]
2891. Carles se pasmet, ne s'en pout astenir. AOI. [se pâmer, pouvoir, s'abstenir]

**Translation**
2881. The emperor came back from his swoons.
2882. Duke Naimes and Count Acelin,
2883. Geoffrey of Anjou and his brother Henry
2884. Take the king and raise him up beneath a pine.
2885. He looks at the ground, sees his nephew lying there.
2886. Very tenderly he began to lament him:
2887. «Dear Roland, may God have mercy on you!
2888. Never did any man see such a knight
2889. For fighting in great battles and bringing them to an end.
2890. My honor has turned to decline.»
2891. Charles swoons; he could not hold himself back. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCVII (lines 2892–2908)

**Original**
2892. Carles li reis se vint de pasmeisuns; [venir]
2893. Par mains le tienent .III. de ses barons. [tenir]
2894. Guardet a tere, veit gesir sun nev[u]ld: [garder (regarder), voir, gésir (être couché)]
2895. Cors ad gaillard, perdue ad sa culur, [avoir, perdre]
2896. Turnez ses oilz, mult li sunt tenebros. [tourner, être]
2897. Carles le pleint par feid e par amur: [plaindre]
2898. «Ami Rollant, Deus metet t'anme en flors, [mettre]
2899. En pareïs, entre les glorius!
2900. Cum en Espaigne venis [a] mal seignur! [venir]
2901. Jamais n'ert jurn que de tei n'aie dulur. [être, avoir]
2902. Cum decarrat ma force e ma baldur! [decheoir (déchoir)]
2903. N'en avrai ja ki sustienget m'onur; [avoir, soutenir]
2904. Suz ciel ne quid aveir ami un sul! [cuidier (croire), avoir]
2905. Se jo ai parenz, n'en i ad nul si proz.» [avoir]
2906. Trait ses crignels, pleines ses mains amsdous, [traire (tirer)]
2907. Cent milie Franc en unt si grant dulur [avoir]
2908. N'en i ad cel ki durement ne plurt. AOI. [avoir, pleurer]

**Translation**
2892. Charles the king came round from his swoons;
2893. Three of his barons hold him by the hands.
2894. He looks at the ground, sees his nephew lying there:
2895. His body is vigorous, but he has lost his color,
2896. His eyes are rolled back, very dark to him now.
2897. Charles laments him with faith and with love:
2898. «Dear Roland, may God set your soul among flowers,
2899. In paradise, among the glorious!
2900. How you came to Spain to an unlucky lord! <!-- uncertain: "venis [a] mal seignur" has an editorial [a]; read as "came to Spain (serving) an unlucky lord" -->
2901. There will never be a day when I do not feel grief for you.
2902. How my strength and my vigor will fail!
2903. I will never have anyone to uphold my honor;
2904. Under heaven I do not think I have a single friend!
2905. If I have kinsmen, there is not one so valiant.»
2906. He pulls his hair, both hands full of it,
2907. A hundred thousand Franks feel such great grief over it
2908. That there is not one of them who does not weep bitterly. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCVIII (lines 2909–2915)

**Original**
2909. «Ami Rollant, jo m'en irai en France. [s'en aller]
2910. Cum jo serai a Loün, en ma chambre, [être]
2911. De plusurs regnes vendrunt li hume estrange; [venir]
2912. Demanderunt: «U est quens cataignes?» [demander, être]
2913. Jo lur dirrai qu'il est morz en Espaigne. [dire, être, mourir]
2914. A grant dulur tendrai puis mun reialme: [tenir]
2915. Jamais n'ert jur que ne plur ne n'en pleigne.» [être, pleurer, plaindre]

**Translation**
2909. «Dear Roland, I will go away to France.
2910. When I am at Laon, in my chamber,
2911. Men from many foreign kingdoms will come;
2912. They will ask: «Where is the captain count?»
2913. I will tell them that he is dead in Spain.
2914. In great grief I will hold my kingdom thereafter:
2915. There will never be a day when I do not weep and lament.»

---

## Laisse CCIX (lines 2916–2932)

**Original**
2916. - «Ami Rollant, prozdoem, juvente bele,
2917. Cum jo serai a Eis, em ma chapele, [être]
2918. Vendrunt li hume, demanderunt noveles; [venir, demander]
2919. Jes lur dirrai, merveilluses e pesmes: [dire]
2920. «Morz est mis nies, ki tant me fist cunquere.» [être, mourir, faire, conquérir]
2921. Encuntre mei revelerunt li Seisne, [reveler (se révolter)] <!-- spelling: revelerunt is OF reveler < rebellare "rebel", not modern révéler -->
2922. E Hungre e Bugre e tante gent averse,
2923. Romain, Puillain e tuit icil de Palerne
2924. E cil d'Affrike e cil de Califerne;
2925. Puis entrerunt mes peines e mes suffraites. [entrer]
2926. Ki guierat mes oz a tel poeste, [guier (guider)]
2927. Quant cil est [morz] ki tuz jurz nos cadelet? [être, mourir, cadeler (commander)]
2928. E! France, cum remeines deserte! [remaindre (rester)]
2929. Si grant doel ai que jo ne vuldreie estre!» [avoir, vouloir, être]
2930. Sa barbe blanche cumencet a detraire, [commencer, detraire (arracher)] <!-- spelling: detraire < detrahere "tear", distinct from estraire -->
2931. Ad ambes mains les chevels de sa teste.
2932. Cent milie Francs s'en pasment cuntre tere. [se pâmer]

**Translation**
2916. - «Dear Roland, worthy man, fair youth,
2917. When I am at Aix, in my chapel,
2918. The men will come and will ask for news;
2919. I will tell it to them, marvelous and dire:
2920. «Dead is my nephew, who made me conquer so much.»
2921. Against me the Saxons will rise in revolt,
2922. And Hungarians and Bulgars and many hostile peoples,
2923. Romans, Apulians and all those of Palermo
2924. And those of Africa and those of Califerne;
2925. Then my pains and my sufferings will begin.
2926. Who will lead my armies with such power,
2927. When he is dead who always commands us?
2928. Ah, France, how deserted you remain!
2929. I have such great grief that I would not wish to be!»
2930. He begins to tear his white beard,
2931. With both hands the hair of his head.
2932. A hundred thousand Franks swoon to the ground over it.

---

## Laisse CCX (lines 2933–2944)

**Original**
2933. «Ami Rollant, de tei ait Deus mercit! [avoir]
2934. L'anme de tei seit mise en pareïs! [être, mettre]
2935. Ki tei ad mort France ad mis en exill. [avoir, mourir, mettre] <!-- spelling: mort = "killed" (transitive), as at 1683 -->
2936. Si grant dol ai que ne voldreie vivre, [avoir, vouloir, vivre]
2937. De ma maisnee, ki por mei est ocise! [être, ocire (tuer)]
2938. Ço duinset Deus, le filz sainte Marie, [donner]
2939. Einz que jo vienge as maistres porz de Sirie, [venir]
2940. L'anme del cors me seit oi departie, [être, départir (se séparer)]
2941. Entre les lur aluee e mise, [aloer (placer), mettre] <!-- spelling: aluee is aloer < allocare "assign", distinct from luer (payer) < locare -->
2942. E ma car fust delez els enfuïe!» [être, enfouir]
2943. Ploret des oilz, sa blanche bar[b]e tiret. [pleurer, tirer]
2944. E dist dux Naimes: «Or ad Carles grant ire.» AOI. [dire, avoir]

**Translation**
2933. «Dear Roland, may God have mercy on you!
2934. May your soul be placed in paradise!
2935. Whoever has killed you has put France into exile.
2936. I have such great grief that I would not wish to live,
2937. For my household, who are slain for my sake!
2938. May God, the son of holy Mary, grant this:
2939. Before I come to the main passes of Syria,
2940. May my soul be parted from my body today,
2941. Allotted and placed among theirs, <!-- uncertain: "aluee" read as aloer "assign, place" -->
2942. And may my flesh be buried beside them!»
2943. He weeps from his eyes, he pulls at his white beard.
2944. And Duke Naimes says: «Now Charles has great grief.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXI (lines 2945–2950)

**Original**
2945. - «Sire emperere,» ço dist Gefrei d'Anjou, [dire]
2946. «Ceste dolor ne demenez tant fort! [demener (manifester)]
2947. Par tut le camp faites querre les noz, [faire, querre (chercher)]
2948. Que cil d'Espaigne en la bataille unt mort; [avoir, mourir]
2949. En un carne(l)[r] cumandez que hom les port.» [commander, porter]
2950. Ço dist li reis: «Sunez en vostre corn!» AOI. [dire, sonner]

**Translation**
2945. - «Lord emperor,» said Geoffrey of Anjou,
2946. «Do not show this grief so strongly!
2947. Have our men sought out across the whole field,
2948. Those whom the Spaniards killed in the battle;
2949. Order that they be carried to a charnel house.»
2950. The king said: «Sound your horn!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXII (lines 2951–2961)

**Original**
2951. Gefreid d'Anjou ad sun greisle sunet. [avoir, sonner]
2952. Franceis descendent, Carles l'ad comandet. [descendre, avoir, commander]
2953. Tuz lur amis qu'il i unt morz truvet, [avoir, trouver]
2954. Ad un carne(l)[r] sempres les unt portet. [avoir, porter]
2955. Asez i ad evesques e abez, [avoir]
2956. Munies, canonies, proveires coronez:
2957. Si sunt asols e seignez de part Deu. [être, absoudre, seignier (bénir)]
2958. Mirre e timoine i firent alumer, [faire, allumer]
2959. Gaillardement tuz les unt encensez; [avoir, encenser]
2960. A grant honor pois les unt enterrez. [avoir, enterrer]
2961. Sis unt laisez: qu'en fereient il el? AOI. [avoir, laisser, faire]

**Translation**
2951. Geoffrey of Anjou has sounded his trumpet.
2952. The Franks dismount, Charles has ordered it.
2953. All their friends whom they found dead there,
2954. At once they have carried them to a charnel house.
2955. There are many bishops and abbots there,
2956. Monks, canons, tonsured priests:
2957. They are absolved and blessed in God's name.
2958. They had myrrh and incense lit there,
2959. Vigorously they have censed them all;
2960. Then with great honor they have buried them.
2961. They left them: what else would they do? AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXIII (lines 2962–2973)

**Original**
2962. Li emperere fait Rollant costeïr [faire, costeïr (préparer)]
2963. E Oliver e (e) l'arcevesque Turpin,
2964. Devant sei les ad fait tuz uvrir [avoir, faire, ouvrir]
2965. E tuz les quers en paile recuillir: [recueillir]
2966. Un blanc sarcou de marbre sunt enz mis; [être, mettre]
2967. E puis les cors des barons si unt pris, [avoir, prendre]
2968. En quirs de cerf les seignurs unt mis: [avoir, mettre]
2969. Ben sunt lavez de piment e de vin. [être, laver]
2970. Li reis cumandet Tedbalt e Gebuin, [commander]
2971. Milun le cunte e Otes le marchis:
2972. «En .III. carettes les guiez [...] tres ben.» [guier (guider)]
2973. Bien sunt cuverz d'un palie galazin. AOI. [être, couvrir]

**Translation**
2962. The emperor has Roland prepared <!-- uncertain: costeïr is obscure; read as "make ready, lay out" -->
2963. And Oliver and the archbishop Turpin,
2964. In front of himself he has had them all opened
2965. And all the hearts gathered in a silk cloth:
2966. A white marble sarcophagus, they are put inside;
2967. And then they took the bodies of the barons,
2968. In stag hides they put the lords:
2969. They are well washed with spiced wine and with wine.
2970. The king commands Thibaut and Gebuin,
2971. Count Milon and Eudes the marquis:
2972. «In three carts guide them [...] very well.»
2973. They are well covered with a Galatian silk cloth. AOI. <!-- uncertain: galazin read as "of Galatia"; the cloth's origin is not certain -->

---

## Laisse CCXIV (lines 2974–2986)

**Original**
2974. Venir s'en volt li emperere Carles, [venir, vouloir]
2975. Quant de paiens li surdent les enguardes. [sourdre (surgir)]
2976. De cels devant i vindrent dui messages, [venir]
2977. De l'amirail li nuncent la bataille: [noncier (annoncer)]
2978. «Reis orguillos, nen est fins que t'en alges! [être, s'en aller]
2979. Veiz Baligant, ki apres tei chevalchet: [voir, chevaucher]
2980. Granz sunt les oz qu'il ameinet d'Arabe. [être, amener]
2981. Encoi verrum se tu as vasselage.« AOI. [voir, avoir]
2982. Carles li reis en ad prise sa barbe; [avoir, prendre]
2983. Si li remembret del doel e [del] damage, [remembrer (se souvenir)]
2984. Mult fierement tute sa gent reguardez; [regarder]
2985. Puis si s'escriet a sa voiz grand e halte: [escrier (s'écrier)]
2986. «Barons franceis, as chevals e as armes!» AOI.

**Translation**
2974. Emperor Charles wants to be on his way,
2975. When the pagans' scouts appear before him.
2976. From those in front two messengers came,
2977. They announce the battle to him on behalf of the emir:
2978. «Proud king, it is too late for you to go away! <!-- uncertain: "nen est fins que t'en alges" read as "there is no longer a limit/way for you to leave" -->
2979. See Baligant, who rides after you:
2980. Great are the armies that he brings from Arabia.
2981. Today we shall see whether you have valor.»
2982. Charles the king has taken hold of his beard;
2983. So he remembers the grief and the harm,
2984. Very proudly he looks over all his people;
2985. Then he cries out in a loud, high voice:
2986. «Frankish barons, to horses and to arms!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXV (lines 2987–2998)

**Original**
2987. Li empereres tuz premereins s'adubet: [aduber (s'équiper)]
2988. Isnelement ad vestue sa brunie, [avoir, vêtir]
2989. Lacet sun helme, si ad ceinte Joiuse, [lacer, avoir, ceindre]
2990. Ki pur soleill sa clartet n'en muet; [muer]
2991. Pent a sun col un escut de Biterne, [pendre]
2992. Tient sun espiet, sin fait brandir la hanste, [tenir, faire, brandir]
2993. En Tencendur, sun bon cheval, puis muntet: [monter]
2994. Il le cunquist es guez desuz Marsune, [conquérir]
2995. Sin getat mort Malpalin de Nerbone; [jeter]
2996. Laschet la resne, mult suvent l'esperonet, [lâcher, éperonner]
2997. Fait sun eslais, veant cent mil humes, AOI. [faire, voir]
2998. Recleimet Deu e l'apostle de Rome. [réclamer]

**Translation**
2987. The emperor is the very first to arm himself:
2988. Swiftly he has put on his hauberk,
2989. He laces his helmet, and has girded on Joyeuse,
2990. Which does not change its brightness for the sun;
2991. He hangs from his neck a shield from Biterne,
2992. He holds his spear, and makes its shaft quiver,
2993. Then he mounts Tencendur, his good horse:
2994. He had conquered it at the fords below Marsonne,
2995. And there he threw Malpalin of Narbonne down dead;
2996. He loosens the rein, very often he spurs it,
2997. He makes his charge in sight of a hundred thousand men, AOI.
2998. He calls upon God and the apostle of Rome.

---

## Laisse CCXVI (lines 2999–3013)

**Original**
2999. Par tut le champ cil de France descendent, [descendre]
3000. Plus de cent milie s'en adubent ensemble; [aduber (s'équiper)]
3001. Guarnemenz unt ki ben lor atalente[n]t, [avoir, atalenter (plaire)]
3002. Cevals curanz e lur armes mult gentes;
3003. Puis sunt muntez e unt grant science. [être, monter, avoir]
3004. S'il troevent oí, bataille quident rendre. [trouver, cuidier (croire), rendre]
3005. Cil gunfanun sur les helmes lur pendent. [pendre]
3006. Quant Carles veit si beles cuntenances, [voir]
3007. Sin apelat Jozeran de Provence, [appeler]
3008. Naimon li duc, Antelme de Maience:
3009. «En tels vassals deit hom aveir fiance! [devoir, avoir]
3010. Asez est fols ki entr'els se dem[ent]et. [être, se démenter (se lamenter)] <!-- spelling: dem[ent]et is an editorial reading, taken as se dementer "lament" -->
3011. Si Arrabiz de venir ne se repentent, [venir, se repentir]
3012. La mort Rollant lur quid cherement rendre.» [cuidier (croire), rendre]
3013. Respunt dux Neimes: «E Deus le nos cunsente!» AOI. [répondre, consentir]

**Translation**
2999. Across the whole field those of France dismount,
3000. More than a hundred thousand arm themselves together;
3001. They have equipment that pleases them well,
3002. Swift horses and their very handsome arms;
3003. Then they have mounted and have great skill.
3004. If they find them today, they expect to give battle.
3005. Those pennons hang from their helmets.
3006. When Charles sees such fine bearing,
3007. He calls Jozeran of Provence,
3008. Duke Naimon, Antelme of Mayence:
3009. «In such vassals a man ought to have trust!
3010. He is quite a fool who despairs among them. <!-- uncertain: the editor's emended "dem[ent]et" is read as "despairs, gives way to lament" -->
3011. If the Arabs do not repent of coming,
3012. I mean to make them pay dearly for Roland's death.»
3013. Duke Naimes replies: «May God grant it to us!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXVII (lines 3014–3025)

**Original**
3014. Carles apelet Rabe[l] e Guinemán. [appeler]
3015. Ço dist li reis: «Seignurs, jo vos cumant, [dire, commander]
3016. Seiez es lius Oliver e Rollant: [être]
3017. L'un port l'espee e l'altre l'olifant, [porter]
3018. Si chevalcez el premer chef devant, [chevaucher]
3019. Ensembl'od vos .XV. milie de Franc(ei)s,
3020. De bachelers, de noz (...) meillors vaillanz.
3021. Apres icels en avrat altretant, [avoir]
3022. Sis guierat Gibuins e Guinemans.» [guier (guider)]
3023. Naimes li dux e li quens Jozerans
3024. Icez eschieles ben les vunt ajustant. [aller, ajuster (rassembler)]
3025. S'il troevent oí, bataille i ert mult grant. AOI. [trouver, être]

**Translation**
3014. Charles calls Rabel and Guinemant.
3015. The king said: «Lords, I command you,
3016. Be in the places of Oliver and Roland:
3017. Let one carry the sword and the other the oliphant,
3018. And ride in the first rank, at the head, ahead,
3019. Together with you fifteen thousand Franks,
3020. Of young knights, of our (...) best and most valiant. <!-- uncertain: the verse has a gap "(...)" in the source, so the sense is incomplete -->
3021. After these there will be as many again,
3022. And Gibuin and Guinemant will lead them.»
3023. Duke Naimes and Count Jozeran
3024. Are lining up those squadrons well.
3025. If they find them today, there will be a very great battle. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXVIII (lines 3026–3034)

**Original**
3026. De Franceis sunt les premeres escheles. [être]
3027. Apres les dous establisent la terce; [établir]
3028. En cele sunt li vassal de Baivere, [être]
3029. A .XX. [milie] chevalers la preiserent; [priser]
3030. Ja devers els bataille n'ert lessee. [être, laisser]
3031. Suz cel n'ad gent que Carles ait plus chere, [avoir]
3032. Fors cels de France, ki les regnes cunquerent. [conquérir]
3033. Li quens Oger li Daneis, li puinneres,
3034. Les guierat, kar la cumpaigne est fiere. AOI. [guier (guider), être]

**Translation**
3026. Of Franks are the first divisions.
3027. After the two they draw up the third;
3028. In it are the vassals of Bavaria,
3029. They valued it at twenty thousand knights;
3030. Never on their side will the battle be left.
3031. Under heaven there is no people that Charles holds dearer,
3032. Except those of France, who conquered the kingdoms.
3033. Count Oger the Dane, the fighter,
3034. Will lead them, for the company is fierce. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXIX (lines 3035–3043)

**Original**
3035. Treis escheles ad l'emperere Carles. [avoir]
3036. Naimes li dux puis establist la quarte [établir]
3037. De tels barons qu'asez unt vasselage: [avoir]
3038. Alemans sunt e si sunt d'Alemaigne; [être]
3039. Vint milie sunt, ço dient tuit li altre. [être, dire]
3040. Ben sunt guarniz e de chevals e d'armes; [être, garnir]
3041. Ja por murir ne guerpirunt bataille. [mourir, guerpir (abandonner)]
3042. Sis guierat Hermans li dux de Trace. [guier (guider)]
3043. Einz i murat que cuardise i facet. AOI. [mourir, faire]

**Translation**
3035. Emperor Charles has three divisions.
3036. Duke Naimes then draws up the fourth
3037. From such barons that have plenty of valor:
3038. They are Germans and they are from Germany;
3039. They are twenty thousand, all the others say so.
3040. They are well equipped both with horses and with arms;
3041. Never, even to die, will they abandon battle.
3042. Hermann, duke of Thrace, will lead them.
3043. He will die there sooner than commit cowardice there. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXX (lines 3044–3051)

**Original**
3044. Naimes li dux e li quens Jozerans
3045. La quinte eschele unt faite de Normans: [avoir, faire]
3046. .XX. milie sunt, ço dient tuit li Franc. [être, dire]
3047. Armes unt beles e bons cevals curanz; [avoir]
3048. Ja pur murir cil n'erent recreanz. [mourir, être]
3049. Suz ciel n'ad gent ki plus poissent en camp. [avoir, pouvoir]
3050. Richard li velz les guierat el camp: [guier (guider)]
3051. Cil i ferrat de sun espiet trenchant. AOI. [ferir (frapper)]

**Translation**
3044. Duke Naimes and Count Joceran
3045. Have made the fifth division of Normans:
3046. They are twenty thousand, all the Franks say so.
3047. They have fine arms and good running horses;
3048. Never, even to die, will they be recreant.
3049. Under heaven there is no people who have more power in the field.
3050. Richard the Old will lead them in the field:
3051. He will strike there with his sharp spear. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXI (lines 3052–3059)

**Original**
3052. La siste eschele unt faite de Bretuns: [avoir, faire]
3053. .XXX. milie chevalers od els unt. [avoir]
3054. Icil chevalchent en guise de baron, [chevaucher]
3055. Peintes lur hanstes, fermez lur gunfanun. [peindre, fermer]
3056. Le seignur d'els est apelet Oedun: [être, appeler]
3057. Icil cumandet le cunte Nevelun, [commander]
3058. Tedbald de Reins e le marchis Otun:
3059. «Guiez ma gent, jo vos en faz le dun!» AOI. [guier (guider), faire]

**Translation**
3052. The sixth division they have made of Bretons:
3053. They have thirty thousand knights with them.
3054. These ride in the manner of barons,
3055. Their lances painted, their gonfanons fastened.
3056. The lord of them is called Oedon:
3057. He commands Count Nevelon,
3058. Tedbald of Reims and Marquis Oton:
3059. «Lead my people, I make you a gift of it!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXII (lines 3060–3067)

**Original**
3060. Li emperere ad .VI. escheles faites. [avoir, faire]
3061. Naimes li dux puis establist la sedme [établir]
3062. De Peitevins e des barons d'Alverne:
3063. .XL. milie chevalers poeent estre. [pouvoir, être]
3064. Chevals unt bons e les armes mult beles. [avoir]
3065. Cil sunt par els en un val suz un tertre; [être]
3066. Sis beneïst Carles de sa main destre. [bénir]
3067. Els guierat Jozerans e Godselmes. AOI. [guier (guider)]

**Translation**
3060. The emperor has made six divisions.
3061. Duke Naimes then draws up the seventh
3062. Of Poitevins and of the barons of Auvergne:
3063. There may be forty thousand knights.
3064. They have good horses and very fine arms.
3065. They are by themselves in a valley beneath a hill;
3066. Charles blessed them with his right hand.
3067. Joceran and Godselme will lead them. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXIII (lines 3068–3074)

**Original**
3068. E l'oidme eschele ad Naimes establie: [avoir, établir]
3069. De Flamengs est [e] des barons de Frise. [être]
3070. Chevalers unt plus de .XL. milie; [avoir]
3071. Ja devers els n'ert bataille guerpie. [être, guerpir (abandonner)]
3072. Ço dist li reis «Cist ferunt mun servise.» [dire, faire]
3073. Entre Rembalt e Hamon de Galice
3074. Les guierunt tut par chevalerie. AOI. [guier (guider)]

**Translation**
3068. And the eighth division Naimes has drawn up:
3069. It is of Flemings and of barons of Frisia.
3070. They have more than forty thousand knights;
3071. Never on their side will battle be abandoned.
3072. The king said: «These will do my service.»
3073. Between Rembalt and Hamon of Galicia
3074. They will lead them, all in knightly fashion. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXIV (lines 3075–3083)

**Original**
3075. Entre Naimon e Jozeran le cunte
3076. La noefme eschele unt faite de prozdomes [avoir, faire]
3077. De Loherengs e de cels de Borgoigne.
3078. .L. milie chevalers unt par cunte, [avoir]
3079. Helmes laciez e vestues lor bronies; [lacer, vêtir]
3080. Espiez unt forz e les hanstes sunt curtes. [avoir, être]
3081. Si Arrabiz de venir ne demurent, [venir, demeurer]
3082. Cil les ferrunt, s'il a els s'abandunent. [ferir (frapper), s'abandonner]
3083. Sis guierat Tierris, li dux d'Argone. AOI. [guier (guider)]

**Translation**
3075. Between Naimon and Count Joceran
3076. They have made the ninth division of worthy men
3077. Of Lorrainers and of those of Burgundy.
3078. They have fifty thousand knights by count,
3079. Helmets laced and their hauberks put on;
3080. They have strong spears and the shafts are short;
3081. If the Arabs do not delay in coming,
3082. These will strike them, if they throw themselves at them.
3083. Thierry, duke of Argonne, will lead them. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXV (lines 3084–3095)

**Original**
3084. La disme eschele est des baruns de France: [être]
3085. Cent milie sunt de noz meillors cataignes. [être]
3086. Cors unt gaillarz e fieres cuntenances, [avoir]
3087. Les chefs fluriz e les barbes unt blanches, [fleurir, avoir]
3088. Osbercs vestuz e lur brunies dubleines, [vêtir]
3089. Ceintes espees franceises e d'Espaigne; [ceindre]
3090. Escuz unt genz, de multes cunoisances. [avoir]
3091. Puis sunt muntez, la bataille demandent; [être, monter, demander]
3092. «Munjoie!» escrient: Od els est Carlemagne. [escrier (s'écrier), être]
3093. Gefreid d'Anjou portet l'orieflambe: [porter]
3094. Seint Piere fut (...), si aveit num Romaine; [être, avoir]
3095. Mais de Munjoie iloec out pris eschange. AOI. [avoir, prendre]

**Translation**
3084. The tenth division is of the barons of France:
3085. They are a hundred thousand, of our best captains.
3086. They have sturdy bodies and fierce countenances,
3087. Their heads hoary and their beards white,
3088. Hauberks put on and their double mail-coats,
3089. Swords girded, French ones and Spanish ones;
3090. They have fine shields, with many distinguishing marks.
3091. Then they are mounted, they demand battle;
3092. «Munjoie!» they cry out: With them is Charlemagne.
3093. Geoffrey of Anjou carries the oriflamme:
3094. It was Saint Peter's (...), and it had the name Romaine; <!-- uncertain: the source shows a gap (...) after fut; the sense of the line is reconstructed -->
3095. But there it took Munjoie in exchange. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXVI (lines 3096–3120)

**Original**
3096. Li emperere de sun cheval descent; [descendre]
3097. Sur l'erbe verte se est culchet adenz, [se coucher]
3098. Turnet su[n] vis vers le soleill levant, [tourner]
3099. Recleimet Deu mult escordusement: [réclamer]
3100. «Veire Paterne, hoi cest jor me defend, [défendre]
3101. Ki guaresis Jonas tut veirement [garir (protéger)]
3102. De la baleine ki en sun cors l'aveit [avoir]
3103. E espar(i)gnas le rei de Niniven [épargner]
3104. E Daniel del merveillus turment
3105. Enz en la fosse des leons o fut enz, [être]
3106. Les .III. enfanz tut en un fou ardant:
3107. La tue amurs me seit hoi en present! [être]
3108. Par ta mercit, se tei plaist, me cunsent [plaire, consentir]
3109. Que mun nevold pois[se] venger Rollant!» [pouvoir, venger]
3110. Cum ad oret, si se drecet en estant, [avoir, orer (prier), se dresser]
3111. Seignat sun chef de la vertut poisant. [seignier (bénir)]
3112. Muntet li reis en sun cheval curant; [monter]
3113. L'estreu li tindrent Neimes e Jocerans; [tenir]
3114. Prent sun escut e sun espiet trenchant. [prendre]
3115. Gent ad le cors, gaillart e ben seant, [avoir]
3116. Cler le visage e de bon cuntenant.
3117. Puis si chevalchet mult aficheement. [chevaucher]
3118. Sunent cil greisle e derere e devant; [sonner]
3119. Sur tuz les altres bundist li olifant. [bondir (retentir)]
3120. Plurent Franceis pur pitet de Rollant. [pleurer]

**Translation**
3096. The emperor dismounts from his horse;
3097. On the green grass he has lain down face first,
3098. Turns his face toward the rising sun,
3099. Calls upon God most earnestly:
3100. «True Father, defend me this very day,
3101. Who truly saved Jonah
3102. From the whale that had him in its body
3103. And spared the king of Nineveh
3104. And Daniel from the dreadful torment
3105. In the lions' pit where he was,
3106. The three children all together in a burning fire:
3107. May your love be with me today!
3108. By your mercy, if it please you, grant me
3109. That I may avenge my nephew Roland!»
3110. When he has prayed, he rises to his feet.
3111. He signed his head with the cross of powerful virtue.
3112. The king mounts his running horse;
3113. Naimes and Joceran held the stirrup for him;
3114. He takes his shield and his sharp spear.
3115. He has a fine body, sturdy and comely,
3116. A bright face and a good bearing.
3117. Then he rides on most eagerly.
3118. They sound their trumpets, behind and ahead;
3119. Above all the others the oliphant resounds.
3120. The Franks weep out of pity for Roland.

---

## Laisse CCXXVII (lines 3121–3136)

**Original**
3121. Mult gentement li emperere chevalchet: [chevaucher]
3122. Desur sa bronie fors ad mise sa barbe. [avoir, mettre]
3123. Pur sue amor altretel funt li altre: [faire]
3124. Cent milie Francs en sunt reconoisable. [être]
3125. Passent cez puis e cez roches plus haltes, [passer]
3126. E cez parfunz val(ee)s, ces destreit anguisables,
3127. Issent des porz e de la tere guaste, [issir (sortir)]
3128. Devers Espaigne sunt alez en la marche; [être, aller]
3129. En un emplein unt prise lur estage. [avoir, prendre]
3130. A Baligant repairent ses enguardes. [repairer (rentrer)]
3131. Uns Sulians ki ad dit sun message: [avoir, dire]
3132. «Veüd avum li orguillus reis Carles. [avoir, voir]
3133. Fiers sunt si hume, n'unt talent qu'il li faillent. [être, avoir, faillir]
3134. Adubez vus, sempres avrez bataille!» [aduber (s'équiper), avoir]
3135. Dist Baligant: «Or oi grant vasselage. [dire, oïr (entendre)]
3136. Sunez voz graisles, que mi paien le sace[n]t!» [sonner, savoir]

**Translation**
3121. Most nobly the emperor rides:
3122. Over his hauberk he has placed his beard outside.
3123. For love of him the others do the same:
3124. A hundred thousand Franks are recognizable by it.
3125. They cross these hills and these higher rocks,
3126. And these deep valleys, these distressing defiles,
3127. They come out of the passes and out of the wasted land,
3128. Toward Spain they have gone into the march;
3129. On an open plain they have taken up their camp.
3130. To Baligant his scouts return.
3131. A Syrian who has delivered his message:
3132. «We have seen the proud King Charles.
3133. His men are fierce, they have no desire to fail him.
3134. Arm yourselves, you will soon have battle!»
3135. Baligant said: «Now I hear great valor.
3136. Sound your trumpets, so that my pagans may know it!»

---

## Laisse CCXXVIII (lines 3137–3171)

**Original**
3137. Par tute l'ost funt lur taburs suner, [faire, sonner]
3138. E cez buisines e cez greisles mult cler:
3139. Paien descendent pur lur cors aduber. [descendre, aduber (s'équiper)]
3140. Li amiralz ne se voelt demurer: [vouloir, demeurer]
3141. Vest une bronie dunt li pan sunt sasfret, [vêtir, être, safrer (orner d'or)]
3142. Lacet sun elme, ki ad or est gemmet, [lacer, être, gemmer]
3143. Puis ceint s'espee al senestre costet. [ceindre]
3144. Par sun orgoill li ad un num truvet: [avoir, trouver]
3145. Par la spee Carlun dunt il oït parler [oïr (entendre), parler]
3146. [lacuna — line lost in Oxford ms]
3147. Ço ert s'enseigne en bataille campel: [être]
3148. Ses chevalers en ad fait escrier. [avoir, faire, escrier (s'écrier)]
3149. Pent a sun col un soen grant escut let: [pendre]
3150. D'or est la bucle e de cristal listet, [être, lister (border)]
3151. La guige en est d'un bon palie roet; [être]
3152. Tient sun espiet, si l'apelet Maltet: [tenir, appeler]
3153. La hanste grosse cume uns tinels;
3154. De sul le fer fust uns mulez trusset. [être, trousser]
3155. En sun destrer Baligant est muntet; [être, monter]
3156. L'estreu li tint Marcules d'Ultremer. [tenir]
3157. La forcheüre ad asez grant li ber, [avoir]
3158. Graisles les flancs e larges les costez;
3159. Gros ad le piz, belement est mollet, [avoir, être, mouler]
3160. Lees les espalles e le vis ad mult cler, [avoir]
3161. Fier le visage, le chef recercelet, [recerceler (boucler)]
3162. Tant par ert blancs cume flur en estet; [être]
3163. De vasselage est suvent esprovet. [être, éprouver]
3164. Deus! quel baron, s'oüst chrestientet! [avoir] <!-- spelling: oüst is the subjunctive of aveir (eüst), not oïr -->
3165. Le cheval brochet, li sancs en ist tuz clers, [brocher (éperonner), issir (sortir)]
3166. Fait sun eslais, si tressalt un fosset, [faire, tressaillir (franchir d'un bond)]
3167. Cinquante pez i poet hom mesurer. [pouvoir, mesurer]
3168. Paien escrient: «Cist deit marches tenser! [escrier (s'écrier), devoir, tenser (protéger)]
3169. N'i ad Franceis, si a lui vient juster, [avoir, venir, jouster (combattre)]
3170. Voeillet o nun, n'i perdet sun edet. [vouloir, perdre]
3171. Carles est fols que ne s'en est alet.» AOI. [être, s'en aller]

**Translation**
3137. Throughout the host they have their drums sounded,
3138. And these bugles and these trumpets, very clear:
3139. The pagans dismount to arm themselves.
3140. The emir does not wish to delay:
3141. He puts on a hauberk whose skirts are set with gold, <!-- uncertain: sasfret (safré) taken as set with gold; exact sense of the ornament is unclear -->
3142. Laces his helmet, which is set with gems in gold, <!-- uncertain: ad or read as a or, with gold; if ad is avoir the line would read differently -->
3143. Then girds his sword at his left side.
3144. Out of his pride he has found a name for it:
3145. After the sword of Charles of which he had heard tell
3146. [lacuna]
3147. That was his rallying cry in pitched battle:
3148. He has had his knights cry it out.
3149. He hangs from his neck a large, broad shield of his:
3150. Of gold is the boss and edged with crystal, <!-- uncertain: listet read as edged or bordered, from lister -->
3151. The strap of it is of a fine silk with a roundel pattern, <!-- uncertain: roet read as patterned with roundels -->
3152. He holds his spear, and he calls it Maltet:
3153. The shaft thick as a pole;
3154. The iron alone would be a mule's load.
3155. Baligant has mounted his warhorse;
3156. Marcules of Overseas held his stirrup for him.
3157. The baron has a quite ample trunk, <!-- uncertain: forcheüre read as the trunk from the fork of the legs upward -->
3158. Slender at the flanks and broad at the sides;
3159. He has a thick chest, it is finely molded; <!-- uncertain: mollet read as molded, shaped -->
3160. Broad shoulders and a very bright face;
3161. Fierce is the face, the head curly; <!-- uncertain: recercelet read as curly, from recerceler -->
3162. As white as a flower in summer;
3163. He is often proved in valor.
3164. God! what a baron, if only he were a Christian!
3165. He spurs the horse, the clear blood pours out of it,
3166. Makes his dash and leaps over a ditch,
3167. Fifty feet one can measure there.
3168. The pagans cry out: «This one is fit to defend marches!
3169. There is no Frank who, if he comes to joust with him,
3170. Whether he wishes it or not, does not lose his years there. <!-- uncertain: edet is literally age; the idiom probably means he loses his life -->
3171. Charles is a fool not to have gone away.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXIX (lines 3172–3183)

**Original**
3172. Li amiralz ben resemblet barun. [ressembler]
3173. Blanche ad la barbe ensement cume flur, [avoir]
3174. E de sa lei mult par est saives hom, [être]
3175. E en bataille est fiers e orgoillus. [être]
3176. Ses filz Malpramis mult est chevalerus: [être]
3177. Granz est e forz e trait as anceisurs. [être, traire (tirer)]
3178. Dist a sun perre: «Sire, car cevalchum! [dire, chevaucher]
3179. Mult me merveill se ja verrum Carlun.» [se merveiller (s'étonner), voir]
3180. Dist Baligant: «Oïl, car mult est proz. [dire, être]
3181. En plusurs gestes de lui sunt granz honurs. [être]
3182. Il n'en at mie de Rollant sun nevold: [avoir]
3183. N'avrat vertut ques tienget cuntre nus.» AOI. [avoir, tenir]

**Translation**
3172. The emir looks very much like a baron.
3173. His beard is white, just like a flower,
3174. And in his religion he is a very wise man,
3175. And in battle he is fierce and proud.
3176. His son Malpramis is very knightly:
3177. He is tall and strong and takes after his forebears.
3178. He says to his father: «Sire, let us ride on!
3179. I very much wonder whether we shall ever see Charles.»
3180. Baligant says: «Yes, for he is very valiant.
3181. In many chronicles there is great honor for him.
3182. He has nothing left of Roland, his nephew: <!-- uncertain: "Il n'en at mie de Rollant sun nevold" is elliptical; read as "he no longer has Roland his nephew" -->
3183. He will not have the strength to stand against us.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXX (lines 3184–3200)

**Original**
3184. - «Bels filz Malpramis,» ço li dist Baligant, [dire]
3185. «Li altrer fut ocis le bon vassal Rollant [être, ocire (tuer)]
3186. E Oliver, li proz e li vaillanz,
3187. Li .XII. per qui Carles amat tant, [aimer]
3188. De cels de France .XX. milie cumbatanz.
3189. Trestuz les altres ne pris jo mie un guant. [priser]
3190. Li empereres repairet veirement, [repairer (rentrer)]
3191. S'il m'at nunciet mes mes, li Sulians, [avoir, noncier (annoncer)]
3192. .X. escheles en unt faites mult granz. [avoir, faire]
3193. Cil est mult proz ki sunet l'olifant, [être, sonner]
3194. D'un graisle cler racatet ses cumpaignz; [rachater (rallier)]
3195. E si cevalcent el premer chef devant, [chevaucher]
3196. Ensembl'od els .XV. milie de Francs,
3197. De bachelers que Carles cleimet enfanz. [clamer (nommer)]
3198. Apres icels en i ad ben altretanz: [avoir]
3199. Cil i ferrunt mult orgoillusement.» [ferir (frapper)]
3200. Dist Malpramis: «Le colp vos en demant.» AOI. [dire, demander]

**Translation**
3184. «Fair son Malpramis,» Baligant said to him,
3185. «The other day the good vassal Roland was killed,
3186. And Oliver, the valiant and brave,
3187. The twelve peers whom Charles loved so much,
3188. Of those of France twenty thousand fighters.
3189. All the others I do not value at a glove.
3190. The emperor is truly coming back,
3191. If my messenger, the Syrian, has reported to me, <!-- uncertain: syntax of "S'il m'at nunciet mes mes, li Sulians" (subject of "S'il" and construction with the next line) -->
3192. They have made ten very large divisions.
3193. He is very valiant who sounds the oliphant,
3194. With a clear trumpet he rallies his companions; <!-- uncertain: sense of "racatet" (rallies, calls back); head follows rachater at 1833 -->
3195. And they ride in the first van ahead,
3196. Together with them fifteen thousand Franks,
3197. Of young men whom Charles calls "children."
3198. After those there are quite as many more:
3199. They will strike very proudly.»
3200. Malpramis says: «I ask the first blow of you for it.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXXI (lines 3201–3213)

**Original**
3201. - «Filz Malpramis,» Baligant li ad dit, [avoir, dire]
3202. «Jo vos otri quanque m'avez ci quis. [otreier (octroyer), avoir, querre (chercher)]
3203. Cuntre Franceis sempres irez ferir; [aller, ferir (frapper)]
3204. Si i merrez Torleu, le rei persis, [mener]
3205. E Dapamort, un altre rei leutiz.
3206. Le grant orgoill se ja puez matir, [pouvoir, mater]
3207. Jo vos durrai un pan de mun païs [donner]
3208. Des Cheriant entresqu'en Val Marchis.»
3209. Cil respunt: «Sire, vostre mercit!» [répondre]
3210. Passet avant, le dun en requeillit, [passer, recueillir]
3211. Ço est de la tere ki fut al rei Flurit, [être]
3212. A itel ore unches puis ne la vit, [voir]
3213. Ne il n'en fut ne vestut ne saisit. [être, vêtir, saisir]

**Translation**
3201. «Son Malpramis,» Baligant said to him,
3202. «I grant you whatever you have asked of me here.
3203. You will soon go strike against the Franks;
3204. And you will lead there Torleu, the Persian king,
3205. And Dapamort, another king, of the Lutici.
3206. If you can ever crush their great pride,
3207. I will give you a tract of my country
3208. From Cheriant as far as Val Marchis.»
3209. He answers: «Sire, my thanks!»
3210. He steps forward and receives the gift of it,
3211. That is, of the land that belonged to King Flurit,
3212. At such an hour that he never saw it afterwards,
3213. And was never invested with it nor given possession of it.

---

## Laisse CCXXXII (lines 3214–3236)

**Original**
3214. Li amiraill chevalchet par cez oz. [chevaucher]
3215. Sis filz le siut, ki mult ad grant le cors. [sivre (suivre), avoir]
3216. Li reis Torleus e li reis Dapamort
3217. .XXX. escheles establissent mult tost: [établir]
3218. Chevalers unt a merveillus esforz; [avoir]
3219. En la menur .L. milie en out. [avoir]
3220. La premere est de cels de Butentrot, [être]
3221. E l'altre apres de Micenes as chefs gros;
3222. Sur les eschines qu'il unt en mi les dos [avoir]
3223. Cil sunt seiet ensement cume porc. AOI. [être] <!-- spelling: "seiet" read as an adjective from "seie" (bristle), not a form of seoir -->
3224. E la t(er)erce est de Nubles e de Blos, [être]
3225. E la quarte est de Bruns e d'Esclavoz, [être]
3226. E la quinte est de Sorbres e de Sorz, [être]
3227. E la siste est d'Ermines e de Mors, [être]
3228. E la sedme est de cels de Jericho, [être]
3229. E l'oitme est de Nigres e la noefme de Gros, [être]
3230. E la disme est de Balide la fort: [être]
3231. Ço est une gent ki unches ben ne volt. AOI. [être, vouloir]
3232. Li amiralz en juret quanqu'il poet [jurer, pouvoir]
3233. De Mahumet les vertuz e le cors:
3234. «Karles de France chevalchet cume fols. [chevaucher]
3235. Bataille i ert, se il ne s'en destolt; [être, destolir (se détourner)]
3236. Jamais n'avrat el chef corone d'or.» [avoir]

**Translation**
3214. The emir rides through these hosts.
3215. His son follows him, who has a very large body.
3216. King Torleu and King Dapamort
3217. They draw up thirty divisions very quickly:
3218. They have knights in marvelous strength;
3219. In the smallest there were fifty thousand.
3220. The first is of those of Butentrot,
3221. And the next of the big-headed men of Micenes;
3222. Along the spines that they have in the middle of their backs
3223. They are bristled just like pigs. AOI. <!-- uncertain: "seiet" taken as "bristled" (adjective from seie), not a participle -->
3224. And the third is of the Nubles and the Blos,
3225. And the fourth is of the Bruns and the Slavs,
3226. And the fifth is of the Sorbs and the Sorz,
3227. And the sixth is of the Armenians and the Mors,
3228. And the seventh is of those of Jericho,
3229. And the eighth is of the Nigres and the ninth of the Gros,
3230. And the tenth is of mighty Balide:
3231. It is a people that never wished any good. AOI.
3232. The emir swears with all his might
3233. By the powers and the body of Mohammed:
3234. «Charles of France rides like a madman.
3235. There will be battle, if he does not turn aside;
3236. Never again will he have a gold crown on his head.»

---

## Laisse CCXXXIII (lines 3237–3251)

**Original**
3237. Dis escheles establisent apres. [établir]
3238. La premere est des Canelius les laiz, [être]
3239. De Val Fuit sun venuz en traver. [être, venir]
3240. L'altre est de Turcs e la terce de Pers, [être]
3241. E la quarte est de Pinceneis e de Pers, [être]
3242. E la quinte est de Solteras e d'Avers, [être]
3243. E la siste est d'Ormaleus e d'Eugiez, [être]
3244. E la sedme est de la gent Samuel, [être]
3245. L'oidme est de Bruise e la noefme de Clavers, [être]
3246. E la disme est d'Occian le desert: [être]
3247. Ço est une gent ki Damnedeu ne sert; [être, servir]
3248. De plus feluns n'orrez parler jamais. [oïr (entendre), parler]
3249. Durs unt les quirs ensement cume fer: [avoir]
3250. Pur ço n'unt soign de elme ne d'osberc; [avoir]
3251. En la bataille sunt felun e engres. AOI. [être]

**Translation**
3237. They draw up ten divisions next.
3238. The first is of the hateful Canelius,
3239. They have come from Val Fuit across the land. <!-- uncertain: "en traver" (across, crosswise) -->
3240. The second is of Turks and the third of Persians,
3241. And the fourth is of Pincenates and of Persians,
3242. And the fifth is of Solteras and of Avers,
3243. And the sixth is of Ormaleus and of Eugiez,
3244. And the seventh is of the people of Samuel,
3245. The eighth is of Bruise and the ninth of Clavers,
3246. And the tenth is of Occian the desert:
3247. It is a people that does not serve the Lord God;
3248. You will never hear of more wicked men.
3249. They have hides as hard as iron:
3250. For that reason they care nothing for helmet or hauberk;
3251. In battle they are wicked and fierce. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXXIV (lines 3252–3264)

**Original**
3252. Li amiralz .X. escheles ad justedes: [avoir, ajuster (rassembler)]
3253. La premere est des jaianz de Malprese, [être]
3254. L'altre est de Hums e la terce de Hungres, [être]
3255. E la quarte est de Baldise la lunge [être]
3256. E la quinte est de cels de Val Penuse [être]
3257. E la siste est de [...] Maruse, [être]
3258. E la sedme est de Leus e d'Astrimónies; [être]
3259. L'oidme est d'Argoilles e la noefme de Clarbone, [être]
3260. E la disme est des barbez de Fronde: [être]
3261. Ço est une gent ki Deu nen amat unkes. [être, aimer]
3262. Geste Francor .XXX. escheles i numbrent. [nombrer]
3263. Granz sunt les oz u cez buisines sunent. [être, sonner]
3264. Paien chevalchent en guise de produme. AOI. [chevaucher]

**Translation**
3252. The emir has assembled ten divisions:
3253. The first is of the giants of Malprose,
3254. The second is of Huns and the third of Hungarians,
3255. And the fourth is of long Baldise
3256. And the fifth is of those of Val Penuse
3257. And the sixth is of [...] Maruse,
3258. And the seventh is of Leus and of Astrimonies;
3259. The eighth is of Argoilles and the ninth of Clarbone,
3260. And the tenth is of the bearded men of Fronde:
3261. It is a people that never loved God.
3262. Against the race of the Franks they count thirty divisions here. <!-- uncertain: "Geste Francor ... i numbrent" (who counts, and whether thirty is the combined total) -->
3263. Great are the hosts where these trumpets sound.
3264. The pagans ride in the manner of worthy men. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXXV (lines 3265–3278)

**Original**
3265. Li amiralz mult par est riches hoem. [être]
3266. Dedavant sei fait porter sun dragon [faire, porter]
3267. E l'estandart Tervagan e Mahum
3268. E un ymagene Apolin le felun.
3269. Des Canelius chevalchent envirun. [chevaucher]
3270. Mult haltement escrient un sermun: [escrier (s'écrier)]
3271. «Ki par noz Deus voelt aveir guarison, [vouloir, avoir]
3272. Sis prit e servet par grant afflictiun!» [prier, servir]
3273. Paien i baissent lur chefs e lur mentun; [baisser]
3274. Lor helmes clers i suzclinent enbrunc. [suzcliner (incliner)] <!-- spelling: suz- (sous-) + cliner; a distinct head from the known cliner (incliner) -->
3275. Dient F[r]anceis: «Sempres murrez, glutun! [dire, mourir]
3276. De vos seit hoi male confusiun! [être]
3277. Li nostre Deu, guarantisez Carlun! [garantir]
3278. Ceste bataille seit ju(ic)get en sun num!» AOI. [être, juger]

**Translation**
3265. The emir is a very powerful man.
3266. He has his dragon carried before him
3267. And the standard of Tervagant and Mohammed
3268. And an image of Apollo the wicked.
3269. They ride around among the Canelius.
3270. They cry out a sermon very loudly:
3271. «Whoever wants to have healing through our gods,
3272. Let him pray to them and serve them with great affliction!»
3273. The pagans lower their heads and their chins;
3274. They bow their bright helmets down, head forward.
3275. The Franks say: «You will soon die, villains!
3276. May ill confusion fall on you today!
3277. Our God, protect Charles!
3278. May this battle be judged in his name!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXXVI (lines 3279–3290)

**Original**
3279. Li amiralz est mult de grant saveir; [être]
3280. A sei apelet sis filz e les dous reis: [appeler]
3281. «Seignurs barons, devant chevalchereiz, [chevaucher]
3282. Mes escheles tutes les guiereiz; [guier (guider)]
3283. Mais des meillors voeill jo retenir treis: [vouloir, retenir]
3284. L'un ert de Turcs e l'altre d'Ormaleis, [être]
3285. E la terce est des jaianz de Malpreis. [être]
3286. Cil d'Ociant ierent e[n]sembl'ot mei, [être]
3287. Si justerunt a Charles e a Franceis. [jouster (combattre)]
3288. Li emperere, s'il se cumbat od mei, [se combattre]
3289. Desur le buc la teste perdre en deit. [perdre, devoir]
3290. Trestut seit fiz, n'i avrat altre dreit.» AOI. [être, avoir]

**Translation**
3279. The emir is a man of great wisdom;
3280. He calls to him his son and the two kings:
3281. «Lords barons, you will ride in front,
3282. And you will lead all my divisions;
3283. But I want to keep three of the best:
3284. One will be of Turks and the other of Ormaleus,
3285. And the third is of the giants of Malprose.
3286. Those of Occian will be together with me,
3287. And they will fight against Charles and the Franks.
3288. The emperor, if he fights with me,
3289. Must lose his head from off his trunk.
3290. Let it be wholly certain; there will be no other right.» AOI. <!-- uncertain: "seit fiz" (taken as "be certain/settled") -->

---

## Laisse CCXXXVII (lines 3291–3304)

**Original**
3291. Granz sunt les oz e les escheles beles; [être]
3292. Entr'els nen at ne pui ne val ne tertre, [avoir]
3293. Selve ne bois; asconse n'i poet estre; [pouvoir, être]
3294. Ben s'entreveient en mi la pleine tere. [s'entrevoir]
3295. Dist Baligant: «La meie gent averse, [dire]
3296. Car chevalchez pur la bataille quere!» [chevaucher, querre (chercher)]
3297. L'enseigne portet Amborres d'Oluferne. [porter]
3298. Paien escrient, «Precieuse» l'apelent. [escrier (s'écrier), appeler]
3299. Dient Franceis: «De vos seit hoi grant perte!» [dire, être]
3300. Mult haltement: «Munjoie!» renuvelent. [renouveler]
3301. Li emperere i fait suner ses greisles, [faire, sonner]
3302. E l'olifan, ki tres(tu)tuz les esclairet. [esclairier (dominer)]
3303. Dient paien: «La gent Ca[r]lun est bele. [dire, être]
3304. Bataille avrum e aduree e pesme.» AOI. [avoir, adurer (endurcir)]

**Translation**
3291. Great are the hosts and handsome the divisions;
3292. Between them there is neither hill nor valley nor mound,
3293. Neither forest nor wood; no hiding place can be there;
3294. They see one another clearly in the middle of the open land.
3295. Baligant says: «My hostile people, <!-- uncertain: "averse" taken as an adjective (hostile) -->
3296. Ride on, to seek the battle!»
3297. Amborre of Oluferne carries the banner.
3298. The pagans cry out, and call it «Precieuse.»
3299. The Franks say: «May great loss fall on you today!»
3300. Very loudly they renew the cry «Munjoie!»
3301. The emperor has his trumpets sounded,
3302. And the oliphant, which rings out above them all. <!-- uncertain: sense of "esclairet" (rings out clear above, dominates) -->
3303. The pagans say: «Charles's people are fine.
3304. We shall have a battle hard and bitter.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXXVIII (lines 3305–3328)

**Original**
3305. Grant est la plaigne e large la cuntree. [être]
3306. Luisent cil elme as perres d'or gemmees, [luire, gemmer]
3307. E cez escuz e cez bronies safrees, [safrer (orner d'or)]
3308. E cez espiez, cez enseignes fermees. [fermer]
3309. Sunent cez greisles, les voiz en sunt mult cleres; [sonner, être]
3310. De l'olifan haltes sunt les menees. [être]
3311. Li amiralz en apelet sun frere, [appeler]
3312. Ço est Canabeus, li reis de Floredee: [être]
3313. Cil tint la terre entresqu'en Val Sevree. [tenir]
3314. Les escheles Charlun li ad mustrees: [avoir, montrer]
3315. «Veez l'orgoil de France la loee! [voir, louer]
3316. Mult fierement chevalchet li emperere; [chevaucher]
3317. Il est darere od cele gent barbee. [être]
3318. Desur lur bronies lur barbes unt getees, [avoir, jeter]
3319. Altresi blanches cume neif sur gelee.
3320. Cil i ferrunt de lances e d'espees, [ferir (frapper)]
3321. Bataille avrum e forte e aduree: [avoir, adurer (endurcir)]
3322. Unkes nuls hom ne vit tel ajustee.» [voir]
3323. Plus qu'om ne lancet une verge pelee, [lancer]
3324. Baligant ad ses cumpaignes trespassees. [avoir, trespasser (dépasser)]
3325. Une raisun lur ad dit e mustree: [avoir, dire, montrer]
3326. «Venez, paien, kar jo(n) irai en l'estree.» [venir, aller]
3327. De sun espiet la hanste en ad branlee; [avoir, brandir]
3328. Envers Karlun la mure en ad turnee. AOI. [avoir, tourner]

**Translation**
3305. The plain is great and the country wide.
3306. Those helmets gleam, set with gold gems,
3307. And those shields and those gold-adorned hauberks,
3308. And those spears, those fastened banners.
3309. Those trumpets sound; their voices are very clear;
3310. The blasts of the olifant are loud.
3311. The emir calls his brother over,
3312. That is Canabeus, the king of Floredee:
3313. He held the land as far as Val Sevree.
3314. He has shown him Charles's battalions:
3315. «Look at the pride of France the praised!
3316. Very proudly the emperor rides;
3317. He is at the rear with that bearded host.
3318. Over their hauberks they have thrown their beards,
3319. As white as snow on frost.
3320. They will strike there with lances and swords,
3321. We shall have a battle strong and hard:
3322. Never did any man see such a gathering.»
3323. Farther than one throws a peeled rod,
3324. Baligant has outdistanced his companies.
3325. He has spoken a speech to them and set it out:
3326. «Come, pagans, for I shall go ahead on the way.» <!-- uncertain: "en l'estree" taken as "on the road, at the head" -->
3327. He has brandished the shaft of his spear;
3328. He has turned its point toward Charles. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXXXIX (lines 3329–3344)

**Original**
3329. Carles li magnes, cum il vit l'amiraill, [voir]
3330. E le dragon, l'enseigne e l'estandart,
3331. De cels d'Arabe si grant force i par (ar)ad, [avoir]
3332. De la cuntree unt purprises les parz, [avoir, porprendre (occuper)]
3333. Ne mes que tant (scue)[cume] l'empereres en ad, [avoir]
3334. Li reis de France s'en escriet mult halt: [escrier (s'écrier)]
3335. «Barons franceis, vos estes bons vassals. [être]
3336. Tantes batailles avez faites en camps! [avoir, faire]
3337. Veez paien: felun sunt e cuart, [voir, être]
3338. Tutes lor leis un dener ne lur valt. [valoir]
3339. S'il unt grant gent, d'iço, seignurs, qui calt? [avoir, chaloir (importer)]
3340. Ki errer voelt, a mei venir s'en alt!» [errer (voyager), vouloir, venir, s'en aller]
3341. Des esperons puis brochet le cheval, [brocher (éperonner)]
3342. E Tencendor li (a)ad fait .IIII. salz. [avoir, faire]
3343. Dient Franceis: «Icist reis est vassals! [dire, être]
3344. Chevalchez, bers! Nul de nus ne vus falt.» [chevaucher, faillir]

**Translation**
