3329. Charles the Great, when he sees the emir,
3330. And the dragon, the banner, and the standard,
3331. There is such a great force of those of Arabia there, <!-- uncertain: verse is corrupt; "i par (ar)ad" read as "i par ad", "there is very much" -->
3332. They have occupied the parts of the country,
3333. Except for as much of it as the emperor holds,
3334. The king of France cries out very loudly:
3335. «French barons, you are good vassals.
3336. So many battles you have fought in the field!
3337. Look at the pagans: they are wicked and cowardly,
3338. All their laws are not worth a penny to them.
3339. If they have a great host, what of it, lords? Who cares?
3340. Whoever wishes to ride, let him come to me!»
3341. Then with his spurs he spurs his horse,
3342. And Tencendor has made four leaps for him.
3343. The French say: «This king is a valiant vassal!
3344. Ride, brave men! Not one of us will fail you.»

---

## Laisse CCXL (lines 3345–3351)

**Original**
3345. Clers fut li jurz e li soleilz luisanz. [être, luire]
3346. Les oz sunt beles e les cumpaignes granz. [être]
3347. Justees sunt les escheles devant. [être, joster (joindre)]
3348. Li quens Rabels e li quens Guinemans
3349. Lascent les resnes a lor cevals curanz, [lâcher, courir]
3350. Brochent a eit; dunc laisent curre Francs: [brocher (éperonner), laisser, courir]
3351. Si vunt ferir de lur espiez trenchanz. AOI. [aller, ferir (frapper), trancher]

**Translation**
3345. Bright was the day and the sun shining.
3346. The hosts are fair and the companies large.
3347. The foremost battalions have come together.
3348. Count Rabel and Count Guineman
3349. Loosen the reins to their running horses,
3350. Spur at full speed; then the Franks let them run:
3351. So they go to strike with their sharp spears. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXLI (lines 3352–3359)

**Original**
3352. Li quens Rabels est chevaler hardiz, [être]
3353. Le cheval brochet des esperuns d'or fin, [brocher (éperonner)]
3354. Si vait ferir Torleu, le rei persis, [aller, ferir (frapper)]
3355. N'escut ne bronie ne pout sun colp tenir: [pouvoir, tenir]
3356. L'espiet a or li ad enz el cors mis, [avoir, mettre]
3357. Que mort l'abat sur un boissun petit. [abattre]
3358. Dient F[r]anceis: «Damnedeus nos aït!» [dire, aïdier (aider)]
3359. Carles ad dreit, ne li devom faillir.» AOI. [avoir, devoir, faillir]

**Translation**
3352. Count Rabel is a bold knight,
3353. He spurs his horse with spurs of fine gold,
3354. And goes to strike Torleu, the Persian king;
3355. Neither shield nor hauberk could withstand his blow:
3356. He has driven the gold-adorned spear into his body,
3357. So that he strikes him dead upon a small bush.
3358. The French say: «May the Lord God help us!»
3359. Charles is in the right; we must not fail him.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXLII (lines 3360–3368)

**Original**
3360. E Guineman justet a un rei leutice. [jouster (combattre)]
3361. Tute li freint la targe, ki est flurie; [fraindre (briser), être, fleurir]
3362. Apres li ad la bronie descunfite; [avoir, desconfire (défaire)]
3363. Tute l'enseigne li ad enz el cors mise, [avoir, mettre]
3364. Que mort l'abat, ki qu'en plurt u kin riet. [abattre, pleurer, rire]
3365. A icest colp cil de France s'esc(ri)rient: [escrier (s'écrier)]
3366. «Ferez, baron, ne vos targez mie! [ferir (frapper), targier (tarder)]
3367. Carles ad dreit vers la gent [pa]iesnie; [avoir]
3368. Deus nus ad mis al plus verai juïse.» AOI. [avoir, mettre]

**Translation**
3360. And Guineman jousts with a Lutician king.
3361. He shatters his shield entirely, which is flowered;
3362. After that he has undone his hauberk;
3363. He has put the whole banner into his body,
3364. So that he strikes him dead, whoever weeps or laughs at it.
3365. At this blow those of France cry out:
3366. «Strike, barons, do not delay at all!
3367. Charles is in the right against the pagan people;
3368. God has set us on the truest judgment.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXLIII (lines 3369–3382)

**Original**
3369. Malpramis siet sur un cheval tut blanc; [seoir (être assis)]
3370. Cunduit sun cors en la presse des Francs. [conduire]
3371. De (u) uns es altres granz colps i vait ferant, [aller, ferir (frapper)]
3372. L'un mort sur l'altre suvent vait trescevant. [aller]
3373. Tut premereins s'escriet Baligant: [escrier (s'écrier)]
3374. «Li mien baron, nurrit vos ai lung tens. [avoir, nourrir]
3375. Veez mun fils, Carlun le vait querant, [voir, aller, querre (chercher)]
3376. A ses armes tanz barons calunjant: [chalengier (revendiquer)]
3377. Meillor vassal de lui ja ne demant. [demander]
3378. Succurez le a voz espiez trenchant!» [secourir, trancher]
3379. A icest mot paien venent avant, [venir]
3380. Durs colps i fierent, mult est li caples granz. [ferir (frapper), être]
3381. La bataille est merveilluse e pesant: [être, peser]
3382. Ne fut si fort enceis ne puis cel tens. AOI. [être]

**Translation**
3369. Malpramis sits on a horse all white;
3370. He guides his body into the press of the Franks.
3371. From one to another he goes striking great blows,
3372. One dead upon the other he often goes piling up. <!-- uncertain: "trescevant" is unidentified; the sense seems to be piling the dead up or tumbling them -->
3373. First of all Baligant cries out:
3374. «My barons, I have nourished you a long time.
3375. See my son, he goes seeking Charles,
3376. Challenging so many barons with his arms: <!-- uncertain: "calunjant" glossed with the known head; the sense is challenge or claim against so many barons -->
3377. A better vassal than he I never ask for.
3378. Help him with your sharp spears!»
3379. At this word the pagans come forward,
3380. They strike hard blows there; the fighting is great.
3381. The battle is marvelous and heavy:
3382. Never was it so fierce before or since that time. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXLIV (lines 3383–3395)

**Original**
3383. Granz sunt les oz e les cumpaignes fieres, [être]
3384. Justees sunt trestutes les escheles, [être, joster (joindre)]
3385. E li paien merveillusement fierent. [ferir (frapper)]
3386. Deus! tantes hanstes i ad par mi brisees, [avoir, briser]
3387. Escuz fruisez e bronies desmaillees! [fruisier (briser), desmailler (démailler)]
3388. La veïsez la tere si junchee: [voir, joncher]
3389. L'erbe del camp, ki est verte e delgee [être]
3390. [lacuna — line lost in Oxford ms]
3391. Li amiralz recleime sa maisnee: [réclamer]
3392. «Ferez, baron, sur la gent chrestiene!» [ferir (frapper)]
3393. La bataille est mult dure e afichee; [être, afichier (s'acharner)]
3394. Unc einz ne puis ne fut si fort ajustee; [être, ajuster (joindre)]
3395. Josqu'a la [nuit] nen ert fins otriee. AOI. [être, otreier (octroyer)]

**Translation**
3383. Great are the hosts and fierce the companies,
3384. All the battalions are joined,
3385. And the pagans strike marvelously.
3386. God! so many spears broken there through the middle,
3387. Shields shattered and hauberks stripped of their mail!
3388. There you would see the earth so strewn:
3389. The grass of the field, which is green and fine,
3390. [lacuna]
3391. The emir calls on his household:
3392. «Strike, barons, at the Christian people!»
3393. The battle is very hard and relentless;
3394. Never before or since was one so fiercely joined;
3395. Until [night] no end will be granted to it. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXLV (lines 3396–3404)

**Original**
3396. Li amiralz la sue gent apelet: [appeler]
3397. «Ferez, paien: por el venud n'i estes! [ferir (frapper), être, venir]
3398. Jo vos durrai muillers gentes e beles, [donner]
3399. Si vos durai feus e honors e teres.» [donner]
3400. Paien respundent: «Nus le devuns ben fere.» [répondre, devoir, faire]
3401. A colps pleners de lor espiez i perdent: [perdre]
3402. Plus de cent milie espees i unt traites. [avoir, traire (tirer)]
3403. Ais vos le caple e dulurus e pesmes;
3404. Bataille veit cil ki entr'els volt estre. AOI. [voir, vouloir, être]

**Translation**
3396. The emir calls his people:
3397. «Strike, pagans: you have not come here for anything else!
3398. I shall give you wives, noble and fair,
3399. And I shall give you fiefs and honors and lands.»
3400. The pagans answer: «We ought to do it well.»
3401. With full blows they lose some of their spears there:
3402. More than a hundred thousand swords they have drawn.
3403. Behold the fight, both grievous and cruel;
3404. He sees battle who wishes to be among them. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXLVI (lines 3405–3420)

**Original**
3405. Li emperere recleimet ses Franceis: [réclamer]
3406. «Seignors barons, jo vos aim, si vos crei. [aimer, croire]
3407. Tantes batailles avez faites pur mei, [avoir, faire]
3408. Regnes cunquis e desordenet reis! [conquérir, desordener (détrôner)]
3409. Ben le conuis que gueredun vos en dei [connaître, devoir]
3410. E de mun cors, de terres e d'aveir.
3411. Vengez voz fi[l]z, voz freres e voz heirs, [venger]
3412. Qu'en Rencesvals furent morz l'altre seir! [être, mourir]
3413. Ja savez vos cuntre paiens ai dreit.» [savoir, avoir]
3414. Respondent Franc: «Sire, vos dites veir.» [répondre, dire]
3415. Itels .XX. miliers en ad od sei, [avoir]
3416. Cumunement l'en prametent lor feiz, [promettre]
3417. Ne li faldrunt pur mort ne pur destreit. [faillir]
3418. N'en i ad cel sa lance n'i empleit; [avoir, empleier (asséner)]
3419. De lur espees i fierent demaneis. [ferir (frapper)]
3420. La bataille est de merveillus destreit. AOI. [être]

**Translation**
3405. The emperor calls on his French:
3406. «Lord barons, I love you and I trust you.
3407. So many battles you have fought for me,
3408. Kingdoms conquered and kings overthrown! <!-- uncertain: "desordenet" read as "thrown out of order, deposed" -->
3409. I know well that I owe you a reward for it,
3410. From my person as well, from lands and from wealth.
3411. Avenge your sons, your brothers, and your heirs,
3412. Who were dead at Roncevaux the other evening!
3413. You know well that I am in the right against the pagans.»
3414. The Franks answer: «Lord, you speak truly.»
3415. He has with him twenty thousand such men,
3416. Together they pledge him their faith,
3417. They will not fail him for death or for distress.
3418. There is not one of them who does not drive his lance in there;
3419. With their swords they strike there at once.
3420. The battle is one of marvelous hardship. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXLVII (lines 3421–3428)

**Original**
3421. E Malpramis parmi le camp chevalchet; [chevaucher]
3422. De cels de France i fait mult grant damage. [faire]
3423. Naimes li dux fierement le reguardet, [regarder]
3424. Vait le ferir cum hume vertudable. [aller, ferir (frapper)]
3425. De sun escut li freint la pene halte, [fraindre (briser)]
3426. De sun osberc les dous pans li desaffret; [desaffrer (déchirer)]
3427. El cors li met tute l'enseigne jalne, [mettre]
3428. Que mort [l'abat] entre .VII.C. des altres. [abattre]

**Translation**
3421. And Malpramis rides across the field;
3422. He does great damage there to those of France.
3423. Duke Naimes looks at him fiercely,
3424. Goes to strike him like a man of valor.
3425. From his shield he breaks the upper rim, <!-- uncertain: "pene halte" taken as the upper edge of the shield -->
3426. From his hauberk he tears off both skirts, <!-- uncertain: sense of "desaffret" (tears apart) and its head; no match among known heads -->
3427. He puts the whole yellow banner into his body,
3428. So that he strikes him dead among seven hundred others.

---

## Laisse CCXLVIII (lines 3429–3443)

**Original**
3429. Reis Canabeus, le frere a l'amiraill,
3430. Des esporuns ben brochot sun cheval; [brocher (éperonner)]
3431. Trait ad l'espee, le punt est de cristal, [avoir, traire (tirer), être]
3432. Si fiert Naimun en l'elme principal: [ferir (frapper)]
3433. L'une meitiet l'en fruissed d'une part, [fruisier (briser)]
3434. Al brant d'acer l'en trenchet .V. des laz, [trancher]
3435. Li capelers un dener ne li valt; [valoir]
3436. Trenchet la coife entresque a la char, [trancher]
3437. Jus a la tere une piece en abat. [abattre]
3438. Granz fut li colps, li dux en estonat, [être, estoner (étourdir)]
3439. Sempres caïst, se Deus ne li aidast. [cheoir (tomber), aïdier (aider)]
3440. De sun destrer le col en enbraçat. [embrasser]
3441. Se li paiens une feiz recuvrast, [recuvrer (recouvrer)]
3442. Sempres fust mort li nobilies vassal. [être, mourir]
3443. Carles de France i vint, kil succurat. AOI. [venir, secourir]

**Translation**
3429. King Canabeus, the emir's brother,
3430. Was spurring his horse hard with his spurs;
3431. He has drawn his sword, whose pommel is of crystal,
3432. And strikes Naimes on the chief helmet:
3433. One half of it he shatters off on one side,
3434. With the steel blade he cuts five of its laces away,
3435. The cap is not worth a penny to him;
3436. He cuts the coif down to the flesh,
3437. And strikes a piece of it down to the ground.
3438. Great was the blow; the duke was stunned by it,
3439. He would at once have fallen, had God not helped him.
3440. He threw his arms around his charger's neck.
3441. If the pagan had once got another chance, <!-- uncertain: object of "recuvrast" is not stated; read as recovering the chance for a second blow -->
3442. The noble vassal would at once be dead.
3443. Charles of France came there, and rescued him. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCXLIX (lines 3444–3450)

**Original**
3444. Naimes li dux tant par est anguissables, [être]
3445. E li paiens de ferir mult le hastet. [ferir (frapper), hâter]
3446. Carles li dist: «Culvert, mar le baillastes!» [dire, baillir (traiter)]
3447. Vait le ferir par sun grant vasselage: [aller, ferir (frapper)]
3448. L'escut li freint, cuntre le coer li quasset, [fraindre (briser), casser]
3449. De sun osberc li desrumpt la ventaille, [derompre (rompre)]
3450. Que mort l'abat: la sele en remeint guaste. [abattre, remaindre (rester)]

**Translation**
3444. Duke Naimes is in such great anguish,
3445. And the pagan presses hard upon him to strike.
3446. Charles says to him: «Villain, you handled him badly!»
3447. He goes to strike him with his great valor:
3448. He breaks his shield, and shatters it against his heart,
3449. Tears the ventail from his hauberk,
3450. So that he strikes him dead: the saddle stays empty there.

---

## Laisse CCL (lines 3451–3462)

**Original**
3451. Mult ad grant doel Carlemagnes li reis, [avoir]
3452. Quant Naimun veit nafret devant sei, [voir, nasfrer (blesser)]
3453. Sur l'erbe verte le sanc tut cler caeir. [cheoir (tomber)]
3454. Li empereres li ad dit a cunseill: [avoir, dire]
3455. «Bel sire Naimes, kar chevalcez od mei! [chevaucher]
3456. Morz est li gluz ki en destreit vus teneit; [être, mourir, tenir]
3457. El cors li mis mun espiet une feiz.» [mettre]
3458. Respunt li dux: «Sire, jo vos en crei. [répondre, croire]
3459. Se jo vif alques, mult grant prod i avreiz.» [vivre, avoir]
3460. Puis sunt justez par amur e par feid, [être, joster (joindre)]
3461. Ensembl'od els tels .XX. milie Franceis.
3462. N'i ad celoi que n'i fierge o n'i capleit. AOI. [avoir, ferir (frapper), capler (frapper)]

**Translation**
3451. King Charlemagne feels very great grief,
3452. When he sees Naimes wounded before him,
3453. And the bright blood fall on the green grass.
3454. The emperor has said to him in private:
3455. «Fair lord Naimes, come, ride with me!
3456. Dead is the wretch who held you in distress;
3457. I drove my spear into his body once.»
3458. The duke answers: «Sire, I believe you in this.
3459. If I live a while, you will gain great profit from it.»
3460. Then they join together, in love and in faith,
3461. With them some twenty thousand Franks.
3462. There is no one there who does not strike or hack. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLI (lines 3463–3472)

**Original**
3463. Li amiralz chevalchet par le camp, [chevaucher]
3464. Si vait ferir le cunte Guneman, [aller, ferir (frapper)]
3465. Cuntre le coer li fruisset l'escut blanc, [fruisier (briser)]
3466. De sun osberc li derumpit les pans, [derompre (rompre)]
3467. Les dous costez li deseivret des flancs, [dessevrer (séparer)]
3468. Que mort l'abat de sun cheval curant. [abattre]
3469. Puis ad ocis Gebuin e Lorain, [avoir, ocire (tuer)]
3470. Richard le Veill, li sire des Normans.
3471. Paien escrient: «Preciuse est vaillant! [escrier (s'écrier), être]
3472. Ferez, baron, nus i avom guarant!» AOI. [ferir (frapper), avoir]

**Translation**
3463. The emir rides across the field,
3464. And goes to strike Count Guneman,
3465. Against his heart he shatters his white shield,
3466. Breaks apart the skirts of his hauberk,
3467. Severs both his sides from his flanks,
3468. So that he lays him dead from his running horse.
3469. Then he has killed Gebuin and Lorain,
3470. And Richard the Old, lord of the Normans.
3471. The pagans cry out: «Precieuse is valiant!
3472. Strike, barons, we have a protector in her!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLII (lines 3473–3480)

**Original**
3473. Ki puis veist li chevaler d'Arabe, [voir]
3474. Cels d'Occiant e d'Argoillie e de Bascle!
3475. De lur espiez ben i fierent e caplent; [ferir (frapper), capler (frapper)]
3476. E li Franceis n'unt talent que s'en algent; [avoir, s'en aller]
3477. Asez i moerent e des uns e des altres. [mourir]
3478. Entresqu'al vespre est mult fort la bataille, [être]
3479. Des francs barons i ad mult gran damage. [avoir]
3480. Doel i avrat, enceis qu'ele departed. AOI. [avoir, départir (se séparer)]

**Translation**
3473. Whoever might then see the knights of Arabia,
3474. Those of Occiant and of Argoillie and of Bascle!
3475. With their spears they strike and hack well there;
3476. And the Franks have no wish to leave;
3477. Many die there, both of the one side and of the other;
3478. Until evening the battle is very fierce,
3479. And among the noble barons there is great loss.
3480. There will be grief before it ends. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLIII (lines 3481–3507)

**Original**
3481. Mult ben i fierent Franceis e Arrabit; [ferir (frapper)]
3482. Fruissent cil hanste se cil espiez furbit. [fruisier (briser)]
3483. Ki dunc veïst cez escuz si malmis, [voir, malmetre (maltraiter)]
3484. Cez blancs osbercs ki dunc oïst fremir, [oïr (entendre), frémir]
3485. E cez escuz sur cez helmes cruisir, [croissir (craquer)]
3486. Cez chevalers ki dunc veïst caïr [voir, cheoir (tomber)]
3487. E humes braire, contre tere murir, [braire, mourir]
3488. De grant dulor li poüst suvenir! [pouvoir, souvenir] <!-- spelling: suvenir is the impersonal souvenir, "to be reminded", not remembrer -->
3489. Ceste bataille est mult fort a suffrir. [être, souffrir]
3490. Li amiralz recleimet Apolin [réclamer]
3491. E Tervagan e Mahumet altresi:
3492. «Mi Damnedeu, jo vos ai mult servit! [avoir, servir]
3493. Tutes tes ymagenes ferai d'or fin.» AOI. [faire]
3494. [lacuna — line lost in Oxford ms]
3495. As li devant un soen drut, Gemalfin.
3496. Males nuveles li aportet e dit: [apporter, dire]
3497. «Baligant, sire, mal este oi baillit. [être, baillir (traiter)]
3498. Perdut avez Malpramis vostre fils, [avoir, perdre]
3499. E Canabeus, vostre frere, est ocis. [être, ocire (tuer)]
3500. A dous Franceis belement en avint. [avenir (arriver)]
3501. Li empereres en est l'uns, ço m'est vis: [être]
3502. Granz ad le cors, ben resenblet marchis; [avoir, ressembler]
3503. Blanc[he] ad la barbe cume flur en avrill.» [avoir]
3504. Li amiralz en ad le helme enclin, [avoir]
3505. E en apres sin enbrunket sun vis: [embruncher (pencher)]
3506. Si grant doel ad sempres quiad murir. [avoir, cuidier (croire), mourir]
3507. Sin apelat Jangleu l'Ultremarin. [appeler]

**Translation**
3481. Very well the Franks and the Arabs strike there;
3482. These shafts shatter, and these burnished spears.
3483. Whoever then might see those shields so battered,
3484. And hear those white hauberks clash,
3485. And those shields crack on those helmets,
3486. Whoever then might see those knights fall,
3487. And men cry out and die against the ground,
3488. Of great sorrow he might be reminded! <!-- uncertain: li poüst suvenir read as "he might be reminded of / put in mind of", parallel to li poüst remembrer elsewhere -->
3489. This battle is very hard to endure.
3490. The emir calls upon Apollin
3491. And Tervagan and Mohammed likewise:
3492. «My Lord Gods, I have served you greatly!
3493. I will make all your images of fine gold.» AOI.
3494. [lacuna]
3495. Behold, before him, one of his favorites, Gemalfin.
3496. He brings him bad news and tells it:
3497. «Baligant, sire, you have been badly treated today.
3498. You have lost Malpramis your son,
3499. And Canabeus, your brother, is killed.
3500. It turned out well for two Franks. <!-- uncertain: "A dous Franceis belement en avint" read as "it turned out well for two Franks", i.e. two Franks did this handsomely; the sense is not fully clear -->
3501. The emperor is one of them, it seems to me:
3502. He is big of body, and looks very much like a margrave;
3503. His beard is white as a flower in April.»
3504. The emir has his helmet bowed,
3505. And afterwards he lowers his face:
3506. He has such grief that he thinks he will die at once. <!-- uncertain: "quiad" read as a corrupt "quidet" (cuidier), "he thinks he will die"; the form is not otherwise explained -->
3507. And then he called Jangleu the man from Overseas.

---

## Laisse CCLIV (lines 3508–3519)

**Original**
3508. Dist l'amiraill: «Jangleu, venez avant! [dire, venir]
3509. Vos estes proz e vostre saveir est grant; [être]
3510. Vostre conseill ai oc evud tuz tens. [avoir]
3511. Que vos en semblet d'Arrabiz e de Francs? [sembler]
3512. Avrum nos la victorie del champ?» [avoir]
3513. E cil respunt: «Morz estes, Baligant! [répondre, être, mourir]
3514. Ja vostre deu ne vos erent guarant. [être]
3515. Carles est fiers e si hume vaillant; [être]
3516. Unc ne vi gent ki si fust cumbatant. [voir, être, combattre]
3517. Mais reclamez les barons d'Occiant, [réclamer]
3518. Turcs e Enfruns, Arabiz e Jaianz.
3519. Ço que estre en deit, ne l'alez demurant.» [être, devoir, aller, demeurer]

**Translation**
3508. The emir said: «Jangleu, come forward!
3509. You are valiant and your wisdom is great;
3510. I have always had your counsel. <!-- uncertain: "oc" in "ai oc evud" is unexplained; read as an intensifier or a scribal slip -->
3511. What do you think of the Arabs and of the Franks?
3512. Shall we have the victory of the field?»
3513. And he answers: «You are dead, Baligant!
3514. Your gods will never be your protectors.
3515. Charles is fierce and his men valiant;
3516. I never saw a people so warlike.
3517. But summon the barons of Occiant,
3518. Turks and Enfruns, Arabs and Giants.
3519. Whatever is to come of it, do not delay it.»

---

## Laisse CCLV (lines 3520–3530)

**Original**
3520. Li amiraill ad sa barbe fors mise, [avoir, mettre]
3521. Altresi blanche cume flur en espine:
3522. Cument qu'il seit, ne s'i voelt celer mie. [être, vouloir, celer]
3523. Met a sa buche une clere buisine, [mettre]
3524. Sunet la cler, que si paien l'oïrent; [sonner, oïr (entendre)]
3525. Par tut le camp ses cumpaignes ralient. [rallier]
3526. Cil d'Ociant i braient e henissent, [braire, hennir]
3527. Arguille si cume chen i glatissent; [glatir]
3528. Requerent Franc par si grant estultie, [requerre (attaquer)]
3529. El plus espes ses rumpent e partissent: [rompre, partir (diviser)]
3530. A icest colp en jetent mort .VII. milie. [jeter]

**Translation**
3520. The emir has let his beard show,
3521. Just as white as a flower on the thorn:
3522. Whatever may happen, he does not wish to hide at all.
3523. He puts a clear trumpet to his mouth,
3524. Sounds it loudly, so that his pagans heard it;
3525. Throughout the field his companies rally.
3526. Those of Occiant bray and neigh there,
3527. Those of Arguille yelp there like dogs; <!-- uncertain: "Arguille" read as the people of Argoillie named in 3474; the construction is elliptical -->
3528. They charge the Franks with great recklessness,
3529. In the thickest part they break and split them:
3530. At this one blow they cast down dead seven thousand.

---

## Laisse CCLVI (lines 3531–3542)

**Original**
3531. Li quens Oger cuardise n'out unkes; [avoir]
3532. Meillor vassal de lui ne vestit bronie. [vêtir]
3533. Quant de Franceis les escheles vit rumpre, [voir, rompre]
3534. Si apelat Tierri, le duc d'Argone, [appeler]
3535. Gefrei d'Anjou e Jozeran le cunte,
3536. Mult fierement Carles en araisunet: [araisoner (haranguer)]
3537. «Veez paien cum ocient voz humes! [voir, ocire (tuer)]
3538. Ja Deu ne placet qu'el chef portez corone, [plaire, porter]
3539. S'or n'i ferez pur venger vostre hunte.» [ferir (frapper), venger]
3540. N'i ad icel ki un sul mot respundet: [avoir, répondre]
3541. Brochent ad eit, lor cevals laissent cure, [brocher (éperonner), laisser, courir]
3542. Vunt les ferir la o il les encuntrent. [aller, ferir (frapper), encontrer (rencontrer)]

**Translation**
3531. Count Oger never had cowardice;
3532. No better vassal than he ever put on a hauberk.
3533. When he saw the battalions of the Franks breaking,
3534. He called Thierry, the duke of Argone,
3535. Geoffrey of Anjou and Count Jozeran,
3536. And very boldly he addresses Charles about it:
3537. «See how the pagans are killing your men!
3538. May God never grant that you wear a crown on your head,
3539. If you do not strike now to avenge your shame.»
3540. There is not one of them who says a single word:
3541. They spur eagerly, let their horses run,
3542. They go to strike them wherever they meet them.

---

## Laisse CCLVII (lines 3543–3559)

**Original**
3543. Mult ben i fiert Carlemagnes li reis, AOI. [ferir (frapper)]
3544. Naimes li dux e Oger li Daneis,
3545. Geifreid d'Anjou ki l'enseigne teneit. [tenir]
3546. Mult par est proz danz Ogers li Daneis! [être]
3547. Puint le ceval, laisset curre ad espleit, [poindre (éperonner), laisser, courir]
3548. Si vait ferir celui ki le dragun teneit, [aller, ferir (frapper), tenir]
3549. Qu'Ambure cravente en la place devant sei [cravanter (s'écrouler)]
3550. E le dragon e l'enseigne le rei.
3551. Baligant veit sun gunfanun cadeir [voir, cheoir (tomber)]
3552. E l'estandart Mahumet remaneir. [remaindre (rester)]
3553. Li amiralz alques s'en aperceit [s'apercevoir]
3554. Que il ad tort e Carlemagnes dreit. [avoir]
3555. Paien d'Arabe s'en turnent plus .C. [s'en aller]
3556. Li emperere recleimet ses parenz: [réclamer]
3557. «Dites, baron, por Deu, si m'aidereiz.» [dire, aider]
3558. Respundent Francs: «Mar le demandereiz. [répondre, demander]
3559. Trestut seit fel ki n'i fierget a espleit!» AOI. [être, ferir (frapper)]

**Translation**
3543. Very well King Charlemagne strikes there, AOI.
3544. Naimes the duke and Oger the Dane,
3545. Geoffrey of Anjou, who held the standard.
3546. Very valiant is Lord Oger the Dane!
3547. He spurs the horse, lets it run at full speed,
3548. And goes to strike the one who held the dragon,
3549. So that Ambure collapses on the spot before him <!-- uncertain: Ambure read as the subject of cravente, falling with the dragon and the standard; he could instead be the object, felled by Oger -->
3550. Along with the dragon and the king's standard.
3551. Baligant sees his gonfalon fall
3552. And Mohammed's standard left behind.
3553. The emir somewhat perceives
3554. That he is in the wrong and Charlemagne in the right.
3555. More than a hundred pagans of Arabia leave.
3556. The emperor calls upon his kinsmen:
3557. «Tell me, barons, for God's sake, whether you will help me.»
3558. The Franks answer: «You need not ask.
3559. Let him be utterly a villain who does not strike hard here!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLVIII (lines 3560–3578)

**Original**
3560. Passet li jurz, si turnet a la vespree. [passer, tourner]
3561. Franc e paien i fierent des espees. [ferir (frapper)]
3562. Cil sunt vassal ki les oz ajusterent. [être, ajuster (rassembler)]
3563. Lor enseignes n'i unt mie ubliees: [avoir, oublier]
3564. Li amira[l]z «Preciuse!» ad criee, [avoir, crier]
3565. Carles «Munjoie!», l'enseigne renumee.
3566. L'un conuist l'altre as haltes voiz e as cleres; [connaître]
3567. En mi le camp amdui s'entr'encuntrerent: [encontrer (rencontrer)]
3568. Si se vunt ferir, granz colps s'entredunerent [aller, ferir (frapper), donner]
3569. De lor espiez en lor targes roees.
3570. Fraites les unt desuz cez bucles lees. [avoir, fraindre (briser)]
3571. De lor osbercs les pans en desevrerent: [dessevrer (séparer)]
3572. Dedenz cez cors mie ne s'adeserent: [adeser (toucher)]
3573. Rumpent cez cengles e cez seles verserent, [rompre, verser]
3574. Cheent li rei, a tere se turbecherent, [cheoir (tomber), trebuchier (s'abattre)]
3575. Isnelement sur lor piez releverent. [relever]
3576. Mult vassalment unt traites les espees. [avoir, traire (tirer)]
3577. Ceste bataille n'en ert mais destornee: [être, détourner]
3578. Seinz hume mort ne poet estre achevee. AOI. [pouvoir, être, achever]

**Translation**
3560. The day passes, and turns toward evening.
3561. Franks and pagans strike there with their swords.
3562. They are valiant men who assembled the armies.
3563. They have by no means forgotten their battle cries:
3564. The emir has cried «Precieuse!»,
3565. Charles «Munjoie!», the renowned battle cry.
3566. Each recognizes the other by their loud and clear voices;
3567. In the middle of the field they both meet each other:
3568. They go to strike one another, they dealt each other great blows
3569. With their spears on their wheel-patterned shields. <!-- uncertain: targes roees read as shields decorated with a wheel or round pattern -->
3570. They have broken them below the broad bosses.
3571. They cut the skirts away from their hauberks;
3572. They did not at all touch the bodies inside them:
3573. They break the girths and overturned the saddles,
3574. The kings fall, tumble to the ground,
3575. Swiftly they get back up on their feet.
3576. Very valiantly they have drawn their swords.
3577. This battle will not be turned aside from now on:
3578. Without a man dead it cannot be ended. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLIX (lines 3579–3588)

**Original**
3579. Mult est vassal Carles de France dulce; [être]
3580. Li amiralt, il nel crent ne ne dutet. [craindre, douter (craindre)]
3581. Cez lor espees tutes nues i mustrent, [montrer]
3582. Sur cez escuz mult granz colps s'entredunent, [donner]
3583. Trenchent les quirs e cez fuz ki sunt dubles; [trancher, être]
3584. Cheent li clou, si pecerent les bucles; [cheoir (tomber), peceier (dépecer)]
3585. Puis fierent il nud a nud sur les bronies; [ferir (frapper)]
3586. Des helmes clers li fous en escarbunet. [escarbuncler (étinceler)]
3587. Ceste bataille ne poet remaneir unkes, [pouvoir, remaindre (rester)]
3588. Josque li uns sun tort i reconuisset. AOI. [reconoistre (reconnaître)]

**Translation**
3579. Very valiant is Charles of sweet France;
3580. The emir, he neither fears him nor dreads him.
3581. They show those swords of theirs, all bare, there,
3582. On those shields they deal each other very great blows,
3583. They cut the leathers and the wooden boards that are double;
3584. The nails fall, and they shatter the bosses;
3585. Then they strike on the hauberks, bare to bare; <!-- uncertain: "nud a nud" read as striking directly, with the shields destroyed and nothing between the blow and the mail -->
3586. From the bright helmets the fire sparkles out. <!-- uncertain: escarbunet read as a verb "to flash like a carbuncle" (from escarbuncle); not otherwise attested in the first half -->
3587. This battle can never be left off,
3588. Until one of them admits his wrong there. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLX (lines 3589–3601)

**Original**
3589. Dist l'amiraill: «Carles, kar te purpenses, [dire, se porpenser (réfléchir)]
3590. Si pren cunseill que vers mei te repentes! [prendre, se repentir]
3591. Mort as (...) mun filz, par le men esciente; [avoir]
3592. A mult grant tort mun païs me calenges; [chalengier (revendiquer)]
3593. Deven mes hom, en fedeltet voeill rendre; [devenir, vouloir, rendre]
3594. Ven mei servir d'ici qu'en Oriente!» [venir, servir]
3595. Carles respunt: «Mult grant viltet me sembl[e]; [répondre, sembler]
3596. Pais ne amor ne dei a paien rendre. [devoir, rendre]
3597. Receif la lei que Deus nos apresentet, [recevoir, présenter]
3598. Christientet, e pui te amerai sempres; [aimer]
3599. Puis serf e crei le rei omnipotente!» [servir, croire]
3600. Dist Baligant: «Malvais sermun cumences!» [dire, commencer]
3601. Puis vunt ferir des espees qu'unt ceintes. AOI. [aller, ferir (frapper), avoir, ceindre]

**Translation**
3589. The emir said: «Charles, pray reflect,
3590. And take counsel so that you repent toward me!
3591. You have killed (...) my son, to my certain knowledge; <!-- uncertain: the source has a gap (...) after the participle; reading mort as a verbal participle with as, "you have slain" -->
3592. With great wrong you claim my country from me;
3593. Become my man, I wish you to pay homage;
3594. Come serve me from here as far as the East!»
3595. Charles answers: «It seems to me very great shame;
3596. I must not give peace or love to a pagan.
3597. Receive the law that God offers us,
3598. Christianity, and then I will love you forever;
3599. Then serve and believe in the all-powerful King!»
3600. Baligant said: «You begin an evil sermon!»
3601. Then they go to strike with the swords they have girded on. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXI (lines 3602–3611)

**Original**
3602. Li amiralz est mult de grant vertut. [être]
3603. Fier Carlemagne sur l'elme d'acer brun, [ferir (frapper)]
3604. Desur la teste li ad frait e fendut; [avoir, fraindre (briser), fendre]
3605. Met li l'espee sur les chevels menuz, [mettre]
3606. Prent de la carn grant pleine palme e plus: [prendre]
3607. Iloec endreit remeint li os tut nut. [remaindre (rester)]
3608. Carles cancelet, por poi qu'il n'est caüt; [chanceler, être, cheoir (tomber)]
3609. Mais Deus ne volt qu'il seit mort ne vencut. [vouloir, être, mourir, vaincre]
3610. Seint Gabriel est repairet a lui, [être, repairer (rentrer)]
3611. Si li demande: «Reis magnes, que fais tu?» [demander, faire]

**Translation**
3602. The emir is of very great strength.
3603. He strikes Charlemagne on the helmet of brown steel,
3604. Above, he has broken and split his head;
3605. He sets the sword on his thin hair,
3606. Takes from the flesh a full great palm and more:
3607. Right there the bone is left all bare.
3608. Charles staggers; he has all but fallen;
3609. But God does not wish him to be dead or conquered.
3610. Saint Gabriel has come back to him,
3611. And asks him: «Great king, what are you doing?»

---

## Laisse CCLXII (lines 3612–3624)

**Original**
3612. Quant Carles oït la seinte voiz de l'angle, [oïr (entendre)]
3613. N'en ad poür ne de murir dutance; [avoir, mourir]
3614. Repairet loi vigur e remembrance. [repairer (rentrer)]
3615. Fiert l'amiraill de l'espee de France, [ferir (frapper)]
3616. L'elme li freint o li gemme reflambent, [fraindre (briser), reflamber (resplendir)]
3617. [T]renchet la teste pur la cervele espandre [trancher, épandre]
3618. [E] tut le vis tresqu'en la barbe blanche,
3619. Que mort l'abat senz nule recuvrance. [abattre]
3620. «Munjoie!» escriet pur la reconuisance. [escrier (s'écrier)]
3621. A icest mot venuz i est dux Neimes: [être, venir]
3622. Prent Tencendur, muntet i est li reis magnes. [prendre, être, monter]
3623. Paien s'en turnent, ne volt Deus qu'il i remainent. [s'en aller, vouloir, remaindre (rester)]
3624. Or sunt Franceis a icels qu'il demandent. [être, demander]

**Translation**
3612. When Charles heard the holy voice of the angel,
3613. He has no fear and no dread of dying;
3614. Vigor and memory return to him.
3615. He strikes the emir with the sword of France,
3616. Breaks his helmet, where the gems blaze,
3617. Cuts the head so as to spill the brains
3618. And all the face down to the white beard,
3619. So that he fells him dead, with no recovery.
3620. «Montjoie!» he cries as the rallying cry.
3621. At this word Duke Naimes has come up:
3622. He takes Tencendur, and the great king has mounted.
3623. The pagans go away; God does not wish them to stay there.
3624. Now the Franks are upon those they were seeking.

---

## Laisse CCLXIII (lines 3625–3632)

**Original**
3625. Paien s'en fuient, cum Damnesdeus le volt. [s'enfuir, vouloir]
3626. Encalcent Francs e l'emperere avoec. [enchacier (pourchasser)]
3627. Ço dist li reis: «Seignurs, vengez voz doels, [dire, venger]
3628. Si esclargiez voz talenz e voz coers, [esclargir (éclaircir)]
3629. Kar hoi matin vos vi plurer des oilz.» [voir, pleurer]
3630. Respondent Franc: «Sire, çó nus estoet.» [répondre, estovoir (falloir)]
3631. Cascuns i fiert tanz granz colps cum il poet. [ferir (frapper), pouvoir]
3632. Poi s'en estoerstrent d'icels ki sunt iloec. [estordre (échapper), être]

**Translation**
3625. The pagans flee, as the Lord God wills.
3626. The Franks give chase, and the emperor with them.
3627. The king said: «Lords, avenge your sorrows,
3628. And clear your minds and your hearts,
3629. For this morning I saw you weeping from your eyes.»
3630. The Franks answer: «Sire, that we must.»
3631. Each one strikes there as many great blows as he can.
3632. Few of those who are there escaped.

---

## Laisse CCLXIV (lines 3633–3647)

**Original**
3633. Granz est li calz, si se levet la puldre. [être, se lever]
3634. Paien s'en fuient e Franceis les anguissent; [s'enfuir, angoisser]
3635. Li enchalz duret d'ici qu'en Sarraguce. [durer]
3636. En sum sa tur muntee est Bramidonie, [être, monter]
3637. Ensembl'od li si clerc e si canonie
3638. De false lei, que Deus nen amat unkes: [aimer]
3639. Ordres nen unt ne en lor chefs corones. [avoir]
3640. Quant ele vit Arrabiz si cunfundre, [voir, confondre]
3641. A halte voiz s'escrie: «Aiez nos, Mahum! [escrier (s'écrier), aider]
3642. E! gentilz reis, ja sunt vencuz noz humes, [être, vaincre]
3643. Li amiralz ocis a si grant hunte!» [ocire (tuer)]
3644. Quant l'ot Marsilie, vers sa pareit se turnet, [oïr (entendre), se tourner]
3645. Pluret des oilz, tute sa chere enbrunchet: [pleurer, embruncher (pencher)]
3646. Morz est de doel. Si cum pecchet l'encumbret, [être, mourir, encombrer (accabler)]
3647. L'anme de lui as vifs diables dunet. AOI. [donner]

**Translation**
3633. Great is the heat, and the dust rises.
3634. The pagans flee and the Franks harry them;
3635. The pursuit lasts as far as Saragossa.
3636. At the top of her tower Bramimonde has climbed,
3637. Together with her her clerics and her canons
3638. Of a false law, which God never loved:
3639. They have no orders, nor tonsures on their heads.
3640. When she saw the Arabs so confounded,
3641. She cries out in a loud voice: «Help us, Mahomet!
3642. Oh! noble king, our men are already conquered,
3643. The emir slain in such great shame!»
3644. When Marsile hears it, he turns toward his wall,
3645. Weeps from his eyes, bows down his whole face:
3646. He is dead of grief. As sin weighs upon him,
3647. He gives his soul to the living devils. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXV (lines 3648–3657)

**Original**
3648. Paien sunt morz, alquant cunfundue, [être, mourir, confondre]
3649. E Carles ad sa bataille vencue. [avoir, vaincre]
3650. De Sarraguce ad la porte abatue: [avoir, abattre]
3651. Or set il ben que n'est mais defendue. [savoir, être, défendre]
3652. Prent la citet, od sa gent i est venue; [prendre, être, venir]
3653. Par poestet icele noit i jurent. [gésir (être couché)]
3654. Fiers est li reis a la barbe canue, [être]
3655. E Bramidonie les turs li ad rendues: [avoir, rendre]
3656. Les dis sunt grandes, les cinquante menues. [être]
3657. Mult ben espleitet qui Damnesdeus aiuet. [esploitier (accomplir), aider]

**Translation**
3648. The pagans are dead, some overwhelmed,
3649. And Charles has won his battle.
3650. He has broken down the gate of Saragossa:
3651. Now he knows well that it is no longer defended.
3652. He takes the city, has come in with his people;
3653. By might they lay there that night.
3654. Fierce is the king with the white-grown beard,
3655. And Bramimonde has surrendered the towers to him:
3656. The ten are great, the fifty small.
3657. He does very well whom the Lord God helps.

---

## Laisse CCLXVI (lines 3658–3674)

**Original**
3658. Passet li jurz, la noit est aserie; [passer, être, aserir (devenir nuit)]
3659. Clers est la lune e les esteiles flambient. [être, flamber]
3660. Li emperere ad Sarraguce prise. [avoir, prendre]
3661. A mil Franceis funt ben cercer la vile, [faire, chercher]
3662. Les sinagoges e les mahumeries;
3663. A mailz de fer e a cuignees qu'ils tindrent, [tenir]
3664. Fruissent les ymagenes e trestutes les ydeles: [fruisier (briser)]
3665. N'i remeindrat ne sorz ne falserie. [remaindre (rester)]
3666. Li reis creit en Deu, faire voelt sun servise; [croire, vouloir, faire]
3667. E si evesque les eves beneïssent, [bénir]
3668. Meinent paien ent[r]esqu'al baptisterie: [mener]
3669. S'or i ad cel qui Carles cuntredie voillet, [avoir, contredire, vouloir]
3670. Il le fait pendre o ardeir ou ocire. [faire, pendre, ardre (brûler), ocire (tuer)]
3671. Baptizet sunt asez plus de .C. milie [être, baptiser]
3672. Veir chrestien, ne mais sul la reïne.
3673. En France dulce iert menee caitive: [être, mener]
3674. Ço voelt li reis par amur cunvertisset. [vouloir, convertir]

**Translation**
3658. The day passes, night has fallen;
3659. Clear is the moon and the stars blaze.
3660. The emperor has taken Saragossa.
3661. They have the city thoroughly searched by a thousand Franks,
3662. The synagogues and the mosques;
3663. With iron mallets and with axes that they held,
3664. They smash the images and all the idols:
3665. No sorcery or falsehood will remain there. <!-- uncertain: sorz read as sorcery or divination (sors) -->
3666. The king believes in God, wishes to do his service;
3667. And his bishops bless the waters,
3668. They lead the pagans to the baptistery:
3669. If there is anyone there who wishes to oppose Charles,
3670. He has him hanged or burned or killed.
3671. More than a hundred thousand are baptized,
3672. True Christians, except only the queen.
3673. She will be led captive to sweet France:
3674. That is the king's wish, that she be converted through love.

---

## Laisse CCLXVII (lines 3675–3704)

**Original**
3675. Passet la noit, si apert le cler jor. [passer, apparoir (apparaître)]
3676. De Sarraguce Carles guarnist les turs; [garnir]
3677. Mil chevalers i laissat puigneürs; [laisser]
3678. Guardent la vile a oes l'empereor. [garder]
3679. Mandet li reis e si hume trestuz [mander]
3680. E Bramidonie, qu'il meinet en sa prisun; [mener]
3681. Mais n'ad talent que li facet se bien nun. [avoir, faire]
3682. Repairez sunt a joie e a baldur. [être, repairer (rentrer)]
3683. Passent Nerbone par force e par vigur; [passer]
3684. Vint a Burdeles la citet de... [venir]
3685. Desur l'alter seint Severin le baron
3686. Met l'oliphan plein d'or e de manguns: [mettre]
3687. Li pelerin le veient ki la vunt. [voir, aller]
3688. Passet Girunde a mult granz nefs qu'i sunt; [passer, être]
3689. Entresque a Blaive ad cunduit sun nevold [avoir, conduire]
3690. E Oliver, sun nobilie cumpaignun,
3691. E l'arcevesque, ki fut sages e proz. [être]
3692. En blancs sarcous fait metre les seignurs [faire, mettre]
3693. A Seint Romain; la gisent li baron. [gésir (être couché)]
3694. Francs les cumandent a Deu e a ses nuns. [comander (confier)]
3695. Carles cevalchet e les vals e les munz; [chevaucher]
3696. Entresqu'a Ais ne volt prendre sujurn. [vouloir, prendre]
3697. Tant chevalchat qu'il descent al perrun. [chevaucher, descendre]
3698. Cume il est en sun paleis halçur, [être]
3699. Par ses messages mandet ses jugeors: [mander]
3700. Baivers e Saisnes, Loherencs e Frisuns;
3701. Alemans mandet, si mandet Borguignuns, [mander]
3702. E Peitevins e Normans e Bretuns,
3703. De cels de France des plus saives qui sunt. [être]
3704. Des ore cumencet le plait de Guenelun. [commencer]

**Translation**
3675. The night passes, and the clear day appears.
3676. Charles garrisons the towers of Saragossa;
3677. He left there a thousand fighting knights;
3678. They guard the city for the emperor's use.
3679. The king summons, and all his men,
3680. And Bramimonde, whom he leads in his custody;
3681. But he has no wish to do her anything but good.
3682. They have returned in joy and gladness.
3683. They pass Narbonne by force and by might;
3684. He came to Bordeaux, the city of... <!-- uncertain: the source verse breaks off at the ellipsis (...) -->
3685. Upon the altar of Saint Severin the baron
3686. He sets the oliphant full of gold and mangons:
3687. The pilgrims who go there see it.
3688. He crosses the Gironde with very many ships that are there;
3689. As far as Blaye he has escorted his nephew
3690. And Oliver, his noble companion,
3691. And the archbishop, who was wise and valiant.
3692. In white sarcophagi he has the lords placed
3693. At Saint Romain; there the barons lie.
3694. The Franks commend them to God and to his names.
3695. Charles rides over the valleys and the mountains;
3696. As far as Aix he does not want to take any rest.
3697. He rode until he dismounts at the mounting-block.
3698. When he is in his lofty palace,
3699. He summons his judges by his messengers:
3700. Bavarians and Saxons, Lorrainers and Frisians;
3701. He summons Alemanni, and he summons Burgundians,
3702. And Poitevins and Normans and Bretons,
3703. Of those of France, the wisest there are.
3704. From now begins the trial of Ganelon.

---

## Laisse CCLXVIII (lines 3705–3722)

**Original**
3705. Li empereres est repairet d'Espaigne, [être, repairer (rentrer)]
3706. E vient a Ais, al meillor sied de France; [venir]
3707. Muntet el palais, est venut en la sale. [monter, être, venir]
3708. As li Alde venue, une bele damisele. [venir]
3709. Ço dist al rei: «O est Rollant le catanie, [dire, être]
3710. Ki me jurat cume sa per a prendre?» [jurer, prendre]
3711. Carles en ad e dulor e pesance, [avoir]
3712. Pluret des oilz, tiret sa barbe blance: [pleurer, tirer]
3713. «Soer, cher'amie, de hume mort me demandes. [demander]
3714. Jo t'en durai mult esforcet eschange: [donner]
3715. Ço est Loewis, mielz ne sai a parler; [être, savoir, parler]
3716. Il est mes filz e si tendrat mes marches.» [être, tenir]
3717. Alde respunt: «Cest mot mei est estrange. [répondre, être]
3718. Ne place Deu ne ses seinz ne ses angles [plaire]
3719. Apres Rollant que jo vive remaigne!» [vivre, remaindre (rester)]
3720. Pert la culor, chet as piez Carlemagne, [perdre, cheoir (tomber)]
3721. Sempres est morte, Deus ait mercit de l'anme! [être, mourir, avoir]
3722. Franceis barons en plurent e si la pleignent. [pleurer, plaindre]

**Translation**
3705. The emperor has returned from Spain,
3706. And comes to Aix, the best seat of France;
3707. He goes up into the palace, has come into the hall.
3708. Behold Alde come, a fair maiden.
3709. She said to the king: «Where is Roland the captain,
3710. Who swore to me to take me as his peer?» <!-- uncertain: cume sa per read as "as his peer (equal, consort)" -->
3711. Charles has both sorrow and grief over it,
3712. Weeps from his eyes, pulls at his white beard:
3713. «Sister, dear friend, you ask me for a dead man.
3714. I will give you in exchange for him something greatly enhanced:
3715. That is Louis, I know nothing better to say;
3716. He is my son, and he will hold my marches.»
3717. Alde answers: «This word is strange to me.
3718. May it not please God, nor his saints, nor his angels
3719. That I remain alive after Roland!»
3720. She loses her color, falls at Charlemagne's feet,
3721. At once she is dead; God have mercy on her soul!
3722. The French barons weep for her and lament her.

---

## Laisse CCLXIX (lines 3723–3733)

**Original**
3723. Alde la bel[e] est a sa fin alee. [être, aller]
3724. Quidet li reis que el[le] se seit pasmee; [cuidier (croire), être, se pâmer]
3725. Pited en ad, sin pluret l'emperere; [avoir, pleurer]
3726. Prent la as mains, si l'en ad relevee. [prendre, avoir, relever]
3727. Desur l(es)[']espalles ad la teste clinee. [avoir, cliner (incliner)]
3728. Quant Carles veit que morte l'ad truvee, [voir, avoir, trouver]
3729. Quatre cuntesses sempres i ad mandees. [avoir, mander]
3730. A un muster de nuneins est portee; [être, porter]
3731. La noit la guaitent entresqu'a l 'ajurnee. [guaitier (veiller)]
3732. Lunc un alter belement l'enterrerent. [enterrer]
3733. Mult grant honor i ad li reis dunee, AOI. [avoir, donner]

**Translation**
3723. Alde the fair has gone to her end.
3724. The king thinks that she has swooned;
3725. He has pity for her, and the emperor weeps for it;
3726. He takes her by the hands, and so has raised her up.
3727. Upon her shoulders she has her head bowed.
3728. When Charles sees that he has found her dead,
3729. He has summoned four countesses at once.
3730. She is carried to a convent of nuns;
3731. All night they keep watch over her until daybreak.
3732. Beside an altar they buried her with care.
3733. The king has given her very great honor there. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXX (lines 3734–3741)

**Original**
3734. Li emperere est repairet ad Ais. [être, repairer (rentrer)]
3735. Guenes li fels, en caeines de fer,
3736. En la citet est devant le paleis. [être]
3737. A un estache l'unt atachet cil serf, [avoir, attacher]
3738. Les mains li lient a curreies de cerf; [lier]
3739. Tres ben le batent a fuz e a jamelz: [battre]
3740. N'ad deservit que altre ben i ait; [avoir, deservir (mériter)]
3741. A grant dulur iloec atent sun plait. [attendre]

**Translation**
3734. The emperor has returned to Aix.
3735. Ganelon the traitor, in chains of iron,
3736. Is in the city, before the palace.
3737. To a stake those serfs have tied him,
3738. They bind his hands with straps of deerskin;
3739. They beat him very well with sticks and with staves:
3740. He has not deserved to have any other good there;
3741. In great pain he waits there for his trial.

---

## Laisse CCLXXI (lines 3742–3749)

**Original**
3742. Il est escrit en l'anciene geste [être, écrire]
3743. Que Carles mandet humes de plusurs teres. [mander]
3744. Asemblez sunt ad Ais, a la capele. [être, assembler]
3745. Halz est li jurz, mult par est grande la feste, [être]
3746. Dient alquanz del baron seint Silvestre. [dire]
3747. Des ore cumencet le plait e les noveles [commencer]
3748. De Guenelun, ki traïsun ad faite. [avoir, faire]
3749. Li emperere devant sei l'ad fait traire. AOI. [avoir, faire, traire (tirer)]

**Translation**
3742. It is written in the old chanson de geste
3743. That Charles summons men from many lands.
3744. They are assembled at Aix, at the chapel.
3745. High is the day, great is the feast,
3746. Some say it is of the baron Saint Sylvester.
3747. From now on begin the trial and the news
3748. Of Ganelon, who has committed treason.
3749. The emperor has had him dragged before him. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXXII (lines 3750–3761)

**Original**
3750. «Seignors barons» dist Carlemagnes li reis, [dire]
3751. «De Guenelun car me jugez le dreit! [juger]
3752. Il fut en l'ost tresque en Espaigne od mei, [être]
3753. Si me tolit .XX. milie de mes Franceis [tolir (enlever)]
3754. E mun nevold, que ja mais ne verreiz, [voir]
3755. E Oliver, li proz e li curteis;
3756. Les .XII. pers ad traït por aveir.» [avoir, trahir]
3757. Dist Guenelon: «Fel seie se jol ceil! [dire, être, celer]
3758. Rollant me forfist en or et en aveir, [forsfaire (faire tort)]
3759. Pur que jo quis sa mort e sun destreit; [querre (chercher)]
3760. Mais traïsun nule n'en i otrei.» [otreier (octroyer)]
3761. Respundent Franc: «Ore en tendrum cunseill.» [répondre, tenir]

**Translation**
3750. «Lord barons,» said Charlemagne the king,
3751. «Judge me the right of Ganelon!
3752. He was in the army with me as far as Spain,
3753. And he took from me twenty thousand of my Franks
3754. And my nephew, whom you will never see again,
3755. And Oliver, the worthy and the courteous;
3756. The twelve peers he has betrayed for gain.»
3757. Ganelon said: «May I be a villain if I conceal it!
3758. Roland wronged me in gold and in goods,
3759. Wherefore I sought his death and his distress;
3760. But I grant that there is no treason in it.»
3761. The Franks answer: «Now we shall hold counsel on it.»

---

## Laisse CCLXXIII (lines 3762–3779)

**Original**
3762. Devant le rei la s'estut Guenelun: [ester (se tenir debout)]
3763. Cors ad gaillard, el vis gente color; [avoir]
3764. S'il fust leials, ben resemblast barun. [être, ressembler]
3765. Veit cels de France e tuz les jugeürs, [voir]
3766. De ses parenz .XXX. ki od lui sunt; [être]
3767. Puis s'escriat haltement, a grant voeiz: [escrier (s'écrier)]
3768. «Por amor Deu, car m'entendez, barons! [entendre]
3769. Seignors, jo fui en l'ost avoec l'empereür, [être]
3770. Serveie le par feid e par amur. [servir]
3771. Rollant sis nies me coillit en haür, [cuillir (prendre)]
3772. Si me jugat a mort e a dulur. [juger]
3773. Message fui al rei Marsiliun; [être]
3774. Par mun saveir vinc jo a guarisun. [venir]
3775. Jo desfiai Rollant le poigneor [défier]
3776. E Oliver e tuiz lur cumpaignun;
3777. Carles l'oïd e si nobilie baron. [oïr (entendre)]
3778. Venget m'en sui, mais n'i ad traïsun.» [être, se venger, avoir]
3779. Respundent Francs: «A conseill en irums.» [répondre, aller]

**Translation**
3762. Before the king Ganelon stood there:
3763. He has a sturdy body, a noble color in his face;
3764. If he were loyal, he would well resemble a baron.
3765. He sees those of France and all the judges,
3766. Thirty of his kinsmen who are with him;
3767. Then he cries out loudly, in a great voice:
3768. «For the love of God, listen to me, barons!
3769. Lords, I was in the army with the emperor,
3770. I served him in faith and in love.
3771. Roland, his nephew, took me into hatred,
3772. And judged me to death and to suffering.
3773. I was a messenger to King Marsile;
3774. By my own wit I came to safety.
3775. I defied Roland the warrior
3776. And Oliver and all their companions;
3777. Charles heard it, and his noble barons.
3778. I have avenged myself, but there is no treason in it.»
3779. The Franks answer: «We shall go into council.»

---

## Laisse CCLXXIV (lines 3780–3792)

**Original**
3780. Quant Guenes veit que ses granz plaiz cumencet, [voir, commencer]
3781. De ses parenz ensemble [od li] i out trente. [avoir]
3782. Un en i ad a qui li altre entendent: [avoir, entendre]
3783. Ço est Pinabel del castel de Sorence; [être]
3784. Ben set parler e dreite raisun rendre; [savoir, parler, rendre]
3785. Vassals est bons por ses armes defendre, AOI. [être, défendre]
3786. Ço li dist Guenes: «En vos [...] ami... [dire]
3787. Getez mei hoi de mort e de calunje!» [jeter]
3788. Dist Pinabel: «Vos serez guarit sempres. [dire, être, garir (guérir)]
3789. N'i ad Frances ki vos juget a pendre, [avoir, juger, pendre]
3790. U l'emper[er]e les noz dous cors en asemblet, [assembler]
3791. Al b(a)rant d'acer que jo ne l'en desmente.» [démentir]
3792. Guenes li quens a ses piez se presente. [se présenter]

**Translation**
3780. When Ganelon sees that his great trial begins,
3781. Of his kinsmen, together [with him], there were thirty.
3782. There is one among them to whom the others listen:
3783. That is Pinabel of the castle of Sorence;
3784. He knows well how to speak and to give just reason;
3785. A good vassal he is, for defending his arms, AOI.
3786. Ganelon said this to him: «In you [...] friend... <!-- uncertain: the verse is fragmentary in the source ([...] and trailing ellipsis); translation is partial -->
3787. Free me today from death and from accusation!»
3788. Pinabel said: «You will be safe soon.
3789. There is no Frank who will judge you to hang,
3790. Or else the emperor brings our two bodies together
3791. With the steel blade, so that I give him the lie on it.» <!-- uncertain: the negative in 'que jo ne l'en desmente' is read as expletive, so 'I give him the lie' -->
3792. Count Ganelon presents himself at his feet.

---

## Laisse CCLXXV (lines 3793–3806)

**Original**
3793. Bavier e Saisnes sunt alet a conseill, [être, aller]
3794. E Peitevin e Norman e Franceis;
3795. Asez i ad Alemans e tTiedeis. [avoir]
3796. Icels d'Alverne(ne) i sunt li plus curteis; [être]
3797. Pur Pinabel se cuntienent plus quei. [se tenir]
3798. Dist l'un a l'altre: «Bien fait a remaneir! [dire, faire, remaindre (rester)]
3799. Laisum le plait e si preium le rei [laisser, prier]
3800. Que Guenelun cleimt quite ceste feiz, [clamer (proclamer)]
3801. Puis si li servet par amur e par feid. [servir]
3802. Morz est Rollant, ja mais nel revereiz; [être, mourir, revoir]
3803. N'ert recuvret por ór ne por aveir: [être, recuvrer (recouvrer)]
3804. Mult sereit fols ki [l]a(a) se cumbatreit.» [être, se combattre]
3805. N'en i ad celoi nel graant e otreit, [avoir, graanter (consentir), otreier (octroyer)]
3806. Fors sul Tierri, le frere(re) dam Geifreit. AOI.

**Translation**
3793. Bavarians and Saxons have gone into council,
3794. And Poitevins and Normans and Franks;
3795. There are plenty of Germans and Teutons.
3796. Those of Auvergne are the most courteous there;
3797. For Pinabel they keep themselves the quietest.
3798. One says to the other: «It is good to leave it be!
3799. Let us drop the trial and let us pray the king
3800. To declare Ganelon free this once,
3801. Then he will serve him in love and in faith.
3802. Roland is dead, you will never see him again;
3803. He will not be recovered for gold or for goods:
3804. He would be very foolish who would fight over it.»
3805. There is no one who does not grant and agree to it,
3806. Except Thierry alone, brother of lord Geoffrey. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXXVI (lines 3807–3814)

**Original**
3807. A Charlemagne repairent si barun; [repairer (rentrer)]
3808. Dient al rei: «Sire, nus vos prium [dire, prier]
3809. Que clamez quite le cunte Guenelun, [clamer (proclamer)]
3810. Puis si vos servet par feid e par amor. [servir]
3811. Vivre le laisez, car mult est gentilz hoem. [vivre, laisser, être]
3812. Ja por murir n'en ert veüd gerun, [mourir, être, voir]
3813. Ne por aveir ja nel recuverum.» [recuvrer (recouvrer)]
3814. Ço dist li reis: «Vos estes mi felun!» AOI. [dire, être]

**Translation**
3807. His barons return to Charlemagne;
3808. They say to the king: «Sire, we pray you
3809. To declare Count Ganelon free,
3810. Then he will serve you in faith and in love.
3811. Let him live, for he is a very noble man.
3812. Not by his dying will any gain of him be seen, <!-- uncertain: 'n'en ert veüd gerun' is obscure; 'gerun' rendered as 'gain' by guess -->
3813. And for riches we shall never recover him.
3814. The king said: «You are my felons!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXXVII (lines 3815–3837)

**Original**
3815. Quant Carles veit que tuz li sunt faillid, [voir, être, faillir]
3816. Mult l'enbrunchit e la chere e le vis; [embruncher (pencher)]
3817. Al doel qu'il ad si se cleimet caitifs. [avoir, clamer (s'appeler)]
3818. Ais li devant uns chevalers, [Tierris],
3819. Frere Gefrei a un duc angevin.
3820. Heingre out le cors e graisle e eschewid, [avoir]
3821. Neirs les chevels e alques bruns [le vis];
3822. N'est gueres granz ne trop nen est petiz. [être]
3823. Curteisement a l'emperere ad dit: [avoir, dire]
3824. «Bels sire reis, ne vos dementez si. [se démenter (se lamenter)]
3825. Ja savez vos, que mult vos ai servit; [savoir, avoir, servir]
3826. Par anceisurs dei jo tel plait tenir. [devoir, tenir]
3827. Que que Rollant a Guenelun forsfesist, [forsfaire (faire tort)]
3828. Vostre servise l'en doüst bien guarir! [devoir, garir (protéger)]
3829. Guenes est fels d'iço qu'il le traït; [être, trahir]
3830. Vers vos s'en est parjurez e malmis. [être, se parjurer, malmetre (se ruiner)]
3831. Pur ço le juz jo a prendre e a murir [juger, prendre, mourir]
3832. E sun cors metre... [mettre]
3833. Si cume fel ki felonie fist. [faire]
3834. Se or ad parent ki m'en voeille desmentir, [avoir, vouloir, démentir]
3835. A ceste espee, que jo ai ceinte ici, [avoir, ceindre]
3836. Mun jugement voel sempres guarantir.» [vouloir, garantir]
3837. Respundent Franc: «Or avez vos ben dit.» [répondre, avoir, dire]

**Translation**
3815. When Charles sees that all have failed him,
3816. He bows his countenance and his face greatly;
3817. In the grief he has, he calls himself wretched.
3818. Behold, before him, a knight, Thierry, <!-- uncertain: 'Ais' read as the presentative ez/es 'behold' (as at 2452), not a form of être -->
3819. Brother of Geoffrey, an Angevin duke.
3820. He had a lean body, slender and slim,
3821. Black hair and a somewhat brown face;
3822. He is not very tall, nor is he too small.
3823. Courteously he said to the emperor:
3824. «Fair lord king, do not lament so.
3825. You well know that I have served you greatly;
3826. By my ancestors I must hold such a case.
3827. Whatever wrong Roland did to Ganelon,
3828. Your service ought to have protected him well!
3829. Ganelon is a villain in that he betrayed him;
3830. Toward you he has perjured himself and ruined himself.
3831. For that I judge him to be seized and to die
3832. And his body put...
3833. As a villain who committed villainy.
3834. If now he has a kinsman who wishes to give me the lie on it,
3835. With this sword that I have girded here,
3836. I will at once warrant my judgment.»
3837. The Franks answer: «Now you have spoken well.»

---

## Laisse CCLXXVIII (lines 3838–3849)

**Original**
3838. Devant lu rei est venuz Pinabel, [être, venir]
3839. Granz est e forz e vassals e isnel: [être]
3840. Qu'il fiert a colp, de sun tens n'i ad mais. [ferir (frapper), avoir]
3841. E dist al rei: «Sire, vostre est li plaiz: [dire, être]
3842. Car cumandez, que tel noise n'i ait! [commander, avoir]
3843. Ci vei Tierri, ki jugement ad fait. [voir, avoir, faire]
3844. Jo si li fals, od lui m'en cumbatrai.» [fausser (déclarer faux), se combattre]
3845. Met li el poign de cerf le destre guant. [mettre]
3846. Dist li emper[er]es: «Bons pleges en demant.» [dire, demander]
3847. .XXX. parenz l'i plevissent leial. [plevir (garantir)]
3848. Ço dist li reis: «E jol vos recr[e]rai.» [dire, recroire (libérer sous caution)]
3849. Fait cels guarder tresque li dreiz en serat. AOI. [faire, garder, être]

**Translation**
3838. Before the king Pinabel has come,
3839. He is big and strong and a vassal and swift:
3840. For when he strikes a blow, there is none beyond him in his time. <!-- uncertain: 'de sun tens n'i ad mais' read as 'none surpasses him in his day' -->
3841. And said to the king: «Sire, the case is yours:
3842. So command that there be no such uproar here!
3843. Here I see Thierry, who has pronounced judgment.
3844. I declare it false, and with him I shall fight over it.» <!-- uncertain: 'Jo si li fals' read as 'I declare it false'; the verb fausser (to challenge a judgment) is inferred -->
3845. He places in his hand the right glove of deerskin. <!-- uncertain: 'el poign de cerf' is ambiguous; taken as the glove being of deerskin, set in the king's hand -->
3846. The emperor says: «I ask for good pledges for it.»
3847. Thirty kinsmen pledge him loyally.
3848. The king said: «And I shall release him to you on bail.»
3849. He has them kept under guard until justice is done of it. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXXIX (lines 3850–3857)

**Original**
3850. Quant veit Tierri qu'or en ert la bataille, [voir, être]
3851. Sun destre guant en ad presentet Carle. [avoir, présenter]
3852. Li emperere l'i recreit par hostage, [recroire (libérer sous caution)]
3853. Puis fait porter .IIII. bancs en la place; [faire, porter]
3854. La vunt sedeir cil kis deivent cumbatre. [aller, seoir (être assis), devoir, combattre]
3855. Ben sunt malez, par jugement des altres, [être]
3856. Sil purparlat Oger de Denemarche; [porparler (arranger)]
3857. E puis demandent lur chevals e lur armes. [demander]

**Translation**
3850. When Thierry sees that the battle is now to be,
3851. His right glove he has presented to Charles.
3852. The emperor lets him have it back on hostages, <!-- uncertain: sense of 'l'i recreit par hostage' (what is released, to whom) is not certain -->
3853. Then has four benches carried onto the place;
3854. There go to sit those who must fight.
3855. They are well 'malez', by the judgment of the others, <!-- uncertain: the word 'malez' could not be identified; left untranslated -->
3856. Ogier of Denmark arranged it;
3857. And then they ask for their horses and their arms.

---

## Laisse CCLXXX (lines 3858–3872)

**Original**
3858. Puis que il sunt a bataille jugez, AOI. [être, juger]
3859. Ben sunt cunfes e asols e seignez: [être, confesser, absoudre, seignier (bénir)]
3860. Oent lur messes e sunt acuminiez; [oïr (entendre), être, communier]
3861. Mult granz offrendes metent par cez musters. [mettre]
3862. Devant Carlun andui sunt repairez: [être, repairer (rentrer)]
3863. Lur esperuns unt en lor piez calcez, [avoir, chalcer (enfiler)]
3864. Vestent osberc blancs e forz e legers, [vêtir]
3865. Lur helmes clers unt fermez en lor chefs, [avoir, fermer]
3866. Ceinent espees enheldees d'or mier, [ceindre, enhelder (munir d'une poignée)]
3867. En lur cols pendent lur escuz de quarters, [pendre]
3868. En lur puinz destres unt lur trenchanz espiez; [avoir]
3869. Puis sunt muntez en lur curanz destrers. [être, monter]
3870. Idunc plurerent .C. milie chevalers, [pleurer]
3871. Qui pur Rollant de Tierri unt pitiet. [avoir]
3872. Deus set asez cument la fins en ert. [savoir, être]

**Translation**
3858. Since they have been judged to battle, AOI.
3859. They are well confessed and absolved and blessed:
3860. They hear their masses and receive communion;
3861. They place very great offerings in these minsters.
3862. Before Charles both have come back:
3863. Their spurs they have put on their feet,
3864. They put on hauberks, white and strong and light,
3865. Their bright helmets they have laced on their heads,
3866. They gird swords with hilts of pure gold,
3867. On their necks they hang their quartered shields,
3868. In their right fists they have their sharp spears;
3869. Then they are mounted on their swift destriers.
3870. Then a hundred thousand knights wept,
3871. Who for Roland's sake have pity on Thierry.
3872. God knows well how the end will be.

---

## Laisse CCLXXXI (lines 3873–3882)

**Original**
3873. Dedesuz Ais est la pree mult large: [être]
3874. Des dous baruns justee est la bataille. [être, jouster (combattre)]
3875. Cil sunt produme e de grant vasselage [être]
3876. E lur chevals sunt curanz e aates. [être]
3877. Brochent les bien, tutes les resnes lasquent; [brocher (éperonner), lâcher]
3878. Par grant vertut vait ferir l'uns li altre; [aller, ferir (frapper)]
3879. Tuz lur escuz i fruissent e esquassent, [fruisier (briser), casser]
3880. Lur osbercs rumpent e lur cengles depiecent, [rompre, dépecer]
3881. Les alves turnent, les seles cheent a tere. [tourner, cheoir (tomber)]
3882. .C. mil humes i plurent, kis esguardent. [pleurer, esgarder (regarder)]

**Translation**
3873. Below Aix is the very broad meadow:
3874. By the two barons the battle is fought.
3875. They are worthy men and of great valor
3876. And their horses are swift and spirited.
3877. They spur them well, they slacken all the reins;
3878. With great force each goes to strike the other;
3879. All their shields they shatter and break there,
3880. Their hauberks they break and their girths they tear to pieces,
3881. The saddle-bows turn, the saddles fall to the ground.
3882. A hundred thousand men weep there, who watch them.

---

## Laisse CCLXXXII (lines 3883–3891)

**Original**
3883. A tere sunt ambdui li chevaler, AOI. [être]
3884. Isnelement se drecent sur lur piez. [se dresser]
3885. Pinabels est forz e isnels e legers. [être]
3886. Li uns requiert l'altre, n'unt mie des destrers. [requerre (attaquer), avoir]
3887. De cez espees enheldees d'or mer, [enhelder (munir d'une poignée)]
3888. Fierent e caplent sur cez helmes d'acer; [ferir (frapper), capler (frapper)]
3889. Granz sunt les colps, as helmes detrencher. [être, detrenchier (taillader)]
3890. Mult se dementent cil Franceis chevaler. [se démenter (se lamenter)]
3891. «E! Deus,» dist Carles, «le dreit en esclargiez!» [dire, esclargir (éclaircir)]

**Translation**
3883. Both knights are on the ground. AOI.
3884. Quickly they rise to their feet.
3885. Pinabel is strong and swift and nimble.
3886. Each goes at the other; they have no horses left.
3887. With their swords hilted in pure gold,
3888. They strike and hack at their steel helmets;
3889. Great are the blows, such as to cut helmets to pieces.
3890. The French knights lament loudly.
3891. «Ah! God,» says Charles, «make the right clear!»

---

## Laisse CCLXXXIII (lines 3892–3898)

**Original**
3892. Dist Pinabel: «Tierri, car te recreiz! [dire, recroire (renoncer)]
3893. Tes hom serai par amur e par feid, [être]
3894. A tun plaisir te durrai mun aveir, [donner]
3895. Mais Guenelun fai acorder al rei!» [faire, acorder (accorder)]
3896. Respont Tierri: «Ja n'en tendrai cunseill. [répondre, tenir]
3897. Tut seie fel se jo mie l'otrei! [être, otreier (octroyer)]
3898. Deus facet hoi entre nus dous le dreit!» AOI. [faire]

**Translation**
3892. Pinabel says: «Thierry, come, yield!
3893. I will be your man by love and by faith,
3894. At your pleasure I will give you my wealth,
3895. If only you reconcile Ganelon with the king!»
3896. Thierry answers: «I will never take counsel in this.
3897. May I be a complete villain if I ever agree to it!
3898. May God decide the right between us two today!» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXXXIV (lines 3899–3914)

**Original**
3899. Ço dist Tierri: «Pinabel mult ies ber, [dire, être]
3900. Granz ies e forz e tis cors ben mollez; [être, mouler]
3901. De vasselage te conoissent ti per; [connaître]
3902. Ceste bataille, car la laisses ester! [laisser, ester (rester)]
3903. A Carlemagne te ferai acorder; [faire, acorder (accorder)]
3904. De Guenelun justise ert faite tel, [être, faire]
3905. Jamais n'ert jur que il n'en seit parlet.» [être, parler]
3906. Dist Pinabel: «Ne placet Damnedeu! [dire, plaire]
3907. Sustenir voeill trestut mun parentet; [vouloir, soutenir]
3908. N'en recrerrai pur nul hume mortel; [recroire (renoncer)]
3909. Mielz voeill murir que il me seit reprovet.» [vouloir, mourir, être, reprocher]
3910. De lur espees cumencent a capler [commencer, capler (frapper)]
3911. Desur cez helmes, ki sunt a or gemez; [être, gemmer]
3912. Cuntre le ciel en volet li fous tuz clers. [voler]
3913. Il ne poet estre qu'il seient desevrez: [pouvoir, être, dessevrer (séparer)]
3914. Seinz hume mort ne poet estre afinet. AOI. [pouvoir, être, afiner (achever)]

**Translation**
3899. Thierry says: «Pinabel, you are very valiant,
3900. Big and strong, and your body well shaped;
3901. Your peers know you for valor;
3902. This battle, pray leave it be!
3903. I will reconcile you with Charlemagne;
3904. Such justice will be done on Ganelon
3905. That there will never be a day when it is not spoken of.»
3906. Pinabel says: «May God not allow it!
3907. I want to uphold all my kindred;
3908. I will not yield for any mortal man;
3909. I would rather die than have it reproached to me.»
3910. With their swords they begin to hack
3911. On their helmets, which are gemmed with gold;
3912. Toward the sky the fire flies up, all bright.
3913. It cannot be that they be parted:
3914. Without a man dead it cannot be ended. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXXXV (lines 3915–3923)

**Original**
3915. Mult par est proz Pinabel de Sorence; [être]
3916. Si fiert Tierri sur l'elme de Provence: [ferir (frapper)]
3917. Salt en li fous, que l'erbe en fait esprendre; [saillir (jaillir), faire, esprendre (enflammer)]
3918. Del brant d'acer la mure li presentet, [présenter]
3919. Desur le frunt li ad faite descendre. [avoir, faire, descendre]
3920. Parmi le vis (li ad faite descendre): [avoir, faire, descendre]
3921. La destre joe en ad tute sanglente; [avoir]
3922. L'osberc del dos josque par sum le ventre.
3923. Deus le guarit, que mort ne l'acraventet. AOI. [garir (protéger), acraventer (abattre)]

**Translation**
3915. Very valiant is Pinabel of Sorence;
3916. He strikes Thierry on the helmet of Provence:
3917. Fire leaps from it, so that it sets the grass ablaze;
3918. With the steel sword he offers him the edge, <!-- uncertain: mure is obscure; read here as the sword's cutting edge, but the noun is disputed -->
3919. He has made it come down on his forehead.
3920. Through the face (he has made it come down):
3921. He has his right cheek all bloody from it;
3922. The hauberk (cut) from the back up to the top of the belly.
3923. God protects him, so that Pinabel does not fell him dead. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXXXVI (lines 3924–3933)

**Original**
3924. Ço veit Tierris, que el vis est ferut: [voir, être, ferir (frapper)]
3925. Li sancs tuz clers en chiet el pred herbus [cheoir (tomber)]
3926. Fiert Pinabel sur l'elme d'acer brun, [ferir (frapper)]
3927. Jusqu'al nasel li ad frait e fendut, [avoir, fraindre (briser), fendre]
3928. Del chef li ad le cervel espandut, [avoir, épandre]
3929. Brandit sun colp, si l'ad mort abatut. [brandir, avoir, abattre]
3930. A icest colp est li esturs vencut. [être, vaincre]
3931. Escrient Franc: «Deus i ad fait vertut! [escrier (s'écrier), avoir, faire]
3932. Asez est dreiz que Guenes seit pendut [être, pendre]
3933. E si parent, ki plaidet unt pur lui.» AOI. [plaider, avoir]

**Translation**
3924. Thierry sees this, that he is struck in the face:
3925. The bright blood falls from it onto the grassy meadow.
3926. He strikes Pinabel on the helmet of dark steel,
3927. Down to the nose-guard he has broken and split it,
3928. From the head he has spilled the brain,
3929. He brandishes his stroke and has struck him down dead.
3930. With this blow the combat is won.
3931. The Franks cry out: «God has wrought a miracle here!
3932. It is quite right that Ganelon be hanged
3933. And his kinsmen, who have pleaded for him.» AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXXXVII (lines 3934–3946)

**Original**
3934. Quant Tierris ad vencue sa bataille, [avoir, vaincre]
3935. Venuz i est li emperere Carles, [être, venir]
3936. Ensembl'od lui de ses baruns quarante,
3937. Naimes li dux, Oger de Danemarche,
3938. Geifrei d'Anjou e Willalme de Blaive.
3939. Li reis ad pris Tierri entre sa brace, [avoir, prendre]
3940. Tert lui le vis od ses granz pels de martre, [terdre (essuyer)]
3941. Celes met jus, puis li afublent altres; [mettre, afubler (revêtir)]
3942. Mult suavet le chevaler desarment. [désarmer]
3943. [Munter l'unt] fait en une mule d'Arabe; [monter, avoir, faire]
3944. Repairet s'en a joie e a barnage; [repairer (rentrer)]
3945. Vienent ad Ais, descendent en la place. [venir, descendre]
3946. Des ore cumencet l'ocisiun des altres. [commencer]

**Translation**
3934. When Thierry has won his battle,
3935. Emperor Charles has come there,
3936. With him forty of his barons,
3937. Duke Naimes, Ogier of Denmark,
3938. Geoffrey of Anjou and William of Blaye.
3939. The king has taken Thierry in his arms,
3940. Wipes his face with his great marten furs,
3941. Sets those aside, then others are put on him;
3942. Very gently they disarm the knight.
3943. They have made him mount an Arabian mule;
3944. He goes back with joy and with a noble company;
3945. They come to Aix, they dismount in the square.
3946. From this hour begins the execution of the others.

---

## Laisse CCLXXXVIII (lines 3947–3959)

**Original**
3947. Carles apelet ses cuntes e ses dux: [appeler]
3948. «Que me loez de cels qu'ai retenuz? [loer (conseiller), avoir, retenir]
3949. Pur Guenelun erent a plait venuz, [être, venir]
3950. Pur Pinabel en ostage renduz.» [rendre]
3951. Respundent Franc: «Ja mar en vivrat uns!» [répondre, vivre]
3952. Li reis cumandet un soen veier, Basbrun: [commander]
3953. «Va, sis pent tuz a l'arbre de mal fust! [aller, pendre]
3954. Par ceste barbe dunt li peil sunt canuz, [être]
3955. Se uns escapet, morz ies e cunfunduz.» [échapper, être, mourir, confondre]
3956. Cil li respunt: «Qu'en fereie jo e el?» [répondre, faire]
3957. Od .C. serjanz par force les cunduit. [conduire]
3958. .XXX. en i ad d'icels ki sunt pendut. [avoir, être, pendre]
3959. Ki hume traïst, sei ocit e altroi. AOI. [trahir, ocire (tuer)]

**Translation**
3947. Charles calls his counts and his dukes:
3948. «What do you advise me about those I have kept back?
3949. They had come to the trial for Ganelon,
3950. Handed over as hostages for Pinabel.»
3951. The Franks answer: «Never shall a single one live!»
3952. The king gives orders to one of his bailiffs, Basbrun:
3953. «Go, hang them all on the tree of evil wood!
3954. By this beard whose hairs are hoary,
3955. If one escapes, you are dead and ruined.»
3956. He answers him: «What else would I do about it?»
3957. With a hundred sergeants he leads them by force.
3958. Thirty of them there are who are hanged.
3959. He who betrays a man kills himself and others. AOI.

---

## Laisse CCLXXXIX (lines 3960–3974)

**Original**
3960. Puis sunt turnet Bavier e Aleman [être, tourner]
3961. E Peitevin e Bretun e Norman.
3962. Sor tuit li altre l'unt otriet li Franc [avoir, otreier (octroyer)]
3963. Que Guenes moerget par merveillus ahan. [mourir]
3964. Quatre destrers funt amener avant, [faire, amener]
3965. Puis si li lient e les piez e les mains. [lier]
3966. Li cheval sunt orgoillus e curant; [être, courir]
3967. Quatre serjanz les acoeillent devant, [acueillir (saisir)]
3968. Devers un'ewe ki est en mi un camp. [être]
3969. Guenes est turnet a perdiciun grant; [être, tourner]
3970. Trestuit si nerf mult li sunt estendant [être, étendre]
3971. E tuit li membre de sun cors derumpant: [derumpre (rompre)]
3972. Sur l'erbe verte en espant li cler sanc. [épandre]
3973. Guenes est mort cume fel recreant. [être, mourir]
3974. Hom ki traïst altre, nen est dreiz qu'il s'en vant. [trahir, être, se vanter]

**Translation**
3960. Then the Bavarians and Germans have turned away
3961. And the Poitevins and Bretons and Normans.
3962. Above all the others, the Franks have agreed
3963. That Ganelon shall die in terrible torment.
3964. They have four horses brought forward,
3965. Then they bind both his feet and his hands.
3966. The horses are proud and running;
3967. Four sergeants seize them in front,
3968. Toward a stream that is in the middle of a field.
3969. Ganelon is turned to great perdition;
3970. All his sinews are stretching greatly
3971. And all the limbs of his body breaking apart:
3972. On the green grass his bright blood spills out.
3973. Ganelon is dead like a faithless coward.
3974. A man who betrays another has no right to boast of it.

---

## Laisse CCXC (lines 3975–3987)

**Original**
3975. Quant li empereres ad faite sa venjance, [avoir, faire]
3976. Sin apelat ses evesques de France, [appeler]
3977. Cels de Baviere e icels d'Alemaigne:
3978. «En ma maisun ad une caitive franche. [avoir]
3979. Tant ad oït e sermuns e essamples, [avoir, oïr (entendre)]
3980. Creire voelt Deu, chrestientet demandet. [croire, vouloir, demander]
3981. Baptizez la, pur quei Deus en ait l'anme.» [baptiser, avoir]
3982. Cil li respundent: «Or seit faite par marrenes: [répondre, être, faire]
3983. Asez cruiz e linees dames...»
3984. As bainz ad Aís mult sunt granz les ci... [être]
3985. La baptizent la reïne d'Espaigne: [baptiser]
3986. Truvee li unt le num de Juliane. [avoir, trouver]
3987. Chrestiene est par veire conoisance. [être]

**Translation**
3975. When the emperor has taken his vengeance,
3976. He calls his bishops of France,
3977. Those of Bavaria and those of Germany:
3978. «In my house there is a noble captive woman.
3979. She has heard so many sermons and examples,
3980. She wishes to believe in God, she asks for Christianity.
3981. Baptize her, so that God may have her soul.»
3982. They answer him: «Now let it be done by godmothers:
3983. Plenty of cruiz and noble-born ladies...» <!-- uncertain: the verse is corrupt and unfinished in the manuscript; cruiz is obscure and linees is read as of noble lineage -->
3984. At the baths at Aix very great are the ci... <!-- uncertain: the verse is corrupt and breaks off; the sense of les ci... is lost -->
3985. There they baptize the queen of Spain:
3986. They have found for her the name of Juliana.
3987. She is Christian by true knowledge.

---

## Laisse CCXCI (lines 3988–4002)

**Original**
3988. Quant l'emperere ad faite sa justise [avoir, faire]
3989. E esclargiez est la sue grant ire, [être, esclargir (s'éclaircir)]
3990. En Bramidonie ad chrestientet mise, [avoir, mettre]
3991. Passet li jurz, la nuit est aserie. [passer, être, aserir (devenir nuit)]
3992. Culcez s'est li reis en sa cambre voltice. [être, se coucher]
3993. Seint Gabriel de part Deu li vint dire: [venir, dire]
3994. «Carles, sumun les oz de tun emperie! [semondre (convoquer)]
3995. Par force iras en la tere de Bire, [aller]
3996. Reis Vivien si succuras en Imphe, [secourir]
3997. A la citet que paien unt asise: [avoir, seoir (asseoir)]
3998. Li chrestien te recleiment e crient.» [réclamer, crier]
3999. Li emperere n'i volsist aler mie: [vouloir, aller]
4000. «Deus,» dist li reis, «si penuse est ma vie!» [dire, être]
4001. Pluret des oilz, sa barbe blanche tiret. [pleurer, tirer]
4002. Ci falt la geste que Turoldus declinet. [faillir, décliner]

**Translation**
3988. When the emperor has done his justice
3989. And his great anger is cleared,
3990. He has set Christianity in Bramimonde,
3991. The day passes, the night has fallen.
3992. The king has lain down in his vaulted chamber.
3993. Saint Gabriel on God's behalf came to tell him:
3994. «Charles, summon the armies of your empire!
3995. By force you shall go into the land of Bire,
3996. You shall succor King Vivien at Imphe,
3997. In the city that the pagans have besieged:
3998. The Christians call on you and cry out.»
3999. The emperor would not at all wish to go:
4000. «God,» says the king, «how toilsome is my life!»
4001. He weeps from his eyes, tugs at his white beard.
4002. Here ends the tale that Turoldus relates. <!-- uncertain: declinet is disputed (recites, brings to a close, or declines); décliner is used as the nearest modern verb -->
